The Hasse principle for homogeneous polynomials with random coefficients over thin sets
Kiseok Yeon

Abstract
. In this paper, we investigate the solubility of homogeneous polynomial equations. The work of Browning, Le boudec, Sawin [ References ] shows that almost all homogeneous equations of degree d ≥ 4 d\geq 4 in d + 1 d+1 or more variables satisfy the Hasse principle, and in particular that a positive portion possess a non-trivial integral solution. Our main result, when combined with our sequel joint work with H.Lee and S.Lee, shows that such a conclusion remains true even when the coefficients of homogeneous polynomials are constrained by a polynomial condition under a modest condition on the number of variables. To state the precise result, let d d and n n be natural numbers. Let ν d , n : ℝ n → ℝ N \nu_{d,n}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{N} denote the Veronese embedding with N = ( n + d − 1 d ) N=\binom{n+d-1}{d} , defined by listing all the monomials of degree d d in n n variables using the lexicographical ordering. Let ⟨ 𝒂 , ν d , n ​ ( 𝒙 ) ⟩ ∈ ℤ ⁡ [ 𝒙 ] \langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{x}})\rangle\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}] be a homogeneous polynomial in n n variables of degree d d with integer coefficients 𝒂 {\boldsymbol{a}} , where ⟨ ⋅ , ⋅ ⟩ \langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle denotes the inner product. For a non-singular form P ∈ ℤ ⁡ [ 𝒙 ] P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}] in N N variables of degree k ≥ 2 , k\geq 2, consider a set of integer vectors 𝒂 ∈ ℤ N {\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N} , defined by 𝔄 ( A ; P ) = { 𝒂 ∈ ℤ N | P ( 𝒂 ) = 0 , ∥ 𝒂 ∥ ∞ ≤ A } . \mathfrak{A}(A;P)=\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}|\ P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0,\ \|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\}. We confirm that when d ≥ 4 d\geq 4 , n n is sufficiently large in terms of d d , and k ≤ d , k\leq d, the proportion of integer vectors 𝒂 ∈ ℤ N {\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N} in 𝔄 ⁡ ( A , P ) \mathfrak{A}(A;P) , whose associated equations ⟨ 𝒂 , ν d , n ​ ( 𝒙 ) ⟩ = 0 \langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{x}})\rangle=0 satisfy the Hasse principle, converges to 1 1 as A → ∞ A\rightarrow\infty . We make explicit a lower bound on n n guaranteeing this conclusion. In particular, we show that when d ≥ 14 d\geq 14 it suffices to take n ≥ 32 ​ d + 17 n\geq 32d+17 . Our main tool is the Hardy-Littlewood circle method.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
11E76,14G12

1.
Introduction and statement of the results

In 1962, thanks to the celebrated work of Birch [
References
], we can say that a system of
R
forms
F_{1},\ldots,F_{R}
with integer coefficients of degree
d
in
n
variables, satisfies the
smooth
Hasse principle whenever
n
is sufficiently large in terms of
d
,
R
and the dimension of a certain singular locus. In particular, it suffices to take

(1.1)

n>R(R+1)(d-1)2^{d-1}+\text{dim}\ W,

where

W=\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{A}^{n}|\ \text{rank}(J({\boldsymbol{x}}))<R\},

in which
J({\boldsymbol{x}})
is the Jacobian matrix of size
R\times n
formed from the gradient vectors
\nabla F_{1}({\boldsymbol{x}}),\ldots,\nabla F_{R}({\boldsymbol{x}}).

Later on, Rydin Myerson [
References
] showed that the factor
R(R+1)(d-1)2^{d-1}
in the bound (
1.1
) can be replaced by a factor growing linearly in
R
, in particular,
d2^{d}R+R
(see also [
References
] and [
References
] for the cases
d=2
and
d=3
, respectively). Furthermore, Brüdern and Wooley [
References
] showed that whenever
n\geq 13
, a system of two diagonal cubic forms satisfies the Hasse principle. This result was generalized to that for systems of diagonal cubic forms with a certain non-singularity condition on the coefficients of the system [
References
]. Beyond a system of equations with the same degrees, Browning and Heath-Brown
[\ref{ref9}]
verified the Hasse principle for a system of forms with different degrees.

For the case
R=1
, in particular, the Hasse–Minkowski theorem shows that the Hasse principle holds for a quadratic form. Furthermore, for
d=3
and
R=1,
Heath-Brown [
References
] proved that non-singular cubic forms with
10
variables have a nontrivial integer solution, and later on, a series of Hooley’s works
([\ref{ref18}],[\ref{ref22}],[\ref{ref23}],[\ref{ref24}])
verified the Hasse principle for non-singular cubic forms in
9
variables and cubic forms in
9
variables allowed a certain singularity condition. For
d=4
and
R=1,
due to Marmon and Vishe [
References
], we can say that a non-singular quartic form satisfies the Hasse principle whenever
n
is at least
28.
In general, for
d\geq 3
and
R=1,
Browning and Prendiville [
References
] showed that a non-singular form satisfies the Hasse principle whenever
n
is at least
\left(d-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{d}\right)2^{d}
.

It is known that the Brauer–Manin obstruction to the Hasse principle is empty for smooth Fano varieties of dimension at least 3 over any number field.
Moreover, for such varieties, it follows from a conjecture of Colliot-Thélène that the Brauer–Manin obstruction to the Hasse principle should be the only one. Therefore, smooth Fano varieties are expected to satisfy the Hasse principle. For a smooth variety
V
defined by a form in
n
variables of degree
d

(R=1
in our discussion), the number of variables
n
is greater or equal to
d+1
if and only if
V
is Fano, and thus a non-singular form, with
n\geq d+1
with
d\geq 4
and
R=1
, is expected to satisfy the Hasse principle. We notice here that the current records described in the previous paragraph are very far from this expectation.

In
2003
, Poonen and Voloch [
References
] suggested a probabilistic point of view associated with this expectation. To be specific, let
n
and
d
be natural numbers with
d\geq 2.
Throughout this paper, we let

(1.2)

N:=N_{d,n}=\binom{n+d-1}{d}.

Let
\nu_{d,n}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
denote the Veronese embedding, defined by listing all the monomials of degree
d
in
n
variables using the lexicographical ordering. We denote a homogeneous polynomial in
n
variables of degree
d
with integer coefficients, by
\langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{x}})\rangle
with
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
. For here and throughout, we write
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{x}})\rangle
for simplicity. If we consider a set of integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
, defined by

\mathfrak{A}(A)=\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}|\ \|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\},

Poonen and Voloch [
References
] conjectured that the proportion of integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
in
\mathfrak{A}(A)
, whose associated equations
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
satisfy the Hasse principle, converges to
1
as
A\rightarrow\infty
, provided that
n\geq d+1
and
d\geq 3
. Recently, Browning, Le Boudec, and Sawin [
References
] confirmed this conjecture of Poonen and Voloch except for the case
d=3
and
n=4.

To bridge between this probabilistic point of view and the original expectation with a deterministic nature mentioned in the third paragraph, it is natural to investigate the distribution of integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
in
\mathfrak{A}(A)
whose associated equations
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
satisfy the Hasse principle. However, the conjecture of Poonen and Voloch [
References
] and the conclusion of the work due to Browning, Le Boudec, and Sawin [
References
] seem incapable of describing the distribution of those integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
in
\mathfrak{A}(A)
beyond the bounds of their cardinality. Therefore, we turn our attention to the distribution of those integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
restricted to ‘thin sets’. In
2014,
Brüdern and Dietmann [
References
] showed that the proportion of integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
in
[-A,A]^{n}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
associated with diagonal forms satisfying the Hasse principle, converges to
1
as
A\rightarrow\infty
, provided that
n\geq 3d+2
.

In order to describe our main interest, for
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N},
we fix a non-singular form
P(\boldsymbol{a})\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
in
N
variables of degree
k\geq 2
. For the convenience, we define

(1.3)

\mathfrak{A}(A;P):=\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}|\ \|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A,\ P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\}.

Then, we may ask a question about how many equations
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
, with
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathfrak{A}(A;P)
, satisfy the Hasse principle. Furthermore, even when the coefficients
\boldsymbol{a}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
are restricted to arbitrary algebraic sets, we may ask how many equations
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
, associated with these coefficients
\boldsymbol{a}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
, do satisfy the Hasse principle. Even in the case
R>1
, we may ask analogous questions. With more answers to these questions, we have better information about the distribution of the coefficients
\boldsymbol{a}
whose associated equations satisfy the Hasse principle.

In this paper, we develop a framework via the Hardy-Littlewood circle method, in order to answer the question described in the previous paragraph. We define a set
\mathcal{A}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(A;P)
of integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
in
\mathfrak{A}(A;P)
having the property that the associated equation
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
is everywhere locally soluble. When
d\geq 14,

k\leq d
and
n\geq 32d+17
, we verify that the proportion of integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(A;P)
in
\mathfrak{A}(A;P)
, having the property that the number of integer solutions in
[1,X]^{n}
satisfying
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
is less than
A^{-1}X^{n-d}(\log A)^{-\tau}
for some
\tau>0,
converges to
0
as
A\rightarrow\infty
(see Theorem
1.3
together with Corollary
1.4
below). Meanwhile, for
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
satisfying that there exists
{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
with
P({\boldsymbol{b}})=0
such that
f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
has a smooth integer point, our sequel joint work with H.Lee and S.Lee reveals that the proportion of integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(A;P)
in
\mathfrak{A}(A;P)
converges to a positive number as
A\rightarrow\infty.
Therefore, at least for these
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
, our main theorem is indeed a non-trivial one. Furthermore, we can say that with
d,k,n
and those
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
as above, for almost all
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(A;P)
, the equation
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
has the expected number of integer solutions inferred by the circle method, allowed up to a factor
(\log A)^{-\tau}
. Therefore, this obviously implies that for
d,k,n
as above, the proportion of integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
in
\mathfrak{A}(A;P)
, whose associated equations
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
satisfy the Hasse principle, converges to
1
as
A\rightarrow\infty.

In order to describe our main theorems, we temporarily pause here and provide some definitions. Recall that
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})
is a homogeneous polynomial in
n
variables of degree
d
.
Furthermore, for
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
and
X>0
, we define

(1.4)

\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X)=\left\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in[1,X]^{n}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}|\ f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0\right\}.

We note here that our argument proceeds for fixed
X>0
, and thus for simplicity, we write

(1.5)

w=\log X

and

(1.6)

W=\displaystyle\prod_{p\leq w}p^{\lfloor\log w/\log p\rfloor}.

Observe here that an application of the prime number theorem reveals that
\log W\leq 2w,
which implies

(1.7)

W\leq X^{2}.

For
Q>0
and
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N},
we define

(1.8)

\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};Q)=Q^{-(n-1)}\#\{{\boldsymbol{g}}\in[1,Q]^{n}|\ f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{g}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ Q\}.

We notice that by the Chinese remainder theorem one has

(1.9)

\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};Q)=\displaystyle\prod_{p^{r}\|Q}\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r}).

Then, on recalling the definition
(\ref{def2.3})
of
W
, we write

(1.10)

\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}=\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};W)=\displaystyle\prod_{p^{r}\|W}\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r}).

Recall the definition
(\ref{def2.2})
of
w.
Put
\zeta=w^{-5},
and we introduce an auxiliary function

\mathfrak{w}_{\zeta}(\beta)=\zeta\cdot\left(\frac{\text{sin}(\pi\zeta\beta)}{\pi\zeta\beta}\right)^{2}.

This function has the Fourier transform

\widehat{\mathfrak{w}}_{\zeta}(\xi)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathfrak{w}_{\zeta}(\beta)e(-\beta\xi)d\beta=\text{max}\{0,1-|\xi|/\zeta\}.

For
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
and
A,X>0,
we define

(1.11)

\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}:=\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}(A,X)=A^{-1}X^{n-d}\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{n}}\zeta^{-1}\widehat{\mathfrak{w}}_{\zeta}(A^{-1}f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}))d\boldsymbol{\gamma}.

As we will show in section
4
below, the functions
\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}
and
\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}
behave in a similar manner to the truncated singular series and the truncated singular integral, which traditionally appear in the main term of an asymptotic formula for
\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X)
predicted by the Hardy-Littlewood circle method, on average over
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
in
\mathfrak{A}(A;P).

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
A
and
X
be positive numbers and let
n
and
d
be natural numbers with
d\geq 4.
Let
n_{1}
be the greatest integer with
n_{1}\leq\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor/8.
Suppose that
n_{1}>2d
and
2X^{d}\leq A\leq X^{n_{1}-d}.
Suppose that
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N_{d,n}
variables of degree
k\geq 2.
Then, whenever
N_{d,n}\geq 200k(k-1)2^{k-1},
there is a positive number
\delta<1
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X)-\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\right|^{2}\ll A^{N-k-2}X^{2n-2d}(\log A)^{-\delta}.

Recall the definition
(\ref{1.21.2})
of
N:=N_{d,n}
. In advance of the statement of the following theorem, we recall the definition of
\mathcal{A}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(A;P)
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
A
and
X
be positive numbers with
X^{3}\leq A.
Suppose that
n
and
d
are natural numbers with
n>d+1
and
d\geq 2
. Suppose that
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N_{d,n}
variables of degree
k\geq 2.
Then, whenever
N_{d,n}\geq 1000n^{2}8^{k}
, one has

\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(A;P)\middle|\ \begin{aligned} \mathfrak{S}^{*}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\leq X^{n-d}A^{-1}(\log A)^{-\eta}\end{aligned}\right\}\ll A^{N-k}\cdot(\log A)^{-\eta/(40n)},

for any
\eta>0.

One infers that the next theorem obviously implies that the proportion of integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
in
\mathfrak{A}(A;P)
, whose associated equations
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
satisfy the Hasse principle, converges to
1.
We prove this theorem by making use of Theorem
1.1
and
1.2
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
A
and
X
be positive numbers. Suppose that
A,X,n,d
and
k
satisfy the hypotheses in Theorem
1.1
and
1.2
. Then, the proportion of integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}(A;P)
in
\mathfrak{A}(A;P)
, having the property that

\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X)<A^{-1}X^{n-d}(\log A)^{-1/5},

converges to
0
as
A\rightarrow\infty.

Proof.

Let
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}(A;P)
. Suppose that for
\delta>0
obtained in Theorem
1.1
and for
A,X>0
with
2X^{d}\leq A\leq X^{n_{1}-d}
, one has

(1.12)

\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X)-\mathfrak{S}^{*}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\right|\leq A^{-1}X^{n-d}\left(\log A\right)^{-\delta/4},

and

(1.13)

\mathfrak{S}^{*}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}>A^{-1}X^{n-d}(\log A)^{-\delta/5}.

For sufficiently large
A>0
, it follows from
(\ref{2.11})
and
(\ref{2.12})
that

(1.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X)

\displaystyle\geq\mathfrak{S}^{*}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}-A^{-1}X^{n-d}(\log A)^{-\delta/4}\geq A^{-1}X^{n-d}(\log A)^{-1/5}.

From the argument leading from
(\ref{2.11})
to
(\ref{2.13})
, one finds that it suffices to estimate the proportion of integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}(A;P)
in
\mathfrak{A}(A;P)
having the property that the inequality (
1.12
) or
(\ref{2.12})
fails. Define temporarily
\mathfrak{E}_{1}(A;P)
to be the set of tuples
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}(A;P)
for which
(\ref{2.11})
fails, and define
\mathfrak{E}_{2}(A;P)
to be the set of tuples
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}(A;P)
for which
(\ref{2.12})
fails.

First, we find by applying Theorem
1.1
that the number of integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
in
\mathfrak{A}(A;P)
, such that
(\ref{2.11})
fails, is bounded above by
A^{N-k}(\log A)^{-\delta/2}.
Thus, we have

(1.15)

\#\mathfrak{E}_{1}(A;P)\ll A^{N-k}(\log A)^{-\delta/2}.

Next, one finds by applying Theorem
1.2
that

(1.16)

\#\mathfrak{E}_{2}(A;P)\ll A^{N-k}(\log A)^{-\delta/(200n)}.

Therefore, the number of integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
in
\mathfrak{A}(A;P)
, such that when
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}(A;P)
, the inequality (
1.12
) or
(\ref{2.12})
fails, is
O(A^{N-k}(\log A)^{-\eta})
for some
\eta>0.

Meanwhile, note that since we have
N\geq 1000n^{2}8^{k}
and
P
is a non-singular polynomial in
N
variables having the property that
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0
has a nontrivial integer solution, it follows from
[\ref{ref8}]
that for sufficiently large
A>0
one has

(1.17)

\#\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[-A,A]^{N}|\ P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\}\sim cA^{N-k},

for some
c>0
depending on
P.
Hence, we conclude from
(\ref{2.15})
,
(\ref{2.16})
and
(\ref{2.222})
that the proportion of integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}(A;P)
in
\mathfrak{A}(A;P)
, having the property that the inequality (
1.12
) or
(\ref{2.12})
fails, is
O((\log A)^{-\eta})
for some
\eta>0.
By letting
A\rightarrow\infty,
this completes the proof of Theorem
\ref{thm1.3}.

∎

Corollary 1.4
.

The conclusions of Theorem
1.1
,
1.2
and
1.3
hold for
d\geq 14,

k\leq d
and
n\geq 32d+17
in place of the hypotheses on
n,d
and
k
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the conditions
d\geq 14,

k\leq d
and
n\geq 32d+17
imply the hypotheses on
n,d
and
k
in Theorem
\ref{thm2.2},

1.2
and
\ref{thm1.3}.

For
d\geq 14,
a modicum of computation reveals that we have

1000\cdot 8^{d}\leq\frac{33^{d-2}}{d^{2}}.

Then, we see that whenever
d\geq 14
and
n\geq 32d+17
, we obtain

1000\cdot 8^{d}\leq\frac{1}{d^{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{n+d-1}{d}\right)^{d-2}.

Hence, it follows that whenever
k\leq d
one has

\displaystyle 1000\cdot n^{2}\cdot 8^{k}\leq 1000\cdot 8^{d}\cdot(n+d-1)^{2}\leq\left(\frac{n+d-1}{d}\right)^{d}\leq\binom{n+d-1}{d}=N_{d,n}.

Furthermore, it implies that
N_{d,n}\geq 200k(k-1)2^{k-1}.

Additionally, with the same notation in Theorem
1.1
, we readily see that
n\geq 32d+17
implies that
n_{1}>2d.

∎

Remark 1
.

By our sequel joint work with H.Lee and S.Lee, for
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
satisfying that there exists
{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
with
P({\boldsymbol{b}})=0
such that
f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
has a smooth integer point, the proportion of integer vectors
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(A;P)
in
\mathfrak{A}(A;P)
converges to a positive number as
A\rightarrow\infty.
Hence, at least for those
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}],
our main theorems are indeed non-trivial ones.

We notice here that the required number of variables
n\geq 32d+17
in Corollary
1.4
is more restrictive than that required in the work of Browning, Le Boudec, and Sawin
[\ref{ref3}]
. However, we rather note that the success of establishing Theorem
1.1
,
1.2
and
1.3
illuminates the flexibility of the circle method in investigating the distribution of the set of
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
, whose associated equations
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
do not satisfy the Hasse principle. We note that the condition
k\geq 2
seems not to be removable in our argument. The case
k=1
rather seems accessible to tools used in
[\ref{ref3}]
, which mainly make use of the geometry of numbers.

We note that via the same argument described in this paper, one can readily achieve the same conclusion even if one replaces the condition
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0
with
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=m
for any
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
in
\mathbb{V}^{P}_{d,n}(A)
, under the assumption that
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=m
has a nontrivial integer solution. Furthermore, in general, our hope is that techniques described here may be useful in exploring rational points in a variety in
\mathbb{P}^{m_{1}}\times\mathbb{P}^{m_{2}}
with
m_{1},m_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}},
and confirming the Hasse principle for various classes of systems of homogeneous equations.

Stucture of the paper and notation

In section
2
, we record two auxiliary mean value estimates, which are Theorem
2.8
and Lemma
2.10
. The former one is required in the proof of Proposition
5.1
that is used in proving Theorem
1.2
, and the latter one is used in many places in this paper (proofs of Theorem
2.8
, Proposition
3.4
, Lemma
4.1
and Lemma
4.2
). Furthermore, in section
2
, we record several previous results in order to make this paper self-contained. Utilizing minor arcs estimates from section
3
, we prove Theorem
1.1
in section
4
. By making use of major arcs estimates from section
5
, we prove Theorem
1.2
in section
\ref{sec7}.

In this paper, we use bold symbols to denote vectors, which we consider as row vectors. For a given vector
\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, we write the
i
-th coordinate of
\boldsymbol{v}
by
(\boldsymbol{v})_{i}
or
v_{i}.
We use
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
for the inner product, and use
\|\cdot\|
for the Euclidean norm. We write
0\leq{\boldsymbol{x}}\leq X
to abbreviate the condition
0\leq x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\leq X.
We emphasize here that we preserve summation conditions until different conditions are specified. Additionally, for a prime
p
and natural numbers
n
and
h
, we use
p^{h}\|n
when
p^{h}|n
but
p^{h+1}\nmid n
. Throughout this paper, we use
\gg
and
\ll
to denote Vinogradov’s well-known notation, and write
e(z)
for
e^{2\pi iz}
. We use
A\asymp B
when both
A\ll B
and
A\gg B
hold. We adopt the convention that whenever
\epsilon
appears in a statement, then the statement holds for each
\epsilon>0
, with implicit constants depending on
\epsilon.

2.
Preliminary manoeuvre

Throughout this paper, we write

(2.1)

P({\boldsymbol{a}})=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\leq N}p_{j_{1}\cdots j_{k}}a_{j_{1}}a_{j_{2}}\cdots a_{j_{k}},

with
p_{j_{1}\cdots j_{k}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
symmetric in the suffixes
j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}.

In order to describe the argument in sections
2
and
3
, it is convenient to define differencing operators
\Delta_{1}
by

\Delta_{1}(P({\boldsymbol{x}});{\boldsymbol{h}})=P({\boldsymbol{x}}+{\boldsymbol{h}})-P({\boldsymbol{x}}),

and so we define
\Delta_{j}
for
j\geq 2
recursively by means of the relations

(2.2)

\Delta_{j}(P({\boldsymbol{x}});{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{h}}_{j})=\Delta_{1}(\Delta_{j-1}(P({\boldsymbol{x}});{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{h}}_{j-1});{\boldsymbol{h}}_{j}).

Furthermore, we define

(2.3)

\displaystyle\psi_{j}

\displaystyle:=\psi_{j}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)};P)

\displaystyle=k!\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j_{1},\ldots,j_{k-1}\leq N}p_{j_{1}\cdots j_{k-1}j}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)})_{j_{1}}\cdots({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)})_{j_{k-1}}.

2.1.
Previous results

In order to make this paper self-contained, we record several previous results in this section.

We denote
\|z\|=\min_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}|z-n|
, for the following lemma.

Lemma 2.1
.

([\ref{ref17}
,
Lemma
3.3
]
,
[\ref{ref8}
, Lemma 2.3
])

Let
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
be linear forms, defined by

L_{i}=\gamma_{i1}u_{1}+\cdots+\gamma_{in}u_{n},\ (1\leq i\leq n),

satisfying the symmetry condition
\gamma_{ij}=\gamma_{ji}.
Let
a>1
be real, and let
N(Z)
denote the number of sets of integers
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}
satisfying

\displaystyle|u_{i}|<aZ\ (i=1,\ldots,n),\ \|L_{1}\|<a^{-1}Z,\ldots,\|L_{n}\|<a^{-1}Z.

Then, if
0<Z_{1}\leq Z_{2}\leq 1,
we have

\frac{N(Z_{2})}{N(Z_{1})}\ll\left(\frac{Z_{2}}{Z_{1}}\right)^{n}.

Lemma 2.2
.

([\ref{ref2},\
Lemma 4.3
])

Let
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
and let
A,X\geq 1
. Suppose that
q\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}
are coprime with
|\alpha-a/q|\leq q^{-2}.
Then, one has

\displaystyle\sum_{-A\leq b\leq A}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq x\leq X}e(\alpha bx^{d})\biggr|^{2^{d-1}}\ll AX^{2^{d-1}}\bigl(q^{-1}+X^{-1}+q(AX^{d})^{-1}\bigr)(AXq)^{\epsilon}.

Furthermore, this lemma in conjunction with Hölder’s inequality yields that

(2.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{-A\leq b\leq A}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq x\leq X}e(\alpha bx^{d})\biggr|^{2}\ll A^{1+\epsilon}B^{2+\epsilon}\bigl(q^{-1}+X^{-1}+q(AX^{d})^{-1}\bigr)^{2^{2-d}}

Let
U_{t}(A,B)
denote the number of solutions of

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{t}a_{j}(x_{j}^{k}-y_{j}^{k})=0

in integers
a_{j},x_{j},y_{j}
satisfying

0<|a_{j}|\leq A,\ |x_{j}|\leq B,\ |y_{j}|\leq B.

Lemma 2.3
.

([\ref{ref2}
,
\text{Theorem 2.5}])

Let
t\geq 2.
Then, for real numbers
A
and
B
with
A\geq 2B^{k}\geq 1
, one has

U_{t}(A,B)\ll(AB)^{t}+A^{t-1}B^{2t-k+\epsilon}.

We note that even if one replaces the condition
0<|a_{j}|\leq A
by
0\leq|a_{j}|\leq A
in
U_{t}(A,B)
, one readily obtains

(2.5)

U_{t}(A,B)\ll(AB)^{t}+A^{t-1}B^{2t-k+\epsilon},

under the same conditions as imposed in Lemma
2.3
.

For here and throughout this paper, we define

\mathcal{R}_{m}(Q)=\{{\boldsymbol{b}}\in({\mathbb{Z}}/Q{\mathbb{Z}})^{m}|\ \text{gcd}(Q,{\boldsymbol{b}})=1\},

and define
v_{p^{r}}(\boldsymbol{v})
with
\boldsymbol{v}\in({\mathbb{Z}}/p^{r}{\mathbb{Z}})^{n}
as the largest integer
s\in\{0,\ldots,r\}
such that we have
\boldsymbol{v}\equiv{\bf 0}\ \text{mod}\ p^{s}.
Furthermore, in order to describe Lemma
2.4
, for
e\in\{0,\ldots,r\},
we temporarily define the set
\mathcal{R}^{(e)}_{N}(p^{r})
to be the set of
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{R}_{N}(p^{r})
such that there exists
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{r})
having the property that
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}
and
v_{p^{r}}(\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))=e.

Lemma 2.4
.

([\ref{ref3},\text{Lemma 5.7}])

Let
d\geq 2
and
n\geq 4.
Let also
p
be a prime number and
r\geq 1.
For
e\in\{0,\ldots,r\}
and
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{R}^{(e)}_{N}(p^{r}),
we have

\frac{1}{p^{rn-r}}\cdot\#\{{\boldsymbol{g}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{r})|\ f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{g}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\}\geq\frac{1}{p^{(e+1)(n-1)}}.

Lemma 2.5
.

([\ref{ref3},\text{Proof of Lemma 5.6}])

For
{\boldsymbol{g}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
with
p\nmid{\boldsymbol{g}}
and for
e\in\{0,\ldots,r\}
, we have

\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{R}_{N}(p^{r})\middle|\ \begin{aligned} (f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{g}}),\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{g}}))\equiv\boldsymbol{0}\ \text{mod}\ p^{e}\end{aligned}\right\}\leq p^{r(N-n)+(r-e)n}.

For any
u>0
, we let

B_{m}(u)=\{{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}|\ \|{\boldsymbol{y}}\|\leq u\}
. For
A>0

we set

H_{n}(A)=B_{n}\left(1+\frac{1}{A}\right)\setminus B_{n}\left(1-\frac{1}{A}\right)

Lemma 2.6
.

([\ref{ref3},\text{Proof of Lemma 5.8}])

For fixed
{\boldsymbol{y}}\in H_{n}(A)
with
A>0,
we have

\#\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[-A,A]^{N}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}|\ \|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})\|\ll 1\}\ll A^{N-n}.

Lemma 2.7
.

([\ref{ref3},\text{Proof of Lemma 5.9}])

When
\lambda=O(1)
and
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}|\ \|{\boldsymbol{x}}\|=1\}
, we have

\text{mes}\left(\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in B_{N}(N)\middle|\ \begin{aligned} |f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})|\leq\lambda^{2},\ \|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\|\leq 2\lambda\end{aligned}\right\}\right)\ll\lambda^{n+1}.

2.2.
Auxiliary mean value estimates

In this section, we provide a mean value estimate which plays a crucial role, in section 5.2, in verifying that
\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}
is rarely small over
{\boldsymbol{a}}
with
\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A
and
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0
. Since this mean value may be of independent interest, we record it in this separate section.

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
be a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N
variables of degree
k
. Suppose that
P({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
has a nontrivial integer solution. Suppose that
W
is an integer with
W\in[1,A^{2/3}]
for sufficiently large
A
. Define

\mathbf{N}(A)=\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}\in[-A,A]^{N}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\middle|\ P({\boldsymbol{x}})=P({\boldsymbol{y}})=0,\ {\boldsymbol{x}}\equiv{\boldsymbol{y}}\ \text{mod}\ W\right\}.

Then, whenever
N\geq 18k(k-1)4^{k+3}
, we have

(2.6)

\mathbf{N}(A)\asymp A^{2N-2k}W^{1-N}.

Since
P({\boldsymbol{x}})
is a homogeneous polynomial in
N
variables of degree
k
, probabilistic arguments deliver the expectation that the number of integer solutions
{\boldsymbol{x}}
and
{\boldsymbol{y}}
satisfying
P({\boldsymbol{x}})=P({\boldsymbol{y}})=0
is
O(A^{2N-2k})
, and that the additional congruence condition on
{\boldsymbol{x}}
and
{\boldsymbol{y}}
, which is
{\boldsymbol{x}}\equiv{\boldsymbol{y}}
(mod
W
), provides an extra factor
W^{1-N}.
Therefore, we see that the right-hand side in (
2.6
) has the expected magnitude up to a constant.
Without the congruence condition on
{\boldsymbol{x}}
and
{\boldsymbol{y}}
, the expected magnitude immediately follows by the work of Birch [
References
] whenever
N>(k-1)2^{k}
(see also [
References
]). However, for
\mathbf{N}(A)
stated in
(\ref{5.1})
, Birch’s argument seems not capable of immediately deriving the expected magnitude.

In this subsection, we introduce an argument that may be helpful in general when one deduces an expected magnitude for the number of integer solutions of a system of equations together with congruence conditions.
For the upper bound of
\mathbf{N}(A)
, we first represent
\mathbf{N}(A)
by mean values of exponential sums via orthogonality. Next, on observing that the condition
P({\boldsymbol{x}})=P({\boldsymbol{y}})=0
obviously induces
P({\boldsymbol{x}})\equiv P({\boldsymbol{y}})\equiv 0
(mod
W
) and by taking this induced condition into account in mean values of exponential sums, we are capable of drawing both information about
P({\boldsymbol{x}})=P({\boldsymbol{y}})=0
and
{\boldsymbol{x}}\equiv{\boldsymbol{y}}
(mod
W
) via suitable applications of the Weyl differencing argument. Eventually, we obtain the expected upper bound for
\mathbf{N}(A),
by applying classical estimates for major and minor arcs together with such information.
For the lower bound of
\mathbf{N}(A)
, we first obtain an upper bound for the quantity

(2.7)

\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{b}}\in[1,W]^{N}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\middle|\ P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W\right\},

with
W\in{\mathbb{N}}.
Next, by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality together with the upper bound for
(\ref{quantity})
, we obtain the expected lower bound for
\mathbf{N}(A)
.

By making use of the same idea of this proof, we are also capable of dealing with the case
W=A^{\mu}
with
2/3<\mu<1.
However, the required number of variables
N
becomes more restrictive, as
\mu\rightarrow 1.
As we will see in the proof of Proposition
5.1
, the bound for
\mathbf{N}(A)
with
1\leq W\leq A^{2/3}
is enough for our purpose, and thus we record this theorem only with
\mu\leq 2/3.

For the proof of Theorem
2.8
, we require two auxiliary lemmas. The first lemma of these gives the upper bound for

\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{b}}\in[1,W]^{N}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\middle|\ P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W\right\},

with
W\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
be a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N
variables of degree
k.
Suppose that
W
is a natural number, and that
\mathfrak{p}
is the smallest prime divisor of
W.
Then, whenever
N>(k-1)2^{k+1},
one has

\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{b}}\in[1,W]^{N}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\middle|\ P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W\right\}=W^{N-1}+O\left(W^{N-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{-N/(2^{k}(k-1))}\right).

Proof.

For simplicity, we write

(2.8)

N_{1}(W)=\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{b}}\in[1,W]^{N}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\middle|\ P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W\right\}.

By applying orthogonality, we have

N_{1}(W)=W^{-1}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq W}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r\leq W}e\left(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{b}})r}{W}\right).

By splitting the sum over
r
in terms of values of
r_{0}=W/(W,r)
, we see that

(2.9)

N_{1}(W)=W^{N-1}+W^{-1}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{0}|W\\
r_{0}\neq 1\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r\leq W\\
(W,r)=W/r_{0}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq W}e\left(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{b}})r}{W}\right).

For fixed
r_{0}
and
r
with
r_{0}|W
and
r_{0}=W/(W,r)
, we write
\widetilde{r}=r/(W,r),
and so
(\widetilde{r},r_{0})=1
. Then, since
P
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial, it follows by the Weyl type estimate for exponential sums over minor arcs [
References
, Lemma 5.4] that

(2.10)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{0}|W\\
r_{0}\neq 1\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r\leq W\\
(W,r)=W/r_{0}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq W}e\left(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{b}})r}{W}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{0}|W\\
r_{0}\neq 1\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\widetilde{r}\leq r_{0}\\
(\widetilde{r},r_{0})=1\end{subarray}}\left|\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq W}e\left(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{b}})\widetilde{r}}{r_{0}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{0}|W\\
r_{0}\neq 1\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\widetilde{r}\leq r_{0}\\
(\widetilde{r},r_{0})=1\end{subarray}}(W/r_{0})^{N}\left|\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq r_{0}}e\left(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{b}})\widetilde{r}}{r_{0}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\ll\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{0}|W\\
r_{0}\neq 1\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\widetilde{r}\leq r_{0}\\
(\widetilde{r},r_{0})=1\end{subarray}}W^{N}\cdot r_{0}^{-N/(2^{k-1}(k-1))+\epsilon}.

Recall the definition of
\mathfrak{p}
in the statement of Lemma
2.9
. Then, on noting that for all
\sigma>1
one has

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{0}|W\\
r_{0}\neq 1\end{subarray}}r_{0}^{-\sigma}\leq\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{p}\leq r_{0}\end{subarray}}r_{0}^{-\sigma}\ll\mathfrak{p}^{1-\sigma},

it follows from (
2.10
) and the hypothesis
N>2^{k+1}(k-1)
that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{0}|W\\
r_{0}\neq 1\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{(W,r)=W/r_{0}}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq W}e\left(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{b}})r}{W}\right)

\displaystyle\ll\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{0}|W\\
r_{0}\neq 1\end{subarray}}W^{N}\cdot r_{0}^{1-N/(2^{k-1}(k-1))+\epsilon}

\displaystyle\ll W^{N}\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{2-N/(2^{k-1}(k-1))+\epsilon}

\displaystyle\leq W^{N}\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{-N/(2^{k}(k-1))}.

On substituting this bound into
(\ref{3.73.73.7})
, we conclude that

N_{1}(W)=W^{N-1}+O\left(W^{N-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{-N/(2^{k}(k-1))}\right).

∎

Throughout this paper, we only use certain cases (
l=1,\ l=k-1
) of the following lemma, however, we record this in full generality for convenience of the statement and future works.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\beta
be a real and
l
be a natural number with
1\leq l\leq k-1
. Suppose that
A_{1},\ldots,A_{l}
are sufficiently large positive numbers where
A_{1}\leq\cdots\leq A_{l+1}
with
1\leq\frac{\log A_{l+1}}{\log A_{1}}\leq 3
. Suppose that
B
is a non-negative integer. Define

\mathcal{S}(\beta)=\displaystyle\sum_{y}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)}}\cdots\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l)}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+1)}\in\mathcal{B}}e(\beta\Delta_{l}(P({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+1)});{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l)}))\biggr|^{2},

where the summation in
y
is over
[-B,B]\cap{\mathbb{Z}}
and sums in
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}
are over

[-A_{i},A_{i}]^{N}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\ (i=1,\ldots,l)
,
and the sum in
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+1)}
is over a rectangular box
\mathcal{B}\subseteq[-A_{l+1},A_{l+1}]^{N}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
(
or the empty set
)
depending on
y,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l)}
. Then, for any
0<\sigma<1
, whenever

N\geq 3(\sigma+2)\sigma^{-2}k(k-1)2^{k-l},

we have

(2.11)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{S}(\beta)^{\sigma}d\beta\ll(B+1)^{\sigma}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{2})^{\sigma N}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l})^{-1},

where the implicit constant depends on
k.

We notice that the variable
y
in the outer sum of
\mathcal{S}(\beta)
is not associated with argument of the exponential sum
\mathcal{S}(\beta)
, that is
\Delta_{l}(P({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+1)});{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l)}),
and is only involved in the range of
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+1)}
of the innermost sum. We note that Lemma
2.10
will be required not only for the proof of Theorem
2.8
but also in other places in this paper (proofs of Proposition
3.4
, Lemma
4.1
and Lemma
4.2
), and that corresponding exponential sums
\mathcal{S}(\beta)
in these places are obtained by the Weyl differencing argument.

The proof of Lemma
2.10
makes use of the standard treatment of major arcs and minor arcs used in [
References
]. We emphasize here that the improvement of the required number of variables
N
, described in Theorem
2.8
and Lemma
2.10
, may slightly improve the range of the degree
k
of
P({\boldsymbol{a}})
in Corollary
1.4
. However, as we implicitly reflected in the proof of Corollary
1.4
, since we are rather interested in the required number of variables
n
of
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}
in terms of the degree
d
of
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}
so that the hypotheses on
n,d
, and
k
in Theorem
1.1
,
1.2
and
1.3
hold for large
d
, we do not put our effort into optimizing the required number of variables
N
throughout this paper.

Remark 2
.

One of the ways to improve bound for the required number of variables
N
is to make use of pruning arguments to deal with minor arcs estimates, used in [
References
]. It may provide sharper bounds for the required number of variables
N
than that stated in Theorem
2.8
and Lemma
2.10
.

For the proof of Lemma
2.10
, we recall the definition (
2.3
) of
\psi_{j}
and it is convenient to define

(2.12)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{P}(\beta;A_{1},\ldots,A_{k-1},A_{k})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}\\
\|{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)}\|_{\infty}\leq A_{1},\ldots,\|{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}\|_{\infty}\leq A_{k-1}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N}\min(A_{k},\|\beta\psi_{j}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)};P)\|^{-1}).

Proof of Lemma
2.10
.

We denote
\|z\|=\min_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}|z-n|
, for this proof.
By applying the Weyl differencing argument to
\mathcal{S}(\beta)
, whenever
l\leq k-2
we observe that

(2.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\beta)\ll(B+1)(A_{1}\cdots A_{l+1}^{2})^{N}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l+1}^{k-l})^{-2^{2-k+l}N}\Gamma_{P}(\beta;A_{1},A_{1},\ldots,A_{k})^{2^{2-k+l}},

with
A_{l+1}=\cdots=A_{k}.
When
l=k-1,
by the trivial estimate

\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{B}}e(\beta\Delta_{l}(P({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k)};{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)})))\biggr|\leq A_{k}^{N},

one has

(2.14)

\mathcal{S}(\beta)\ll(B+1)A_{k}^{N}\Gamma_{P}(\beta;A_{1},A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}).

Recall the definition (
2.3
) of

\psi_{j}:=\psi_{j}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)};P).

Then, by using the argument in the proof of [
References
, Lemma 3.2], we deduce that

(2.15)

\Gamma_{P}(\beta;A_{1},\ldots,A_{k-1},A_{k})\ll A_{k}^{N}(\log A_{k})^{N}N(A_{1},\ldots,A_{k-1},A_{k}^{-1};\beta),

where

(2.16)

\displaystyle N(A_{1},\ldots,A_{k-1},A_{k}^{-1};\beta)

\displaystyle=\#\{\|{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}\|_{\infty}\leq A_{i}\ (i=1,\ldots,k-1)|\ \|\beta\psi_{j}\|<A_{k}^{-1}\ (j=1,\ldots,N)\}.

Meanwhile, we see that
\psi_{j}
is a linear form in
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}
for fixed
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l)}\ (1\leq l\leq k-1,l\neq i)
. Hence, for any positive number
X
with
X<A_{1},
we set
n=N,

L_{i}=\psi_{i}
and

\displaystyle Z_{1}=X^{\frac{1}{2}(i+1)}(A_{1}\cdots A_{i}A_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle Z_{2}=(A_{i}X^{i-1})^{\frac{1}{2}}(A_{1}\cdots A_{i-1}A_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle a=(A_{1}\cdots A_{i}A_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}}X^{-\frac{1}{2}(i-1)}

We note here that the condition
X<A_{1}
and the hypothesis
A_{1}\leq\cdots\leq A_{l+1}
in the statement of Lemma
2.10
ensure that
a>1
and
0<Z_{1}<Z_{2}\leq 1.
It is possible to replace the hypothesis
A_{1}\leq\cdots\leq A_{l}
by a weaker condition
\text{max}_{1\leq i\leq l}A_{i}\leq A_{l+1}
, although we have to instead choose
X
less than
\text{min}_{1\leq i\leq l}A_{i}
. However, we adapt the hypothesis
A_{1}\leq\cdots\leq A_{l}\leq A_{l+1}
, purely for notational convenience throughout this proof. With these quantities, it follows by applying Lemma
2.1

that for
i
with
1\leq i\leq k-2
we have

\displaystyle N(X,\ldots,X,A_{i},\ldots,A_{k-1},X^{i-1}(A_{1}\cdots A_{i-1}A_{k})^{-1};\beta)

\displaystyle\ll(A_{i}/X)^{N}N(X,\ldots,X,A_{i+1},\ldots,A_{k-1},X^{i}(A_{1}\cdots A_{i}A_{k})^{-1};\beta),

and for
i=k-1
we have

\displaystyle N(X,\ldots,X,A_{k-1},X^{k-2}(A_{1}\cdots A_{k-2}A_{k})^{-1};\beta)

\displaystyle\ll(A_{k-1}/X)^{N}N(X,\ldots,X,X^{k-1}(A_{1}\cdots A_{k-1}A_{k})^{-1};\beta).

Hence, we deduce by applying this with
i=1,\ldots,k-1
recursively that

(2.17)

\displaystyle N(A_{1},\ldots,A_{k-1},A_{k}^{-1};\beta)

\displaystyle\ll(A_{1}/X)^{N}\cdots(A_{k-1}/X)^{N}N(X,\ldots,X,X^{k-1}(A_{1}\cdots A_{k})^{-1};\beta).

Furthermore, on noting that
P
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial and that
A_{l+1}=\cdots=A_{k}
, application of the argument used in [
References
, Lemma 3.3, Lemma 3.4] together with (
2.15
) and (
2.17
) readily delivers that either

(2.18)

(i)\ \Gamma_{P}(\beta;A_{1},\ldots,A_{k})\ll(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l})^{N}(\log A_{l+1})^{N+1}X^{-N}

or there exist
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
q\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

(2.19)

(ii)\ (q,a)=1,\ q\leq X^{k-1}\ \text{and}\ |q\beta-a|\leq X^{k-1}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l})^{-1}.

In order to analyze the mean value
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{S}(\beta)^{\sigma}d\beta
in
(\ref{3.33.3})
, we must define

\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}(Q)=\displaystyle\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq a\leq q\leq Q\\
(q,a)=1\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}_{q,a}(Q),

where

\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}_{q,a}(Q)=\{\beta\in[0,1)|\ |\beta-a/q|\leq q^{-1}Q(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l})^{-1}\},

and define
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}(Q)=[0,1)\setminus\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}(Q).
Then, we observe that

(2.20)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{S}(\beta)^{\sigma}d\beta\ll\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2},

where

(2.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{1}=\displaystyle\int_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}(A_{1}^{\eta})}\mathcal{S}(\beta)^{\sigma}d\beta\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathcal{J}_{2}=\displaystyle\int_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}(A_{1}^{\eta})}\mathcal{S}(\beta)^{\sigma}d\beta

in which
\eta=\frac{\sigma}{2(\sigma+2)}.

We first analyze
\mathcal{J}_{1}
by making use of the standard treatment of major arcs used in [
References
, section 5]. Let
\beta\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}_{q,a}(A_{1}^{\eta}),
and write
\delta=\beta-a/q
with
|\delta|\leq q^{-1}A^{\eta}_{1}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l})^{-1}.
On recalling the definition of
\mathcal{S}(\beta)
, one finds that

\mathcal{S}(\beta)=\displaystyle\sum_{y}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l)}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+1)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+2)}\in\mathcal{B}}e(\beta\phi_{1}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+1)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+2)})),

where

\displaystyle\phi_{1}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+1)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+2)})

\displaystyle:=\phi_{1}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+1)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+2)};{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l)})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{l}(P({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+1)});{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l)})-\Delta_{l}(P({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+2)});{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l)}).

Let us write
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}=q{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(i)}+{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(i)}\ (i=1,\ldots,l+2)
, where
1\leq{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(i)}\leq q
and
{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(i)}
runs over boxes so that
\|{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}\|_{\infty}\leq A_{i}
with
i=1,\ldots,l
, and
1\leq{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+1)},{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+2)}\leq q
and
{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l+1)},{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l+2)}
run over boxes so that
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+1)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+2)}\in\mathcal{B}
.

(2.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\beta)=\displaystyle\sum_{y}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+2)}}e\left(\frac{a}{q}\phi_{1}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+1)},{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+2)};{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)})\right)\mathcal{T}(\delta,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+2)}),

where

\mathcal{T}(\delta,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+2)})=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l+2)}}e\left(\delta\phi_{2}({\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l+1)},{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l+2)};{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l)})\right)

in which

\displaystyle\phi_{2}({\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l+1)},{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l+2)})

\displaystyle:=\phi_{2}({\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l+1)},{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l+2)};{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l)})

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(q{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l+1)}+{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+1)},q{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l+2)}+{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+2)};q{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(1)}+{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,q{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l)}+{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)}).

By the definition of
\mathcal{B},
we see that the ranges of
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+1)}=q{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l+1)}+{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+1)}
,
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l+2)}=q{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l+2)}+{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+2)}
are equal and depend at most on
y,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)}
and
{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(l)},

Then, by the same treatment used in [
References
, Lemma 5.1], we find that there exist boxes
\mathfrak{D}_{i}\ (i=1,\ldots,l+2)
with side length at most
2
such that

(2.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\delta,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+2)})-\mathcal{U}(\delta)\ll E_{1}+E_{2},

where

\displaystyle E_{1}=q(A_{1}/q)^{N}(A_{2}/q)^{N}\cdots(A_{l+1}/q)^{2N}A_{2}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l}|\delta|

\displaystyle E_{2}\leq(A_{1}/q)^{N-1}(A_{2}/q)^{N}\cdots(A_{l+1}/q)^{2N}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(\delta)=\displaystyle\int_{q\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(1)}+{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)}\in A_{1}\mathfrak{D}_{1}}\cdots\displaystyle\int_{q\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(l+2)}+{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+2)}\in A_{l+2}\mathfrak{D}_{l+2}}e(\delta\phi_{2}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(l+1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(l+2)}))d\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(l+2)}\cdots d\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(1)}

with
A_{l+1}=A_{l+2}
and
\mathfrak{D}_{l+1}=\mathfrak{D}_{l+2}
depending at most on
y,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(1)},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(l)}.

Notice that
E_{2}
accounts for the initial and final interval with length at most
O(1)
for each coordinate of
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(j)}(j=1,\ldots,l+2)
in
\mathcal{U}(\delta).

Furthermore, by change of variables
q\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(i)}+\boldsymbol{z}^{(i)}=A_{i}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}
with
1\leq i\leq l+2
and
A_{l+1}=A_{l+2}
, we find that

(2.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(\delta)=q^{-(l+2)N}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{2})^{N}I(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l}\delta),

where

I(\delta)=\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{D}_{1}}\cdots\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{D}_{l+2}}e(\delta\phi_{1}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(l+1)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(l+2)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)}\ldots,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(l)}))d\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(l+2)}\cdots d\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)}.

Notice here that
I(\delta)
depends at most on
y,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)}.

Meanwhile, on recalling the definition of
\phi_{1}
, observe that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+2)}}e\left(\frac{a}{q}\phi_{1}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+1)},{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l+2)};{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)})\right),

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{z}}\leq q}e\left(\frac{a}{q}\Delta_{l}(P({\boldsymbol{z}});{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)})\right)\biggr|^{2}.

For simplicity, we write the inner sum over
{\boldsymbol{z}}
by

S_{q,a}:=S_{q,a}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)})=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{z}}\leq q}e\left(\frac{a}{q}\Delta_{l}(P({\boldsymbol{z}});{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)})\right).

Hence, by substituting
(\ref{3.123.12})
into (
2.23
) and that into (
2.22
), we obtain

(2.25)

\mathcal{S}(\beta)=\displaystyle\sum_{y}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)}}|S_{q,a}|^{2}\cdot q^{-(l+2)N}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{2})^{N}I(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l}\delta)+O(E(\beta)),

where

E(\beta)=(B+1)q^{(l+2)N}(E_{1}+E_{2}).

This is possible because
I(\delta)
depends at most on
y,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)}.

For simplicity, we write

\mathcal{I}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l}\delta)=\sup_{{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{-B\leq y\leq B}I(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l}\delta)\biggr|

and

\mathcal{L}(q,a)=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)}}|S_{q,a}|^{2}\cdot q^{-(l+2)N}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{2})^{N}.

Then, it follows from (
2.25
) that

(2.26)

\mathcal{S}(\beta)\ll\mathcal{L}(q,a)\cdot\mathcal{I}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l}\delta)+|E(\beta)|.

Meanwhile, since
\beta\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}_{q,a}(A_{1}^{\eta})
, we find from the definition of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
that

(2.27)

\displaystyle E(\beta)\ll(B+1)(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{2})^{N}A_{1}^{-1+\eta}.

Note that

\text{mes}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}(A_{1}^{\eta}))\leq(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l})^{-1}A_{1}^{2\eta}.

Thus, on substituting
(\ref{3.143.14})
into
(\ref{8.14})
and that into
\mathcal{J}_{1}
in (
2.21
), we deduce from the elementary inequality
(a+b)^{\sigma}\leq a^{\sigma}+b^{\sigma}
with
0<\sigma<1
and
a,b>0
that

(2.28)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{1}\ll\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq q\leq A_{1}^{\eta}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq a\leq q\\
(q,a)=1\end{subarray}}(\mathcal{L}(q,a))^{\sigma}\mathfrak{J}(A_{1}^{\eta})+E,

where

\mathfrak{J}(A_{1}^{\eta})=\displaystyle\int_{|\delta|\leq q^{-1}A^{\eta}_{1}\left(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l}\right)^{-1}}\left(\mathcal{I}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l}\delta))\right)^{\sigma}d\delta

and

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=O\left(\text{mes}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}(A_{1}^{\eta}))(B+1)^{\sigma}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{2})^{\sigma N}A_{1}^{-\sigma+\sigma\eta}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left((B+1)^{\sigma}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{2})^{\sigma N}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l})^{-1}A_{1}^{-\sigma+(\sigma+2)\eta}\right).

By the same treatment used in [
References
, Lemma 5.2, Lemma 5.4] together with (
2.23
) using
q=1,a=0
and with the Weyl type estimate over minor arcs for the exponential sum
S(\beta)
derived by (
2.13
), (
2.18
) and (
2.19
), one infers that

\mathcal{I}(\gamma)\ll(B+1)\text{min}(1,|\gamma|^{-2^{2-k+l}N/(k-1)+\epsilon})

and

\mathcal{L}(q,a)\ll q^{-2^{2-k+l}N/(k-1)+\epsilon}\cdot(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{2})^{N}.

Hence, whenever
N\geq 3(k-1)2^{k-l+2}\sigma^{-1}
and
1\leq l\leq k-2
, one infers that

(2.29)

\displaystyle(i)\ \mathfrak{J}(A^{\eta}_{1})\ll(B+1)^{\sigma}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l})^{-1}\ \text{uniformly in}\ q\geq 1.

\displaystyle(ii)\ \displaystyle\sum_{1\leq q\leq A_{1}^{\eta}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq a\leq q\\
(q,a)=1\end{subarray}}(\mathcal{L}(q,a))^{\sigma}\ll(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{2})^{N\sigma}.

By using (
2.14
) in place of
(\ref{3.6})
, when
l=k-1
and
N\geq 3(k-1)\sigma^{-1}
, we obtain the same estimates with (
2.29
).
Hence, since
\eta=\frac{\sigma}{2(\sigma+2)}
, on substituting
(\ref{3.2020})
into (
2.28
) we conclude that

(2.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{1}\ll(B+1)^{\sigma}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l+1}^{2})^{N\sigma}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l})^{-1}.

Next, we turn to estimate
\mathcal{J}_{2}
in
(\ref{3.73.7})
. By (
2.13
), (
2.18
) and
(\ref{3.8})
, whenever
\beta\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}(A_{1}^{\eta})
, one easily infers that when
1\leq l\leq k-2

\mathcal{S}(\beta)\ll(B+1)(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{2})^{N}(\log A_{l+1})^{N+1}A_{1}^{-2^{2-k+l}(k-1)^{-1}N\eta}.

By using (
2.14
) in place of
(\ref{3.6}),
when
l=k-1
, we obtain the bound

\mathcal{S}(\beta)\ll(B+1)(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{2})^{N}(\log A_{l+1})^{N+1}A_{1}^{-(k-1)^{-1}N\eta}.

Therefore, on recalling the hypothesis
1\leq\frac{\log A_{l+1}}{\log A_{1}}\leq 3
in the statement of Lemma
2.10
, in all cases, it follows that whenever
N\geq 6k(k-1)2^{k-l-2}\eta^{-1}\sigma^{-1}
one has

(2.31)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}\ll\sup_{\beta\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}(A_{1}^{\eta})}\mathcal{S}(\beta)^{\sigma}

\displaystyle=o\left((B+1)^{\sigma}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{2})^{\sigma N}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l})^{-1}\right).

The inequality (
2.20
) with (
2.30
) and (
2.31
) delivers that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{S}(\beta)^{\sigma}d\beta\ll(B+1)^{\sigma}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{2})^{\sigma N}(A_{1}\cdots A_{l}A_{l+1}^{k-l})^{-1},

and thus this completes the proof of Lemma
\ref{lem3.2}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

We shall first derive the upper bound for
\mathbf{N}(A).

By making the trivial observation that whenever
P({\boldsymbol{x}})=P({\boldsymbol{y}})=0
, one has
P({\boldsymbol{x}})\equiv P({\boldsymbol{y}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W
, it follows by orthogonality and by applying the triangle inequality that

(2.32)

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}(A)

\displaystyle=W^{-N}\displaystyle\int_{[0,1)^{2}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{l}}\leq W\\
{\boldsymbol{l}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}-A\leq{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{x}})\equiv P({\boldsymbol{y}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W\\
{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\end{subarray}}e(\alpha P({\boldsymbol{x}})+\beta P({\boldsymbol{y}}))e\left(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{y}},{\boldsymbol{l}}\rangle}{W}\right)d\alpha\ d\beta

\displaystyle\leq W^{-N}\displaystyle\int_{[0,1)^{2}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{l}}\leq W\\
{\boldsymbol{l}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\end{subarray}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}-A\leq{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{x}})\equiv P({\boldsymbol{y}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W\\
{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\end{subarray}}e(\alpha P({\boldsymbol{x}})+\beta P({\boldsymbol{y}}))e\left(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{y}},{\boldsymbol{l}}\rangle}{W}\right)\biggr|d\alpha\ d\beta.

We find by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that the last expression in
(\ref{5.25.2})
is bounded above by

(2.33)

W^{-N}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\Xi(\alpha)^{1/2}d\alpha\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\Xi(\beta)^{1/2}d\beta,

where

\Xi(\xi)=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{l}}\leq W}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}-A\leq{\boldsymbol{x}}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{x}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W\end{subarray}}e(\xi P({\boldsymbol{x}}))e\left(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{l}}\rangle}{W}\right)\biggr|^{2}.

By orthogonality again, the sum
\Xi(\xi)
is seen to be

\Xi(\xi)=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{l}}\leq W}\biggl|W^{-1}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r\leq W}\displaystyle\sum_{-A\leq{\boldsymbol{x}}\leq A}e(\xi P({\boldsymbol{x}}))e\left(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{l}}\rangle}{W}\right)e\biggl(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{x}})r}{W}\biggr)\biggr|^{2}.

We now analyze the sum
\Xi(\xi).
Define

S(r/W,\xi)=\displaystyle\sum_{-2A/W\leq{\boldsymbol{h}}\leq 2A/W}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in I_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}}e(\xi(P({\boldsymbol{x}}+W{\boldsymbol{h}})-P({\boldsymbol{x}})))e\biggl(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{x}})r}{W}\biggr),

where
I_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}=\{-A\leq{\boldsymbol{x}}\leq A|\ -A\leq{\boldsymbol{x}}+W{\boldsymbol{h}}\leq A\}
. Then,
by squaring out and inverting the order of summation, we see by applying orthogonality with respect to variables
{\boldsymbol{l}}
and applying the change of variables with respect to
{\boldsymbol{x}}
that

(2.34)

\Xi(\xi)=W^{N-1}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r\leq W}S(r/W,\xi).

In order to apply the Weyl differencing argument to the sum
S(r/W,\xi),
we recall the definition
(\ref{3.3})
of the differencing operator
\Delta_{j}
, and define

\displaystyle U_{1}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)})

\displaystyle:=U_{1}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)};{\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{k-1}(P({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)});W{\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)})

\displaystyle=W\Delta_{k-1}(P({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)});{\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)})

and

\displaystyle U_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)})

\displaystyle:=U_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)};{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)})=\Delta_{k-2}(P({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)});{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}).

Notice here that
U_{1}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)})
is a polynomial in
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)}
of degree
1
, and
U_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)})
is a polynomial in
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)}
of degree
2.
Furthermore, define

\mathcal{B}^{(0)}:=\mathcal{B}^{(0)}({\boldsymbol{h}})=\left\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in[-A,A]^{N}\middle|\ {\boldsymbol{x}}+W{\boldsymbol{h}}\in[-A,A]^{N}\right\}

and define
\mathcal{B}^{(j)},
when
j\geq 1
, by recursively setting

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{(1)}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{B}^{(1)}({\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}^{(0)}\cap\left\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in[-A,A]^{N}\middle|\ {\boldsymbol{x}}+{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}\in\mathcal{B}^{(0)}\right\}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{(j)}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{B}^{(j)}({\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-j)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}^{(j-1)}\cap\left\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in[-A,A]^{N}\middle|\ {\boldsymbol{x}}+{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-j)}\in\mathcal{B}^{(j-1)}\right\}.

Additionally, we define

T(r/W,\xi)=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)}}e(\xi U_{1}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)}))e((r/W)U_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)}))\biggr|,

where
-2A/W\leq{\boldsymbol{h}}\leq 2A/W
,
-2A\leq{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}\leq 2A
, and
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)}
runs over the rectangular box
\mathcal{B}^{(k-2)}.

Then, on observing that
P({\boldsymbol{x}}+W{\boldsymbol{h}})-P({\boldsymbol{x}})=\Delta_{1}(P({\boldsymbol{x}});W{\boldsymbol{h}})
and by applying the standard Weyl differencing argument to the sum
S(r/W,\xi)
, we deduce that

(2.35)

S(r/W,\xi)\ll(A/W)^{N-2^{2-k}N}A^{N-(k-1)2^{2-k}N}T(r/W,\xi)^{2^{2-k}}.

In order to apply the Weyl differencing argument once again to
T(r/W,\xi)
, we define

\displaystyle U_{3}({\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{k-1}(P({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)});{\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)})

\displaystyle U_{4}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{k-1}(P({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)});{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)}),

and

V(r/W,\xi)=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}}e(W\xi U_{3}({\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)}))e((r/W)U_{4}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)})),

where
-2A\leq{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)}\leq 2A
and
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}
runs over the rectangular box
\mathcal{B}^{(k-1)}.

Notice from the definition of
\mathcal{B}^{(k-1)}
that the range of
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}
depends on
{\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}
.
Then, by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Weyl differencing, one deduces that

(2.36)

\displaystyle T(r/W,\xi)\leq W^{-N/2}A^{(k-1)N/2}V(r/W,\xi)^{1/2}.

By applying the triangle inequality and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality again, we find that

(2.37)

\displaystyle V(r/W,\xi)

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}}|Y(r/W,\xi)|\leq A^{(k-1)N/2}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}}|Y(r/W,\xi)|^{2}\biggr)^{1/2},

where

Y(r/W,\xi)=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}}e(W\xi U_{3}({\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)}))e((r/W)U_{4}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)})).

Meanwhile, we see from the triangle inequality that

(2.38)

\displaystyle Y(r/W,\xi)\leq\displaystyle\sum_{-2A/W\leq{\boldsymbol{h}}\leq 2A/W}|Z_{1}(r/W)|,

where

Z_{1}(r/W)=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}\in\mathcal{B}^{(k-1)}}e((r/W)U_{4}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)})).

Note that the range of
{\boldsymbol{h}}
is contained in
[-2A/W,2A/W]
and the range of
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}
is contained in
[-A,A]^{N}.
Hence, if we change the order of sums over
{\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}
in
Y(r/W,\xi)
and if we further define the range of
{\boldsymbol{h}}
to be the empty set when
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}\notin[-A,A]^{N},
one sees from the triangle inequality again that

(2.39)

\displaystyle Y(r/W,\xi)\leq\displaystyle\sum_{-2A\leq{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}\leq 2A}|Z_{2}(\xi)|,

where

Z_{2}(\xi)=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{h}}\in B({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)})}e(W\xi U_{3}({\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)})),

in which
B({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}):=B({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)};{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)})
is an
N
-dimensional set depending on
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}
,
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)}
,
\ldots
,
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}.
We note from the definition of
\mathcal{B}^{(k-1)}
that
B({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)})
is a rectangular box (or the empty set) contained in
[-2A/W,2A/W]^{N}
.

Hence, on substituting
(\ref{5.6})
and
(\ref{5.7})
into
(\ref{5.5})
, we discern that

\displaystyle V(r/W,\xi)

\displaystyle\leq A^{(k-1)N/2}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{-2A/W\leq{\boldsymbol{h}}\leq 2A/W}|Z_{1}(r/W)|\biggr)\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{-2A\leq{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}\leq 2A}|Z_{2}(\xi)|\biggr)\biggr)^{1/2}.

By applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Weyl differencing argument again, we deduce that

(2.40)

\displaystyle V(r/W,\xi)

\displaystyle\leq A^{(k-1)N/2}G_{1}(r/W)^{1/4}G_{2}(\xi)^{1/4},

where

G_{1}(r/W)=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{-2A/W\leq{\boldsymbol{h}}\leq 2A/W}|Z_{1}(r/W)|\biggr)^{2}

and

G_{2}(\xi)=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{-2A\leq{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}\leq 2A}|Z_{2}(\xi)|\biggr)^{2}.

On substituting
(\ref{3.29})
into
(\ref{3.24})
and that into
(\ref{5.4})
, one has

S(r/W,\xi)\ll(A/W)^{N}A^{N}A^{-(k+3)2^{-k}N}W^{2^{1-k}N}G_{1}(r/W)^{2^{-1-k}}G_{2}(\xi)^{2^{-1-k}}.

Then, on substituting this bound for
S(r/W,\xi)
into
(\ref{3.23})
, we deduce that

\Xi(\xi)\ll W^{-1}A^{2N}A^{-(k+3)2^{-k}N}W^{2^{1-k}N}G_{2}(\xi)^{2^{-1-k}}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r\leq W}G_{1}(r/W)^{2^{-1-k}}.

Thus, it follows from (
2.32
) and (
2.33
) that

(2.41)

\mathbf{N}(A)\ll C(A,W)\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}G_{2}(\alpha)^{2^{-2-k}}d\alpha\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}G_{2}(\beta)^{2^{-2-k}}d\beta,

where

C(A,W)=W^{-N-1+2^{1-k}N}A^{2N-(k+3)2^{-k}N}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r\leq W}G_{1}(r/W)^{2^{-1-k}}.

As the endgame of the proof of Theorem
2.8
, we first analyse the mean value

(2.42)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}G_{2}(\alpha)^{2^{-2-k}}d\alpha,

in
(\ref{3.35})
. In order to apply Lemma
2.10
, we temporarily pause to bound
G_{2}(\alpha)
by a different type of exponential sum. On recalling the definition (
2.1
) of
P({\boldsymbol{a}})
, we define

\Psi({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k)})=k!\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\leq N}p_{j_{1}\cdots j_{k}}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)})_{j_{1}}\cdots({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k)})_{j_{k}}.

Then, on recalling the definition of
G_{2}(\alpha)
and noting that
P
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
k,
we see that

G_{2}(\alpha)=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{-2A\leq{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}\leq 2A}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{h}}\in B({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)})}e(W\alpha\Psi^{(1)}({\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}))\biggr|\biggr)^{2},

where
\Psi^{(1)}({\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)})=\Psi({\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}).

Then, we see from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

G_{2}(\alpha)\ll A^{N}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(2)}\in B({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)})}e(W\alpha\Psi^{(1)}({\boldsymbol{h}}^{(1)}-{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)})).

By changing the order of summations, we find that

G_{2}(\alpha)\ll A^{N}\displaystyle\sum_{-2A/W\leq{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(2)}\leq 2A/W}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-2)}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}\in B}e(W\alpha\Psi^{(1)}({\boldsymbol{h}}^{(1)}-{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)})),

where
B
is an
N
-dimensional set depending on
{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-2)}
. We note from the definition of
B({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)})
that
B
is a rectangular box of dimension
N
.
By change of variables
{\boldsymbol{y}}={\boldsymbol{h}}^{(1)}-{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(2)}
and by applying the triangle inequality, we deduce that

\displaystyle G_{2}(\alpha)\ll A^{N}\displaystyle\sum_{-4A/W\leq{\boldsymbol{y}}\leq 4A/W}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(2)}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-2)}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}\in B_{1}}e(W\alpha\Psi^{(1)}({\boldsymbol{y}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}))\biggr|,

where
B_{1}:=B_{1}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)},{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{y}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-2)})\subseteq[-2A,2A]^{N}
is a rectangular box (or the empty set).

For fixed
{\boldsymbol{y}},{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-2)},
we observe that

\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}\in B_{1}}e(W\alpha\Psi^{(1)}({\boldsymbol{y}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}))\biggr|=\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}\in B_{1}}e(W\alpha\Delta_{k-1}(P({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)})))\biggr|,

where
\Delta_{k-1}(P({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)})):=\Delta_{k-1}(P({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)});{\boldsymbol{y}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-2)}).

Hence, by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we find that

(2.43)

G_{2}(\alpha)\ll A^{N}((A/W)^{2N}A^{(k-1)N})^{1/2}G_{3}(\alpha)^{1/2},

where

G_{3}(\alpha)=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)},{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(2)}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-2)}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}\in B_{1}}e(W\alpha\Delta_{k-1}(P({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)})))\biggr|^{2}.

Meanwhile, we note that variables
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}
and
{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(2)}
in
G_{3}(\alpha)
are not associated with the argument of the exponential sum in the innermost sum, and are only involved in
B_{1}
. Consider an arbitrary injective function
\mathcal{F}
mapping from

({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)},{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(2)})\in\left([-2A,2A]^{N}\times[-2A/W,2A/W]^{N}\right)\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{2N}

to

b\in[-(20A)^{2N}W^{-N},(20A)^{2N}W^{-N}]\cap{\mathbb{Z}}.

Furthermore, we define a set
D_{1}:=D_{1}(b,{\boldsymbol{y}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-2)})\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
in the following way. If
b
is not in the image of
\mathcal{F}
, we define
D_{1}
to be the empty set, and if
b
is in the image of
\mathcal{F}
, we define

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle:=D_{1}(b,{\boldsymbol{y}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-2)})

\displaystyle=D_{1}(\mathcal{F}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)},{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(2)}),{\boldsymbol{y}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-2)})

\displaystyle=B_{1}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)},{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{y}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-2)})\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}.

Then, one may write

(2.44)

G_{3}(\alpha)=\displaystyle\sum_{|b|\leq(20A)^{2N}W^{-N}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-2)}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}\in D_{1}}e(W\alpha\Delta_{k-1}(P({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)})))\biggr|^{2}.

Hence, on substituting
(\ref{3.202020})
into
(\ref{3.37})
and that into
(\ref{3.36})
, it follows by applying periodicity that

(2.45)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}G_{2}(\alpha)^{2^{-2-k}}d\alpha

\displaystyle\ll A^{(k+3)2^{-3-k}N}W^{-2^{-2-k}N}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}G_{3}(\alpha)^{2^{-3-k}}d\alpha

\displaystyle=A^{(k+3)2^{-3-k}N}W^{-2^{-2-k}N}W^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{W}G_{3}(W^{-1}\alpha)^{2^{-3-k}}d\alpha

\displaystyle=A^{(k+3)2^{-3-k}N}W^{-2^{-2-k}N}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}G_{3}(W^{-1}\alpha)^{2^{-3-k}}d\alpha.

Since
D_{1}
is a rectangular box depending on
b,{\boldsymbol{y}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-2)}
, by applying Lemma
2.10
to

\int_{0}^{1}G_{3}(W^{-1}\alpha)^{2^{-3-k}}d\alpha,

with

l=k-1,B=(20A)^{2N}W^{-N},A_{1}=4A/W,A_{2}=A_{3}=\cdots=A_{k}=2A,\sigma=2^{-3-k},

it follows that whenever
N\geq 18k(k-1)4^{k+3}
one has

(2.46)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}G_{3}(W^{-1}\alpha)^{2^{-3-k}}d\alpha\ll(A^{k+3}/W^{2})^{2^{-3-k}N}WA^{-k}.

By substituting
(\ref{3.39})
into
(\ref{3.38})
, one has

(2.47)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}G_{2}(\alpha)^{2^{-2-k}}d\alpha\ll A^{(k+3)2^{-2-k}N}W^{-2^{-1-k}N}WA^{-k}.

The same argument leading from (
2.42
) to (
2.47
) yields that

(2.48)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}G_{2}(\beta)^{2^{-2-k}}d\beta\ll A^{(k+3)2^{-2-k}N}W^{-2^{-1-k}N}WA^{-k}.

Next, we turn to estimate
C(A,W)
in
(\ref{3.35}).
Recall the definition of
C(A,W),
that is

(2.49)

C(A,W)=W^{-N-1+2^{1-k}N}A^{2N-(k+3)2^{-k}N}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r\leq W}G_{1}(r/W)^{2^{-1-k}}.

By splitting the summation over
r
into the values of
d=(r,W),
we see that

(2.50)

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r\leq W}G_{1}(r/W)^{2^{-1-k}}=\displaystyle\sum_{d|W}\displaystyle\sum_{(r,W)=W/d}G_{1}(r/W)^{2^{-1-k}}.

Meanwhile, on recalling the definition of
G_{1}(r/W)
, it follows by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

G_{1}(r/W)\ll(A/W)^{N}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(k-1)}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(2)}\in\mathcal{B}^{(k-1)}}e((r/W)U_{4}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)}-{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)})).

Furthermore, if we use the definition (
2.12
) of
\Gamma_{P}
, we find by change of variables
{\boldsymbol{z}}={\boldsymbol{z}}^{(1)}-{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(2)}
that

G_{1}(r/W)\ll(A/W)^{2N}A^{N}\Gamma_{P}(r/W,A,\ldots,A).

Additionally, let us use the definition (
2.16
) of
N(A_{1},\ldots,A_{k-1},A_{k}^{-1};\beta).
Then, whenever
(r,W)=W/d
, one infers from the argument in [
References
, Lemma 3.2] that

\Gamma_{P}(r/W,A,\ldots,A)\ll A^{N}(\log d)^{N}N(A,\ldots,A,A^{-1};r/W).

Notice here that naive application of [
References
, Lemma 3.2] gives
\log A
in place of
\log d
on the right-hand side, however, one readily sees from the fact that
r/W
is a rational number with
(r,W)=W/d
that the factor
\log A
could be replaced by
\log d
.
Hence, since
P
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial, the arguments in [
References
, Lemma 3.3, Lemma 3.4] using (
2.15
) and
(\ref{3.63.63.6})
with
A_{1}=\cdots=A_{k}=A
and
\beta=r/W
delivers that

(2.51)

G_{1}(r/W)\ll(A/W)^{2N}A^{(k+1)N}(\log d)^{N+1}d^{-N/(k-1)}.

On substituting
(\ref{3.43})
into
(\ref{3.42})
, whenever
N\geq k2^{k+2},
we deduce that

(2.52)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r\leq W}G_{1}(r/W)^{2^{-1-k}}

\displaystyle\ll\displaystyle\sum_{d|W}\displaystyle\sum_{(r,W)=W/d}(A/W)^{2^{-k}N}A^{(k+1)2^{-1-k}N}(\log d)^{2^{-1-k}(N+1)}d^{-2^{-1-k}N/(k-1)}

\displaystyle\ll(A/W)^{2^{-k}N}A^{(k+1)2^{-1-k}N}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq d}d^{-2}\ll(A/W)^{2^{-k}N}A^{(k+1)2^{-1-k}N}

Then, substituting (
2.52
) into (
2.49
), one has

(2.53)

C(A,W)\ll W^{-N-1+2^{-k}N}A^{2N-(k+3)2^{-1-k}N}.

Therefore, by substituting (
2.47
), (
2.48
) and (
2.53
) into (
2.41
), we conclude that

(2.54)

\mathbf{N}(A)\ll A^{2N-2k}W^{1-N}.

Next, we shall derive the lower bound for
\mathbf{N}(A).
By making the trivial observation that whenever
P({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
, one has
P({\boldsymbol{x}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W,
it follows by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

(2.55)

\displaystyle\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in[-A,A]^{N}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\middle|\ P({\boldsymbol{x}})=0\right\}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq W\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W\end{subarray}}\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in[-A,A]^{N}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\middle|\ P({\boldsymbol{x}})=0,\ {\boldsymbol{x}}\equiv{\boldsymbol{b}}\ \text{mod}\ W\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq W\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W\end{subarray}}1\biggr)^{1/2}\cdot\mathcal{S}^{1/2},

where

\mathcal{S}=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq W\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W\end{subarray}}\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in[-A,A]^{N}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\middle|\ P({\boldsymbol{x}})=0,\ {\boldsymbol{x}}\equiv{\boldsymbol{b}}\ \text{mod}\ W\right\}^{2}.

On noting that

\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbf{N}(A)
and by applying Lemma
2.9
, it follows from
(\ref{3.313.31})
that

(2.56)

\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in[-A,A]^{N}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\middle|\ P({\boldsymbol{x}})=0\right\}\ll(W^{N-1})^{1/2}\cdot\mathbf{N}(A)^{1/2}.

Meanwhile, since
P({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
has a nontrivial integer solution and
P
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial, we find by [
References
] that

\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in[-A,A]^{N}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\middle|\ P({\boldsymbol{x}})=0\right\}\asymp A^{N-k},

and thus it follows by
(\ref{3.323.32})
that

(2.57)

A^{2N-2k}W^{1-N}\ll\mathbf{N}(A).

Combining
(\ref{3.313.313.31})
and
(\ref{3.34})
, we complete the proof of Theorem
\ref{thm3.1}.

∎

3.
Minor arcs

Our goal in this section is to prove Proposition
3.1
below. In order to describe this proposition, we provide some definitions.
For any measurable set
\mathfrak{B}\subseteq[0,1)
,
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
and
X>1
, define

(3.1)

\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{B})=\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{B}}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{x}}\leq X}e(\alpha f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))d\alpha.

For
B>0,
define the major arcs

(3.2)

\mathfrak{M}(B)=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq a\leq q\leq B\\
(q,a)=1\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{M}(q,a),

where

\mathfrak{M}(q,a)=\{\alpha\in[0,1)|\ |\alpha-a/q|\leq BA^{-1}X^{-d}\},

and define the minor arcs
\mathfrak{m}(B)=[0,1)\setminus\mathfrak{M}(B).
Recall the definition (
1.5
) of
w
. Here and throughout, we abbreviate
\mathfrak{M}(w)
and
\mathfrak{m}(w)
simply to
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{m}.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
n
and
d
be natural numbers with
d\geq 2.
Let
n_{1}
be the greatest integer with
n_{1}\leq\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor/8.
Suppose that
n_{1}>2d
and
2X^{d}\leq A\leq X^{n_{1}-d}.
Suppose that
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N_{d,n}
variables of degree
k\geq 2.
Then, whenever
N\geq 200k(k-1)2^{k-1},
one has

(3.3)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{m})\right|^{2}\ll A^{N-k-2}X^{2n-2d}(\log A)^{-1}.

We shall prove Proposition
3.1
at the end of this section. We temporarily pause here and provide the key idea in dealing with the constraint
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0
in the summation over
{\boldsymbol{a}}
in
(\ref{in4.1})
. By orthogonality, we can represent

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{m})\right|^{2}

by mean values of an associated exponential sum which is the shape of

(3.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}\displaystyle\sum_{\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A}e(\beta P({\boldsymbol{a}}))e(F({\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{x}})),

where
F({\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{x}})
is a polynomial in
{\boldsymbol{x}},
and a linear function in
{\boldsymbol{a}}
. On noting that
F({\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{x}})
is linear in
{\boldsymbol{a}}
, by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Weyl differencing argument, we obtain an upper bound for
(\ref{4.44.4})
in terms of

(3.5)

\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{h}}}e(\beta(P({\boldsymbol{a}}+{\boldsymbol{h}})-P({\boldsymbol{a}})))e(F({\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}})).

By changing the order of summations and applying the triangle inequality, the expression
(\ref{4.54.5})
is bounded above by

(3.6)

\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}e(\beta(P({\boldsymbol{a}}+{\boldsymbol{h}})-P({\boldsymbol{a}})))\biggr|\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}e(F({\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}))\biggr|.

Then, by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality again, the expression
(\ref{4.64.6})
is bounded above by

(3.7)

\left(\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}e(\beta(P({\boldsymbol{a}}+{\boldsymbol{h}})-P({\boldsymbol{a}})))\biggr|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}e(F({\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{x}}))\biggr|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Now, one sees that the first part in the expression
(\ref{4.74.7})
includes all the information about
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0
, and this allows us to deal with the constraint
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0
separately. We emphasize here that even if we replace the constraint
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0
by a more general constraint
P_{1}({\boldsymbol{a}})=P_{2}({\boldsymbol{a}})=\cdots=P_{m}({\boldsymbol{a}})=0
with
P_{i}({\boldsymbol{a}})\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{a}}]
for
