1\leq i\leq m,
we could follow the same procedure leading from
(\ref{4.44.4})
to
(\ref{4.74.7})
and deal with the constraint separately. Thus, we call this procedure ‘the separation procedure’.

At this point, recall the Veronese embedding and that we write
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{x}})\rangle
for a homogeneous polynomial in
n
variables of degree
d
with coefficients
{\boldsymbol{a}}
. We introduce some definitions useful in sections
3
and
4
.

Definition 3.2
.

Define
\boldsymbol{b}:=\boldsymbol{b}(f_{\boldsymbol{a}})=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{c}:=\boldsymbol{c}(f_{\boldsymbol{a}})=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{N-n})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N-n}
to be vectors associated with the coefficients
{\boldsymbol{a}}
of
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})
such that

b_{k}
is the coefficient of
x_{k}^{d}
with
k=1,\ldots,n
, and the coefficients
c_{k}
are the coefficients of the remaining monomials in lexicographical order with
k=1,\ldots,N-n.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
n
and
d
be natural numbers with
d\geq 2.
Consider the monomials of degree
d
in
n
variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
. In particular, the number of these monomials is
N=\binom{n+d-1}{d}.
Then, define
v_{d}({\boldsymbol{x}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
w_{d}({\boldsymbol{x}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N-n}
to be vectors associated with those monomials such that
(v_{d}({\boldsymbol{x}}))_{i}
is
x_{i}^{d}
with
i=1,\ldots,n
and the polynomials
(w_{d}({\boldsymbol{x}}))_{j}
are the remaining monomials in lexicographical order with
j=1,\ldots,N-n
, respectively.

For example, for
f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=a_{1}x_{1}^{3}+a_{2}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}+a_{3}x_{1}x_{2}^{2}+a_{4}x_{2}^{3},
we have
{\boldsymbol{b}}=(a_{1},a_{4})
and
{\boldsymbol{c}}=(a_{2},a_{3}).
Furthermore, we find that

v_{3}(x_{1},x_{2})=(x_{1}^{3},x_{2}^{3})\quad\ \text{and}\quad\ w_{3}(x_{1},x_{2})=(x_{1}^{2}x_{2},x_{1}x_{2}^{2}).

Then, we notice that

f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\langle{\boldsymbol{b}},v_{d}({\boldsymbol{x}})\rangle+\langle{\boldsymbol{c}},w_{d}({\boldsymbol{x}})\rangle.

3.1.
The separation procedure

Our goal in this subsection is to derive Proposition
3.4
below. This is the culmination of our work in this subsection. In order to describe Proposition
3.4
, we recall the definition
(\ref{4.1})
of
\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{B}),
and define

(3.8)

T(\alpha):=\displaystyle\sum_{-A\leq b\leq A}\bigl|\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq x\leq X}e(\alpha bx^{d})\bigr|^{2},

with
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
n
and
d
be natural numbers. Suppose that
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N_{d,n}
variables of degree
k\geq 2
. For any measurable set
\mathfrak{B}\subseteq[0,1)
, whenever
N\geq 200k(k-1)2^{k-1},
we have

(3.9)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\bigl|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{B})\bigr|^{2}\ll A^{N-n/8-k}X^{7n/4}\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{B}}T(\alpha_{1})^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor/8}d\alpha_{1}\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{B}}T(\alpha_{2})^{\lceil n/2\rceil/8}d\alpha_{2}.

We shall prove Proposition
3.4
at the end of this subsection, by combining Lemma
3.5
and Lemma
3.6
. To be specific, in Lemma
\ref{lem4.4},
we bound

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{B})\right|^{2}

by mean values of two exponential sums, via applications of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in a way we mentioned in the explanation following Proposition
\ref{prop4.1}.
From Lemma
3.6
, we have an upper bound for one of these exponential sums, in terms of mean values of the exponential sum
T(\alpha)
. Furthermore, by Lemma
\ref{lem3.2},
we have an upper bound for the other exponential sum. We will combine these estimates to prove Proposition
\ref{pro4.3}.

In order to describe Lemma
3.5
, it is convenient to introduce some definitions. For
\boldsymbol{h}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
, we define

I_{\boldsymbol{h}}=\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}|\ \|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A,\|{\boldsymbol{a}}+\boldsymbol{h}\|_{\infty}\leq A\}.

Furthermore, we define

F_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\boldsymbol{h})=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}\leq X\\
{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\end{subarray}}e(\alpha_{1}\langle\boldsymbol{h},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{x}})\rangle-\alpha_{2}\langle\boldsymbol{h},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{y}})\rangle)

and

F_{2}(\beta,\boldsymbol{h})=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{a}}\in I_{\boldsymbol{h}}\end{subarray}}e(\beta(P({\boldsymbol{a}}+\boldsymbol{h})-P({\boldsymbol{a}}))).

Additionally, we define

G_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|\boldsymbol{h}\|_{\infty}\leq 2A\\
\boldsymbol{h}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\end{subarray}}|F_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\boldsymbol{h})|^{2}\quad\ \text{and}\quad\ G_{2}(\beta)=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|\boldsymbol{h}\|_{\infty}\leq 2A\\
\boldsymbol{h}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\end{subarray}}|F_{2}(\beta,\boldsymbol{h})|^{2}.

Lemma 3.5
.

We have

(3.10)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{B})\right|^{2}\ll X^{n}\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{B}^{2}}G_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})^{1/4}d\alpha_{1}d\alpha_{2}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}G_{2}(\beta)^{1/4}d\beta.

Proof.

Recall the definition of
F_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},{\boldsymbol{a}})
. Then, by orthogonality, we have

(3.11)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{B})\right|^{2}=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{B}^{2}}F_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},{\boldsymbol{a}})e(\beta P({\boldsymbol{a}}))d\alpha_{1}d\alpha_{2}d\beta

By changing the order of summations together with the triangle inequality, one finds that

(3.12)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{B})\right|^{2}\leq\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{B}^{2}}F(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta)d\alpha_{1}d\alpha_{2}d\beta,

where

F(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta)=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}\leq X\\
{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\end{subarray}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\end{subarray}}e(\beta P({\boldsymbol{a}})+\alpha_{1}\langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{x}})\rangle-\alpha_{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{y}})\rangle)\biggr|.

We emphasize again that we preserve summation conditions until different conditions are specified.

By applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and a conventional Weyl differencing argument, one sees that

F(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta)\leq(X^{2n})^{1/2}(\Xi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta))^{1/2},

where

\displaystyle\Xi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|\boldsymbol{h}\|_{\infty}\leq 2A\\
\boldsymbol{h}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in I_{\boldsymbol{h}}}e(\beta(P({\boldsymbol{a}}+\boldsymbol{h})-P({\boldsymbol{a}}))+\alpha_{1}\langle\boldsymbol{h},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{x}})\rangle-\alpha_{2}\langle\boldsymbol{h},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{y}})\rangle\rangle).

By applying the triangle inequality, we have

F(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta)\leq X^{n}\bigl(\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{h}}|F_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\boldsymbol{h})||F_{2}(\beta,\boldsymbol{h})|\bigr)^{1/2},

where

F_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\boldsymbol{h})=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}}e(\alpha_{1}\langle\boldsymbol{h},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{x}})\rangle-\alpha_{2}\langle\boldsymbol{h},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{y}})\rangle)

and

F_{2}(\beta,\boldsymbol{h})=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in I_{\boldsymbol{h}}}e(\beta(P({\boldsymbol{a}}+\boldsymbol{h})-P({\boldsymbol{a}})))

By applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality again and recalling the definition of
G_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
and
G_{2}(\beta)
, we see that

(3.13)

F(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta)\leq X^{n}G_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})^{1/4}G_{2}(\beta)^{1/4}.

Therefore, on substituting
(\ref{4.3})
into
(\ref{4.77}),
we complete the proof of Lemma
3.5
.
∎

Next, Lemma
3.6
provides the upper bound for
G_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
.

Lemma 3.6
.

We have

G_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\ll A^{N-n/2}X^{3n}T(\alpha_{1})^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor/2}T(\alpha_{2})^{\lceil n/2\rceil/2}.

Proof.

Recall the definition
{\boldsymbol{b}}:={\boldsymbol{b}}(f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}})
of Definition
3.2
. By squaring out and applying the triangle inequality, we find that

\displaystyle G_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\ll A^{N-n}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}\leq X\\
1\leq{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(2)}\leq X\end{subarray}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{b}}\|_{\infty}\leq 2A\\
{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\end{subarray}}e(\Psi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},{\boldsymbol{b}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(2)}))\biggr|,

where

\Psi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},{\boldsymbol{b}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(2)})=\alpha_{1}\langle{\boldsymbol{b}},v_{d}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)})-v_{d}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)})\rangle-\alpha_{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{b}},v_{d}({\boldsymbol{y}}^{(1)})-v_{d}({\boldsymbol{y}}^{(2)})\rangle.

By applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the triangle inequality, one sees that

(3.14)

G_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\ll A^{N-n}(X^{4n})^{1/2}\bigl(A^{n}H(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\bigr)^{1/2},

where

H(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}\\
{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(2)}\end{subarray}}e(\Psi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},{\boldsymbol{b}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(2)}))\biggr|.

We note that

\displaystyle H(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{-A\leq b\leq A}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq x\leq X}e(\alpha_{1}bx^{d})\biggr|^{2}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq y\leq X}e(\alpha_{2}by^{d})\biggr|^{2}\biggr)^{n}.

Then, on recalling the definition
(\ref{4.4})
of
T(\alpha)
and applying the trivial bounds that

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq x\leq X}e(\alpha_{1}bx^{d})\leq X\ \textrm{and}\ \displaystyle\sum_{1\leq y\leq X}e(\alpha_{2}by^{d})\leq X,

we see that

(3.15)

H(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\leq X^{2n}T(\alpha_{1})^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}T(\alpha_{2})^{\lceil n/2\rceil}.

On substituting (
3.15
) into
(\ref{ineq4.6})
, we complete the proof of Lemma
\ref{lem4.5}.

∎

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

Recall the definition of
G_{2}(\beta)
and
I_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}
leading to Lemma
3.5
. Since
I_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}
is a rectangular box depending on
{\boldsymbol{h}}
, it follows by Lemma
2.10
with
l=1
,
B=0,

A_{1}=A_{2}=2A
and
\sigma=1/4
that whenever
N\geq 200k(k-1)2^{k-1},
one has

\int_{0}^{1}G_{2}(\beta)^{1/4}d\beta\ll A^{3N/4-k}.

Then, on substituting this estimate and the bound for
G_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
obtained in Lemma
3.6
into (
3.10
), we readily conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\bigl|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{B})\bigr|^{2}\ll A^{N-n/8-k}X^{7n/4}\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{B}}T(\alpha_{1})^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor/8}d\alpha_{1}\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{B}}T(\alpha_{2})^{\lceil n/2\rceil/8}d\alpha_{2}.

Thus, we complete the proof of Proposition
3.4
.
∎

3.2.
Bounds for large moduli

In this subsection, for given
\delta>0
, we derive the bound for

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{m}(X^{\delta}))\right|^{2}.

To derive a bound for this quantity, we make use of Proposition
3.4
and Lemma
\ref{lem3.23.2}.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\delta
be a positive number with
\delta<1/6
. Let
n
and
d
be natural numbers with
d\geq 2.
Let
n_{1}
be the greatest integer with
n_{1}\leq\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor/8.
Suppose that
n_{1}>2d
and
2X^{d}\leq A\leq X^{n_{1}-d}.
Suppose that
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N_{d,n}
variables of degree
k\geq 2.
Then, whenever
N\geq 200k(k-1)2^{k-1},
there exists
\delta^{\prime}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{m}(X^{\delta}))\right|^{2}\ll A^{N-k-2}X^{2n-2d}A^{-\delta^{\prime}}.

In particular, the exponent
\delta^{\prime}
depends on
\delta,n
and
d.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.4
, we obtain the bound

(3.16)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\bigl|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{m}(X^{\delta}))\bigr|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll A^{N-n/8-k}X^{7n/4}\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{m}(X^{\delta})}T(\alpha_{1})^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor/8}d\alpha_{1}\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{m}(X^{\delta})}T(\alpha_{2})^{\lceil n/2\rceil/8}d\alpha_{2}

For simplicity, we write

\displaystyle m_{1}=\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor/8\ \text{and}\ m_{2}=\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil/8,

and write
n_{i}
for the greatest integer less than
m_{i}
for
i=1
and
2
, respectively. Then, on noting that
n/8=m_{1}+m_{2}+1/8
,
one finds from (
3.16
) that

(3.17)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\bigl|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{m}(X^{\delta}))\bigr|^{2}\ll A^{N-n/8-k}X^{7n/8}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{1}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{2}\cdot\sup_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{m}(X^{\delta})}{T}(\alpha)^{\sigma},

where

\displaystyle\sigma=m_{1}+m_{2}-n_{1}-n_{2}+1/8

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}{T}(\alpha_{1})^{n_{1}}d\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{2}=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}{T}(\alpha_{2})^{n_{2}}d\alpha_{2}

Recall the hypothesis
2X^{d}\leq A\leq X^{n_{1}-d}
in the statement of Lemma
3.7
. Then, we find from orthogonality and
(\ref{3.5})
that

(3.18)

\mathcal{L}_{1}\ll A^{n_{1}-1}X^{2n_{1}-d+\epsilon}.

Similarly, one finds that

(3.19)

\mathcal{L}_{2}\ll A^{n_{2}-1}X^{2n_{2}-d+\epsilon}.

Furthermore, it follows by (
2.4
) that

(3.20)

\sup_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{m}(X^{\delta})}{T}(\alpha)^{\sigma}\ll(AX^{2})^{\sigma+\epsilon}X^{-2^{2-k}\sigma\delta}.

On substuting
(\ref{4.13})
,
(\ref{4.14})
and
(\ref{4.15})
into
(\ref{4.12})
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\bigl|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{m}(X^{\delta}))\bigr|^{2}\ll A^{N-k-2}X^{2n-2d}X^{-\delta^{\prime\prime}},

where
\delta^{\prime\prime}
is a positive number with
\delta^{\prime\prime}<2^{2-k}\sigma\delta.
Hence, on recalling that
A\leq X^{n_{1}-d}
, we complete the proof of Lemma
\ref{lem4.6}.

∎

3.3.
Bounds for small moduli

In this section, we derive the bound for

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{M}(X^{\delta})\setminus\mathfrak{M}(\log X))\right|^{2}.

To derive a bound for this quantity, we use the standard treatments of exponential sums in major arcs and pruning arguments.

Lemma 3.8
.

With the same notation in Lemma
3.7
, suppose that
n_{1}>2d
,
2X^{d}\leq A\leq X^{n_{1}-d}
and
0<\delta<1/6
. Suppose that
n_{1}>2d
and
2X^{d}\leq A\leq X^{n_{1}-d}.
Suppose that
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N_{d,n}
variables of degree
k\geq 2.
Then, whenever
N\geq 200k(k-1)2^{k-1},
one has

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{M}(X^{\delta})\setminus\mathfrak{M}(\log X))\right|^{2}\ll A^{N-k-2}X^{2n-2d}(\log A)^{-1}.

Proof.

On observing that

\mathfrak{M}(X^{\delta})\setminus\mathfrak{M}(\log X)=\bigcup_{j=0}^{J}\mathfrak{M}(2^{j+1}\log X)\setminus\mathfrak{M}(2^{j}\log X),

with
J=O(\log X),
we deduce by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

(3.21)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{M}(X^{\delta})\setminus\mathfrak{M}(\log X))\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll J\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{J}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{M}(2^{j+1}\log X)\setminus\mathfrak{M}(2^{j}\log X))\right|^{2}

Now, we analyze the mean value

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{M}(2Q)\setminus\mathfrak{M}(Q))\right|^{2},

with
\log X\leq Q\leq X^{\delta}.
For simplicity, we temporarily write

(3.22)

\mathfrak{C}=\mathfrak{M}(2Q)\setminus\mathfrak{M}(Q).

Then, by Proposition
3.4
, we deduce that

(3.23)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{C})\right|^{2}\ll A^{N-n/8-k}X^{7n/4}\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{C}}T(\alpha_{1})^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor/8}d\alpha_{1}\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{C}}T(\alpha_{2})^{\lceil n/2\rceil/8}d\alpha_{2}.

Meanwhile, whenever
\alpha\in\mathfrak{C},
there exist
q\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
(q,a)=1
such that
Q\leq q\leq 2Q
and

|\alpha-a/q|\leq 2Q(AX^{d})^{-1}.

Also, for
b\in{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}
with
|b|\leq A
, if we write
l=(q,b)
,
\widetilde{q}=q/l
and
\widetilde{b}=b/l
, we have

\left|b\alpha-\frac{a\widetilde{b}}{\widetilde{q}}\right|\leq\frac{2Qb}{AX^{d}}\leq\frac{2Q}{X^{d}}.

Hence, since
\widetilde{q}\leq 2Q
, it follows by [
References
, Lemma 2.7] that

(3.24)

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq x\leq X}e(b\alpha x^{d})-(\widetilde{q})^{-1}S(\widetilde{q},a\widetilde{b})v(\beta)=O((2Q)^{2}),

where
S(q,a)=\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{q}e(an^{d}/q)
and
v(\beta)=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{X}e(\beta\gamma^{d})d\gamma
with
\beta=b\alpha-\frac{a\widetilde{b}}{\widetilde{q}}.
Thus, when
\alpha\in\mathfrak{C}
, we deduce from (
3.24
) that

(3.25)

\displaystyle T(\alpha)

\displaystyle\ll X^{2}+\displaystyle\sum_{l|q}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|b|\leq A\\
(q,b)=l\end{subarray}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq x\leq X}e(b\alpha x^{d})\biggr|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll X^{2}+\displaystyle\sum_{l|q}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|b|\leq A\\
(q,b)=l\end{subarray}}\left(|((\widetilde{q})^{-1}S(\widetilde{q},a\widetilde{b})v(\beta)|^{2}+(2Q)^{4}\right).

By [
References
, Theorem 4.2], we have a bound
S(q/l,a(b/l))\ll(q/l)^{1-1/d}

and a trivial bound
v(\beta)\leq X
. Hence, on substituting these estimates into
(\ref{4.20})
, we find that

(3.26)

T(\alpha)\ll X^{2}+\displaystyle\sum_{l|q}AX^{2}q^{-2/d}l^{2/d-1}+AQ^{4}.

The second term
\sum_{l|q}AX^{2}q^{-2/d}l^{2/d-1}
is bounded above by
AX^{2}q^{-2/d}\sum_{l|q}1,
and by the standard divisor estimate, this bound is
O(AX^{2}q^{-2/d+\epsilon})
. Recall that
\alpha\in\mathfrak{C}
, and thus we have the bound
Q\leq q\leq 2Q
with
\log X\leq Q\leq X^{\delta}.
Furthermore, we recall the hypothesis
X^{d}\leq A
in the statement in Lemma
\ref{lem4.7}.
Hence, it follows from
(\ref{4.21})
that

(3.27)

T(\alpha)\ll AX^{2}Q^{-2/d+\epsilon}.

Therefore, on substituting (
3.27
) into (
3.23
), we obtain the bound

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{C})\right|^{2}\ll A^{N-n/8-k}X^{7n/4}\textrm{mes}(\mathfrak{C})^{2}(AX^{2}Q^{-2/d+\epsilon})^{n/8}.

On noting that
\textrm{mes}(\mathfrak{C})\ll Q^{3}(AX^{d})^{-1}
, we conclude that

(3.28)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{C})\right|^{2}\ll A^{N-k-2}X^{2n-2d}Q^{6-n/(4d)+\epsilon}.

Recall that
n_{1}
is the greatest integer with
n_{1}\leq\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor/8.
We find from (
3.28
) together with the hypothesis
n_{1}>2d
in the statement of Lemma
3.8
that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{C})\right|^{2}\ll A^{N-k-2}X^{2n-2d}Q^{-2}.

Hence, on recalling that
J=O(\log X)
, it follows from (
3.21
) that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{M}(X^{\delta})\setminus\mathfrak{M}(\log X))\right|^{2}\ll A^{N-k-2}X^{2n-2d}(\log X)^{-1}.

On noting that
A\leq X^{n_{1}-d}
in the statement of Lemma
3.8
, we complete the proof of Lemma
\ref{lem4.7}.

∎

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Note that

\mathfrak{m}(\log X)=(\mathfrak{M}(X^{\delta})\setminus\mathfrak{M}(\log X))\cup\mathfrak{m}(X^{\delta}).

Then, by the elementary inequality
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2},
we find that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{m}(\log X))\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{M}(X^{\delta})\setminus\mathfrak{M}(\log X))\right|^{2}+\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{m}(X^{\delta}))\right|^{2}.

Thus, by Lemma
3.7
and Lemma
3.8
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{m}(\log X))\right|^{2}\ll A^{N-k-2}X^{2n-2d}(\log A)^{-1}.

Therefore, we complete the proof of Proposition
\ref{prop4.1}.

∎

4.
Proof of Theorem 1.1

In this section, we provide the proof of Theorem
1.1
. To do this, we require two auxiliary lemmas. These lemmas reveal that
\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}
and
\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}
, defined in the introduction, behave in a similar manner to the truncated singular series and the truncated singular integral on average over
{\boldsymbol{a}}
with
\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A
and
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0
. It is worth noting that in the proofs of these lemmas, we make use of the same idea as that in section
5.1
(the separation procedure). In advance of the statement of the first lemma of these, it is convenient to define the exponential sum
S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(q)
with
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
,
q\in{\mathbb{N}}
by

S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(q):=S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(q;n)=q^{-n}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq b\leq q\\
(q,b)=1\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{r}}\leq q\\
{\boldsymbol{r}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\end{subarray}}e\left(\frac{b}{q}f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{r}})\right).

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
n
and
d
be natural numbers. Suppose that
A,B,C
are sufficiently large positive numbers with
B<C.
Suppose that
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N_{d,n}
variables of degree
k\geq 2.
Then, for any set
\mathcal{C}\subseteq[B,C]\cap{\mathbb{Z}}
, whenever
N\geq 200k(k-1)2^{k-1}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\mathcal{C}}S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(q)\biggr|^{2}\ll A^{N-k}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\mathcal{C}}q^{1+\epsilon}(q^{-1}+q^{-4/d})^{n/16}\biggr)^{2}.

Proof.

We deduce by applying orthogonality and changing the order of summations that

(4.1)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\mathcal{C}}S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(q)\biggr|^{2}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\sum_{q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathcal{C}}S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(q_{1})\overline{S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(q_{2})}e(\beta P({\boldsymbol{a}}))d\beta

\displaystyle=q_{1}^{-n}q_{2}^{-n}\displaystyle\sum_{q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathcal{C}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq b_{1}\leq q_{1}\\
1\leq b_{2}\leq q_{2}\\
(q_{1},b_{1})=(q_{2},b_{2})=1\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}\leq q_{1}\\
1\leq{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}\leq q_{2}\\
{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\end{subarray}}T_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\left(\beta,\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}\right)d\beta,

where

T_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\left(\beta,\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}\right)=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\end{subarray}}e(\beta P({\boldsymbol{a}}))e\left(\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}}f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{r}}_{1})-\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}}f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{r}}_{2})\right).

We now investigate the integrand in (
4.1
), that is

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}\leq q_{1}\\
1\leq{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}\leq q_{2}\end{subarray}}T_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\left(\beta,\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}\right).

In order to obtain the upper bound for this quantity, we
define

F_{1}\left(\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}},{\boldsymbol{h}}\right)=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}\leq q_{1}\\
1\leq{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}\leq q_{2}\end{subarray}}e\left(\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}}f_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}({\boldsymbol{r}}_{1})-\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}}f_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}({\boldsymbol{r}}_{2})\right)

and

F_{2}\bigl(\beta,{\boldsymbol{h}}\bigr)=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in I_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}}e(\beta(P({\boldsymbol{a}}+{\boldsymbol{h}})-P({\boldsymbol{a}})))

in which

I_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}=\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}|\ \|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A,\ \|{\boldsymbol{a}}+{\boldsymbol{h}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\}.

Then,
by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and a conventional Weyl differencing together with the triangle inequality, one deduces that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}\end{subarray}}T_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\left(\beta,\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}\right)\ll q_{1}^{n/2}q_{2}^{n/2}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{h}}\|_{\infty}\leq 2A\\
{\boldsymbol{h}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\end{subarray}}\biggl|F_{1}\biggl(\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}},{\boldsymbol{h}}\biggr)\biggr|\biggl|F_{2}\bigl(\beta,{\boldsymbol{h}}\bigr)\biggr|\biggr)^{1/2}.

If we further define

G_{1}\biggl(\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}}\biggr)=\displaystyle\sum_{\|{\boldsymbol{h}}\|_{\infty}\leq 2A}\biggl|F_{1}\biggl(\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}},{\boldsymbol{h}}\biggr)\biggr|^{2}\ \text{and}\ G_{2}(\beta)=\displaystyle\sum_{\|{\boldsymbol{h}}\|_{\infty}\leq 2A}\biggl|F_{2}\bigl(\beta,{\boldsymbol{h}}\bigr)\biggr|^{2},

we find by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality again that

(4.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}\end{subarray}}T_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\left(\beta,\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}\right)\ll q_{1}^{n/2}q_{2}^{n/2}\left(G_{1}\biggl(\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}}\biggr)\right)^{1/4}\left(G_{2}(\beta)\right)^{1/4}.

We first analyse
G_{1}\bigl(\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}}\bigr).
Recall Definition
3.3
. Furthermore, for
{\boldsymbol{d}},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(2)}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
, we define

(4.3)

\displaystyle\Psi({\boldsymbol{d}},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(2)})

\displaystyle:=\Psi\left({\boldsymbol{d}},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(2)};\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}},\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}}(\langle{\boldsymbol{d}},v_{d}({\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(1)})\rangle-\langle{\boldsymbol{d}},v_{d}({\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(2)})\rangle)-\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}}(\langle{\boldsymbol{d}},v_{d}({\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(1)})\rangle-\langle{\boldsymbol{d}},v_{d}({\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(2)})\rangle).

Then, by squaring out and applying the triangle inequality, we deduce that

(4.4)

\displaystyle G_{1}\biggl(\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}}\biggr)\ll A^{N-n}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(2)}\leq q_{1}\\
1\leq{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(2)}\leq q_{2}\end{subarray}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{d}}\|_{\infty}\leq 2A\\
{\boldsymbol{d}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\end{subarray}}e\left(\Psi({\boldsymbol{d}},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(2)})\right)\biggr|.

By applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality together with the triangle inequality and by changing the order of summations, one sees from (
4.4
) that

\displaystyle G_{1}\biggl(\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}}\biggr)\ll A^{N-n}\cdot q_{1}^{n}q_{2}^{n}\cdot\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{d}}_{1}\|_{\infty}\leq 2A\\
\|{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2}\|_{\infty}\leq 2A\end{subarray}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(2)}\leq q_{1}\\
1\leq{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(2)}\leq q_{2}\end{subarray}}e\left(\Psi({\boldsymbol{d}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(2)})\right)\biggr|\biggr)^{1/2}.

By change of variables
{\boldsymbol{d}}={\boldsymbol{d}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle G_{1}\biggl(\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}}\biggr)\ll A^{N-n}\cdot q_{1}^{n}q_{2}^{n}\cdot\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{d}}\|_{\infty}\leq 4A\end{subarray}}A^{n}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(2)}}e\left(\Psi({\boldsymbol{d}},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(1)},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{2}^{(2)})\right)\biggr|\biggr)^{1/2}.

On writing

H\left(\alpha,q\right)=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r\leq q}e\left(\alpha r^{d}\right),

we find that

\displaystyle G_{1}\biggl(\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}}\biggr)\ll A^{N-n/2}\cdot q_{1}^{n}q_{2}^{n}\cdot\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{|f|\leq 4A}\biggl|H\left(\frac{b_{1}f}{q_{1}},q_{1}\right)\biggr|^{2}\biggl|H\left(\frac{b_{2}f}{q_{2}},q_{2}\right)\biggr|^{2}\biggr)^{n/2}.

By applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality again, one obtains

(4.5)

G_{1}\biggl(\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}}\biggr)\ll A^{N-n/2}\cdot q_{1}^{n}q_{2}^{n}\cdot I_{1}^{n/4}\cdot I_{2}^{n/4},

where

I_{1}=\displaystyle\sum_{|f|\leq 4A}\left|H\left(\frac{b_{1}f}{q_{1}},q_{1}\right)\right|^{4}\ \text{and}\ I_{2}=\displaystyle\sum_{|f|\leq 4A}\left|H\left(\frac{b_{2}f}{q_{2}},q_{2}\right)\right|^{4}.

By splitting sum over
f
in terms of values of
(f,q_{1}),
we see that

(4.6)

I_{1}=\displaystyle\sum_{e|q_{1}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(f,q_{1})=q_{1}/e\\
|f|\leq 4A\end{subarray}}\left|H\left(\frac{b_{1}f}{q_{1}},q_{1}\right)\right|^{4}.

Meanwhile, when
(f,q_{1})=q_{1}/e
, on writing that
f=(q_{1}/e)\widetilde{f}
with
(\widetilde{f},e)=1,
it follows from [
References
, Theorem 4.2] that

(4.7)

\displaystyle H\left(\frac{b_{1}f}{q_{1}},q_{1}\right)=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r\leq q_{1}}e\left(\frac{b_{1}f}{q_{1}}r^{d}\right)=\frac{q_{1}}{e}\cdot\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r\leq e}e\left(\frac{b_{1}\widetilde{f}}{e}r^{d}\right)\ll q_{1}\cdot e^{-1/d}.

Hence, on substituting
(\ref{6.7})
into
(\ref{6.6})
, one has

(4.8)

I_{1}\ll\displaystyle\sum_{e|q_{1}}(Ae/q_{1})(q_{1}e^{-1/d})^{4}\leq Aq_{1}^{3}\displaystyle\sum_{e|q_{1}}e^{1-4/d}\leq Aq_{1}^{4+\epsilon}(q_{1}^{-1}+q_{1}^{-4/d}).

Similarly, we have

(4.9)

I_{2}\ll Aq_{2}^{4+\epsilon}(q_{2}^{-1}+q_{2}^{-4/d}).

Then, on substituting
(\ref{6.8})
and
(\ref{6.9})
into
(\ref{6.5})
, we obtain

(4.10)

G_{1}\biggl(\frac{b_{1}}{q_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{q_{2}}\biggr)\ll A^{N}\cdot q_{1}^{2n+\epsilon}q_{2}^{2n+\epsilon}\cdot(q_{1}^{-1}+q_{1}^{-4/d})^{n/4}(q_{2}^{-1}+q_{2}^{-4/d})^{n/4}.

Next, recall the definition of
G_{2}(\beta).
Since the range of summation
I_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}
in the definition of
G_{2}(\beta)
is a rectangular box depending on
{\boldsymbol{h}},
it follows by Lemma
2.10
with
l=1,\ B=0,\ A_{1}=A_{2}=2A
and
\sigma=1/4
that

(4.11)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}G_{2}(\beta)^{1/4}d\beta\ll A^{3N/4-k}.

On substituting
(\ref{6.10})
into (
4.2
) and that into
(\ref{6.16.1})
, we have

(4.12)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\mathcal{C}}S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(q)\biggr|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll\displaystyle\sum_{q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathcal{C}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq b_{1}\leq q_{1}\\
1\leq b_{2}\leq q_{2}\\
(q_{1},b_{1})=(q_{2},b_{2})=1\end{subarray}}A^{N/4}\cdot q_{1}^{\epsilon}q_{2}^{\epsilon}\cdot(q_{1}^{-1}+q_{1}^{-4/d})^{n/16}(q_{2}^{-1}+q_{2}^{-4/d})^{n/16}\cdot\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}G_{2}(\beta)^{1/4}d\beta.

Therefore, on substituting
(\ref{6.11})
into
(\ref{6.12})
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\mathcal{C}}S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(q)\biggr|^{2}\ll A^{N-k}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\mathcal{C}}q^{1+\epsilon}(q^{-1}+q^{-4/d})^{n/16}\biggr)^{2}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
\ref{lem5.15.1}.

∎

In advance of the statement of the second auxiliary lemma, we define

(4.13)

\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(w)=X^{n-d}A^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{|\beta|\leq w}\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{n}}e(\beta A^{-1}f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}))d\boldsymbol{\gamma}d\beta,

with
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}.

Furthermore, we recall the definition (
1.11
) of
\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}.
Note again that in the proof of Lemma
4.2
below, we shall make use of the same idea as that in section
5.1
(the separation procedure).

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
n
and
d
be natural numbers with
n\geq 8(d+1).
Suppose that
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N_{d,n}
variables of degree
k\geq 2.
Then, whenever
N\geq 200k(k-1)2^{k-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}-\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(w)\right|^{2}\ll A^{N-k-2}X^{2n-2d}(w^{2-n/(2(d+1))}+\zeta).

Proof.

Put
K(\beta)=\zeta^{-1}\mathfrak{w}_{\zeta}(\beta),
and we introduce functions
A_{1}({\boldsymbol{a}})
and
A_{2}({\boldsymbol{a}})
defined by

A_{1}({\boldsymbol{a}})=X^{n-d}A^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{n}}\displaystyle\int_{|\beta|\leq\zeta^{-3}}K(\beta)e(\beta A^{-1}f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}))d\beta d\boldsymbol{\gamma}

and

A_{2}({\boldsymbol{a}})=X^{n-d}A^{-1}\int_{[0,1]^{n}}\int_{|\beta|\leq\zeta^{-3}}e(\beta A^{-1}f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}))d\beta d\boldsymbol{\gamma}.

Then, by applying the elementary inequality
(a+b+c)^{2}\ll(a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2})
, we deduce that

(4.14)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}-\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(w)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|A_{2}({\boldsymbol{a}})-\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(w)\right|^{2}+\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|A_{2}({\boldsymbol{a}})-A_{1}({\boldsymbol{a}})\right|^{2}+\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|A_{1}({\boldsymbol{a}})-\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\right|^{2}.

We first analyze the first term. By orthogonality, we have

(4.15)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|A_{2}({\boldsymbol{a}})-\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(w)\right|^{2}=\displaystyle\sum_{\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\left|A_{2}({\boldsymbol{a}})-\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(w)\right|^{2}e(\alpha P({\boldsymbol{a}}))d\alpha.

On noting that

(4.16)

\displaystyle\left|A_{2}({\boldsymbol{a}})-\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(w)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=X^{2(n-d)}A^{-2}\displaystyle\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}w<|\beta_{1}|\leq\zeta^{-3}\\
w<|\beta_{2}|\leq\zeta^{-3}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{2n}}e(\beta_{1}A^{-1}f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1})-\beta_{2}A^{-1}f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}))d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}d\beta_{1}d\beta_{2},

we find that

(4.17)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|A_{2}({\boldsymbol{a}})-\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(w)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll(X^{n-d}A^{-1})^{2}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}w<|\beta_{1}|\leq\zeta^{-3}\\
w<|\beta_{2}|\leq\zeta^{-3}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{2n}}T(\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2})d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}d\beta_{1}d\beta_{2}d\alpha,

where

T(\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2})=\displaystyle\sum_{-A\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq A}e(\alpha P({\boldsymbol{a}}))e(\beta_{1}A^{-1}f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1})-\beta_{2}A^{-1}f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2})).

In order to apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we define

T_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},{\boldsymbol{h}})=\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{2n}}e(\beta_{1}A^{-1}f_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1})-\beta_{2}A^{-1}f_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}))d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}

and

T_{2}(\alpha,{\boldsymbol{h}})=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in I_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}}e(\alpha(P({\boldsymbol{a}}+{\boldsymbol{h}}))-P({\boldsymbol{a}})))

in which

I_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}=\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}|\ \|{\boldsymbol{a}}+{\boldsymbol{h}}\|_{\infty}\leq A,\ \|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\}.

Then, by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Weyl differencing argument together with the triangle inequality, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{2n}}T(\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2})d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}\leq\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{\|{\boldsymbol{h}}\|_{\infty}\leq 2A}\left|T_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},{\boldsymbol{h}})\right|\left|T_{2}(\alpha,{\boldsymbol{h}})\right|\biggr)^{1/2}.

If we further define

U_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=\displaystyle\sum_{\|{\boldsymbol{h}}\|_{\infty}\leq 2A}\left|T_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},{\boldsymbol{h}})\right|^{2}\ \text{and}\ U_{2}(\alpha)=\displaystyle\sum_{\|{\boldsymbol{h}}\|_{\infty}\leq 2A}\left|T_{2}(\alpha,{\boldsymbol{h}})\right|^{2},

we find by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality again that

(4.18)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{2n}}T(\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2})d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}\leq U_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})^{1/4}\cdot U_{2}(\alpha)^{1/4}.

We investigate
U_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
. Recall Definition
\ref{defn4.2}.
Furthermore, for
{\boldsymbol{d}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)}
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}^{(1)}
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}^{(2)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, we define

\displaystyle\Psi({\boldsymbol{d}},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}^{(2)})

\displaystyle:=\Psi({\boldsymbol{d}},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}^{(2)};\beta_{1},\beta_{2})

\displaystyle=A^{-1}\beta_{1}(\langle{\boldsymbol{d}},v_{d}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)})-v_{d}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)})\rangle)-A^{-1}\beta_{2}(\langle{\boldsymbol{d}},v_{d}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}^{(1)})-v_{d}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}^{(2)})\rangle).

Then, by squaring out and applying the triangle inequality, we deduce that

(4.19)

U_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})\ll A^{N-n}\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{4n}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{d}}\|_{\infty}\leq 2A\\
{\boldsymbol{d}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\end{subarray}}e(\Psi({\boldsymbol{d}},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}^{(2)}))\biggr|d\boldsymbol{\gamma},

where we wrote
d\boldsymbol{\gamma}=d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)}d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}^{(1)}d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}^{(2)},
for simplicity.
By applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the triangle inequality, we see from
(\ref{5.175.17})
that

U_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})\ll A^{N-n}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{d}}\|_{\infty}\leq 4A\\
{\boldsymbol{d}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\end{subarray}}A^{n}\biggl|\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{4n}}e(\Psi({\boldsymbol{d}},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}^{(2)}))d\boldsymbol{\gamma}\biggr|\biggr)^{1/2}.

On writing

I(\xi)=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}e(\xi\gamma^{d})d\gamma,

we find by applying the Hölder’s inequality that

(4.20)

\displaystyle U_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})

\displaystyle\ll A^{N-n/2}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{|f|\leq 4A}|I(\beta_{1}A^{-1}f)|^{2}|I(A^{-1}\beta_{2}f)|^{2}\biggr)^{n/2}

\displaystyle\ll A^{N-2n/(d+1)}\cdot\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{|f|\leq 4A}|I(\beta_{1}A^{-1}f)|^{d+1}\biggr)^{\frac{n}{d+1}}\cdot\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{|f|\leq 4A}|I(\beta_{2}A^{-1}f)|^{d+1}\biggr)^{\frac{n}{d+1}}.

Meanwhile, since we have

I(\xi)\ll\text{min}\left(1,\frac{1}{|\xi|^{1/d}}\right),

it follows that whenever
w<|\beta|\leq\zeta^{-3}
, one has

(4.21)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{|f|\leq 4A}|I(\beta A^{-1}f)|^{d+1}

\displaystyle\ll\displaystyle\sum_{|f|\leq A|\beta|^{-1}}1+\displaystyle\sum_{A|\beta|^{-1}\leq|f|\leq 4A}\frac{1}{|A^{-1}\beta f|^{(d+1)/d}}\ll A|\beta|^{-1}.

Hence, whenever
w<|\beta_{1}|\leq\zeta^{-3}
and
w<|\beta_{2}|\leq\zeta^{-3}
, we have

(4.22)

U_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})\ll A^{N}\cdot|\beta_{1}|^{-\frac{n}{d+1}}\cdot|\beta_{2}|^{-\frac{n}{d+1}}.

Next, recall the definition of
U_{2}(\alpha).
Since
I_{{\boldsymbol{h}}}
in
U_{2}(\alpha)
is a rectangular box depending on
{\boldsymbol{h}},
it follows by Lemma
2.10
with
l=1,B=0,A_{1}=A_{2}=2A
and
\sigma=1/4
that

(4.23)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}U_{2}(\alpha)^{1/4}d\alpha\ll A^{3N/4-k}.

On substituting
(\ref{5.185.18})
into
(\ref{5.165.16})
and that into
(\ref{5.155.15})
, we find that

(4.24)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|A_{2}({\boldsymbol{a}})-\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(w)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll(X^{n-d}A^{-1})^{2}\cdot A^{N/4}\displaystyle\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}w<|\beta_{1}|\leq\zeta^{-3}\\
w<|\beta_{2}|\leq\zeta^{-3}\end{subarray}}|\beta_{1}|^{-n/(4(d+1))}|\beta_{2}|^{-n/(4(d+1))}d\beta_{1}d\beta_{2}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}U_{2}(\alpha)^{1/4}d\alpha

\displaystyle\ll A^{N-k-2}\cdot X^{2n-2d}\cdot w^{2-n/(2(d+1))},

where we have used
(\ref{5.195.19})
.

Next, we turn to estimate the second term in
(\ref{5.145.145.14}).
The same argument leading from
(\ref{5.135.13})
to
(\ref{5.155.15})
delivers that

(4.25)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|A_{1}({\boldsymbol{a}})-A_{2}({\boldsymbol{a}})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll X^{2(n-d)}\cdot A^{-2}\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\beta_{1}|\leq\zeta^{-3}\\
|\beta_{2}|\leq\zeta^{-3}\end{subarray}}(1-K(\beta_{1}))(1-K(\beta_{2}))\mathcal{T}(\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2})d\beta_{1}d\beta_{2}d\alpha,

where

\mathcal{T}(\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{2n}}T(\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2})d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}d\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}.

Furthermore, the same argument leading from
(\ref{5.155.15})
to
(\ref{5.20202020})
provides that

(4.26)

\mathcal{T}(\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2})\ll U_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})^{1/4}\cdot U_{2}(\alpha)^{1/4}

and

(4.27)

\displaystyle U_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})

\displaystyle\ll A^{N-2n/(d+1)}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{|f|\leq 4A}|I(\beta_{1}A^{-1}f)|^{d+1}\biggr)^{n/(d+1)}\cdot\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{|f|\leq 4A}|I(\beta_{2}A^{-1}f)|^{d+1}\biggr)^{n/(d+1)}.

Then, by using the inequality

I(\xi)\ll\text{min}\left(1,\frac{1}{|\xi|^{1/d}}\right)

again together with the same treatment with
(\ref{5.212121})
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\sum_{|f|\leq 4A}|I(\beta A^{-1}f)|^{d+1}\ll A\cdot\text{min}(1,|\beta|^{-1}).

Hence, one has

(4.28)

U_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})\ll A^{N}\cdot\text{min}\left(1,|\beta_{1}|^{-n/(d+1)}\right)\cdot\text{min}\left(1,|\beta_{2}|^{-n/(d+1)}\right).

On substituting
(\ref{5.2626})
into
(\ref{5.2424})
and that into
(\ref{revise})
, we find that

(4.29)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|A_{1}({\boldsymbol{a}})-A_{2}({\boldsymbol{a}})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll X^{2(n-d)}\cdot A^{N/4-2}\biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}|1-K(\beta)|\cdot\text{min}\left(1,|\beta|^{-n/(4(d+1))}\right)d\beta\biggr)^{2}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}U_{2}(\alpha)^{1/4}d\alpha

\displaystyle\ll X^{2n-2d}\cdot A^{N-k-2}\biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}|1-K(\beta)|\cdot\text{min}\left(1,|\beta|^{-n/(4(d+1))}\right)d\beta\biggr)^{2},

where we have used
(\ref{5.195.19}).

Meanwhile, put
\mathfrak{C}=[-\zeta^{-1/2},\zeta^{-1/2}]
and
\mathfrak{D}={\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathfrak{C}.
Then from the Taylor expansion of
\mathfrak{w}_{\zeta}(\beta)
, we have

|1-K(\beta)|\ll\text{min}(1,|\beta\zeta|^{2}).

Whenever
n>4(d+1),
we obtain

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{C}}|1-K(\beta)|\cdot\text{min}(1,|\beta|^{-n/(4(d+1))})d\beta

\displaystyle\ll\text{sup}_{\beta\in\mathfrak{C}}|1-K(\beta)|\cdot\int_{\mathfrak{C}}\text{min}(1,|\beta|^{-n/(4(d+1))})d\beta\ll\zeta.

Whenever
n\geq 8(d+1)
, the contribution arising from
\mathfrak{D}
is

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{D}}|1-K(\beta)|\cdot\text{min}(1,|\beta|^{-n/(4(d+1))})d\beta\ll\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{D}}|\beta|^{-2}d\beta\ll\zeta^{1/2}.

Therefore, on substituting these estimates into
(\ref{5.2727})
, we conclude that

(4.30)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|A_{1}({\boldsymbol{a}})-A_{2}({\boldsymbol{a}})\right|^{2}\ll A^{N-k-2}\cdot X^{2n-2d}\cdot\zeta.

Lastly, we investigate the third term in
(\ref{5.145.145.14})
.
By the definition of
A_{1}({\boldsymbol{a}})
and
\mathfrak{J}^{*}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}
, we deduce that

(4.31)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|A_{1}({\boldsymbol{a}})-\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq X^{2n-2d}A^{-2}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\biggl|\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{n}}\displaystyle\int_{|\beta|>\zeta^{-3}}\left(\frac{\text{sin}(\pi\beta\zeta)}{\pi\beta\zeta}\right)^{2}e(\beta A^{-1}f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}))d\beta d\boldsymbol{\gamma}\biggr|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq X^{2n-2d}A^{-2}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\biggl(\frac{1}{\zeta}\int_{|\beta^{\prime}|>\zeta^{-2}}\left|\frac{\text{sin}(\pi\beta^{\prime})}{\pi\beta^{\prime}}\right|^{2}d\beta^{\prime}\biggr)^{2}

\displaystyle\ll X^{2n-2d}\cdot A^{-2}\cdot\zeta^{2}\cdot\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}1\ll A^{N-k-2}\cdot X^{2n-2d}\cdot\zeta^{2},

where we have used the triangle inequality, change of variable
\beta^{\prime}=\beta\zeta
and the bound

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}1\ll A^{N-k}.

Therefore, on substituting
(\ref{5.205.20})
,
(\ref{5.285.28})
and
(\ref{5.295.29})
into (
4.14
), we complete the proof of Lemma
\ref{lemma5.2}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Recall the definition
(\ref{4.1})
and
(\ref{4.24.2})
of
\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{B})
and
\mathfrak{M}
. Then, we find that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{M})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{M}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{x}}\leq X\\
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\end{subarray}}e(\alpha f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))d\alpha

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq q\leq w}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq b\leq q\\
(q,b)=1\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\int_{|\alpha-b/q|\leq\frac{w}{AX^{d}}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{x}}\leq X\end{subarray}}e(\alpha f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))d\alpha.

Recall the definition (
4.13
) of
\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(w).
By applying classical treatments in major arcs [
References
, Lemma 5.1] and writing
\beta=\alpha-b/q,
we readily find that

(4.32)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{M})=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq q\leq w}S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(q)\mathfrak{J}_{a}(w)+O(A^{-1}X^{n-d-1}w^{5}),

where

S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(q)=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq b\leq q\\
(q,b)=1\end{subarray}}q^{-n}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{r}}\leq q}e\left(\frac{b}{q}f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{r}})\right).

Meanwhile, recall the definition
(\ref{def2.3})
and (
1.9
) of
W
and
\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}
, and note from the classical treatment that

\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}=\displaystyle\prod_{p\leq w}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq h\leq\log_{p}w\end{subarray}}S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(p^{h})\biggr).

If we define a set

\mathcal{Q}=\{q\in(w,W]|\ \text{for all primes}\ p,\ p^{h}\|q\Rightarrow p^{h}\leq w\}

and define

\mathcal{E}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}=\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(q),

we find from the multiplicativity of
S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(q)
that

(4.33)

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq q\leq w}S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(q)=\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}-\mathcal{E}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}.

Meanwhile, note that

\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X)=\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{M})+\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{m}).

Then, on recalling the definition of
\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}
, we deduce from
(\ref{6.1})
and
(\ref{6.2})
together with applications of elementary inequality
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
that

(4.34)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X)-\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{M})-\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\right|^{2}+\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{m})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2}+\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{m})\right|^{2}+O\biggl(A^{-2}X^{2n-2d-2}w^{10}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}1\biggr),

where

\Sigma_{1}=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{E}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\right|^{2}\ \text{and}\ \Sigma_{2}=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|(\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}-\mathcal{E}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}})(\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}-\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(w))\right|^{2}.

First, we estimate the third and fourth terms of the last expression in (
4.34
). Since we have

\#\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}|\ \|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A,\ P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\}\ll A^{N-k},

it follows by Proposition
3.1
that

(4.35)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X,\mathfrak{m})\right|^{2}+O\biggl(A^{-2}X^{2n-2d-2}w^{10}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}1\biggr)\ll A^{N-k-2}X^{2n-2d}(\log A)^{-\delta},

with some
\delta>0.

Next, we turn to estimate the first term of the last expression in (
4.34
). From the trivial bound, we have

(4.36)

\left|\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\right|^{2}\ll X^{2(n-d)}\zeta^{-2}A^{-2}=X^{2(n-d)}w^{10}A^{-2}.

By Lemma
4.1
with
B=w,C=W
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{Q}
, we have

(4.37)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{E}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll A^{N-k}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{q\geq w}q^{1+\epsilon}(q^{-1}+q^{-4/d})^{n/16}\biggr)^{2}

\displaystyle\ll A^{N-k}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{q\geq w}\left(q^{1-n/16+\epsilon}+q^{1-n/(4d)+\epsilon}\right)\biggr)^{2}.

Meanwhile, from the hypotheses
n_{1}\leq\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor/8
and
2X^{d}\leq A\leq X^{n_{1}-d}
in the statement of Theorem
\ref{thm2.2},
we see that
n>32d.
Hence, it follows from
(\ref{6.17})
together with the hypothesis
d\geq 4
that

(4.38)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{E}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll A^{N-k}\left(w^{2-2d}+w^{-6}\right)^{2}\ll A^{N-k}\cdot w^{-12}.

Therefore, combining
(\ref{6.1616})
and
(\ref{6.18})
, we conclude that

(4.39)

\Sigma_{1}=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{E}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\right|^{2}\ll A^{N-k-2}X^{2n-2d}w^{-2}.

Lastly, it remains to estimate the second term of the last expression in (
4.34
). From the trivial bound, we have

\left|\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}-\mathcal{E}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\right|^{2}=\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq q\leq w}S_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(q)\biggr|^{2}\leq w^{4}.

Hence, we deduce by applying Lemma
4.2
with
n>32d
and
d\geq 4
that

(4.40)

\Sigma_{2}=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|(\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}-\mathcal{E}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}})(\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}-\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(w))\right|^{2}\ll A^{N-k-2}\cdot X^{2n-2d}\cdot w^{-1}.

Then, on recalling the definition of
w
and substituting
(\ref{6.161616})
,
(\ref{5.202020})
and
(\ref{5.3030})
into the last expression in (
4.34
), one concludes that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(X)-\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\right|^{2}\ll A^{N-k-2}X^{2n-2d}(\log A)^{-\delta},

for some
\delta
with
0<\delta<1.
This completes the proof of Theorem
\ref{thm2.2}.

∎

5.
Major arcs

In this section, we show that
\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}
and
\mathfrak{J}^{*}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}
are rarely small. In order to verify this, we develop a method combining the classical treatments of major arcs with those used in [
\ref{ref3},
section 5].

5.1.
Singular series treatment I

Our purpose in sections
5.1
and
5.2
is to prove Proposition
5.1
below.
In advance of the statement of this proposition, we recall the definition
(\ref{def2.3})
of
W
, and recall the definition
\mathcal{A}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(A;P)
in section 1 and that

\mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}=\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};W).

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
A
and
X
be positive numbers with
X^{3}\leq A.
Suppose that
n
and
d
are natural numbers with
n>d+1
and
d\geq 2
, and that
X^{3}\leq A
. Suppose that
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N_{d,n}
variables of degree
k\geq 2.
Then, whenever
N\geq 1000n^{2}8^{k}
, one has

A^{-N+k}\cdot\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(A;P)\middle|\ \begin{aligned} \mathfrak{S}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\leq(\log A)^{-\eta}\end{aligned}\right\}\ll(\log A)^{-\eta/(20n)},

for any
\eta>0.

We provide here the structure of sections
5.1
and
5.2
. For a prime number
p
and a natural number
r,
in section
5.1.1
, we give asymptotic formulae for

\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\middle|\ P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ (\textrm{mod}\ p^{r})\right\}\ \text{and}\ \displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\ \textrm{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r}),

respectively. In section
5.1.2
, we provide a bound for a variance of
\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r}),
that is

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}(\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})-1)^{2}.

Recall the definition of
v_{p^{r}}(\boldsymbol{v})
and
\mathcal{R}_{m}(Q)
leading to Lemma
2.4
and Lemma
\ref{lem3.5}.
For
r,v,e\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
0\leq e\leq r-v
, we introduce a condition
\mathcal{C}_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})
as follows. We say that
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
satisfies the condition
\mathcal{C}_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})
when one has

\displaystyle(i)\ p^{v}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\ \text{and}\ P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}

\displaystyle(ii)\ \exists\ {\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{r-v})\ \text{such that}\ v_{p^{r-v}}(\nabla f_{p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))=e\ \textrm{and}\ f_{p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r-v}.

For
r,v,e\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
0\leq e\leq r-v
, define a set

(5.1)

\displaystyle A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})=\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\middle|\ {\boldsymbol{a}}\ \text{satisfies the condition}\ \mathcal{C}_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})\right\}.

In section
5.2.1
, we provide an upper bound for
\#A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r}).
In section
5.2.2
, we give a lower bound for
\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})
with
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r}).
Combining all these estimates together with the strategy used in [
\ref{ref3},
section 5], we shall prove Proposition
5.1
at the end of section
5.2
.

5.1.1.
Proofs of Lemmas
5.2
and
5.3

In order to describe the following lemma, recall that
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N
variables of degree
k
, and write

N_{1}(p^{r})=\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\middle|\ P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ (\textrm{mod}\ p^{r})\right\}.

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose that
p
is a prime number, and
r
is a natural number. Then, whenever
N>(k-1)2^{k+1}
, we have

N_{1}(p^{r})=p^{rN-r}+O(p^{rN-r-N/(2^{k}(k-1))}).

Proof.

This immediately follows from Lemma
2.9
with
W=p^{r}.

∎

Recall the definition
(\ref{def6.1})
of
\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})
. To describe the following lemma, recall again that
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N
variables of degree
k.
It is convenient to define

N_{2}(p^{r})=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ \textrm{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r}).

Lemma 5.3
.

Suppose that
p
is a prime number, and
r
is a natural number. Then, whenever
n>d
and
N>(k-1)2^{k-1}(2+n/d+n-d)
, we have

N_{2}(p^{r})=p^{r(N-1)}+O(p^{rN-\lceil r/d\rceil n}+p^{rN-r-n+d}).

Proof.

Inverting the order of summation we obtain

(5.2)

N_{2}(p^{r})=p^{-r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{g}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{n}}\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\middle|\ \begin{aligned} \langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle&\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})&\equiv 0\ \textrm{mod}\ p^{r}\end{aligned}\right\}.

By orthogonality, one has

(5.3)

\displaystyle\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\middle|\ \begin{aligned} \langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle&\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})&\equiv 0\ \textrm{mod}\ p^{r}\end{aligned}\right\}=p^{-2r}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq l_{1},l_{2}\leq p^{r}}\Xi({\boldsymbol{g}},l_{1},l_{2}),

where

\Xi({\boldsymbol{g}},l_{1},l_{2})=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq p^{r}}e\biggl(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{a}})l_{1}}{p^{r}}\biggr)e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle l_{2}}{p^{r}}\biggr).

Write
(l_{1},p^{r})=p^{r-r_{1}}
with
0\leq r_{1}\leq r.
Then, on substituting (
5.3
) into
(\ref{eqeq5.1})
and by splitting summation over
{\boldsymbol{g}}
in terms of values of
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})
, we see that

(5.4)

N_{2}(p^{r})=p^{-r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2}\leq r\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}(S({\boldsymbol{g}})+T({\boldsymbol{g}})),

where

S({\boldsymbol{g}})=p^{-2r}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r_{1}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l_{1},p^{r})=p^{r-r_{1}}\\
1\leq l_{2}\leq p^{r}\end{subarray}}\Xi({\boldsymbol{g}},l_{1},l_{2})

and

T({\boldsymbol{g}})=p^{-2r}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq l_{2}\leq p^{r}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}}e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle l_{2}}{p^{r}}\biggr).

Recall the definition of
\Xi({\boldsymbol{g}},l_{1},l_{2})
and that
p^{r-r_{1}}=(l_{1},p^{r})
. For fixed
r_{1},
let us write
l_{1}=p^{r-r_{1}}\widetilde{l}_{1}
with
(\widetilde{l}_{1},p)=1.
Then, on writing
{\boldsymbol{a}}=p^{r_{1}}{\boldsymbol{n}}+{\boldsymbol{m}}
with
0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-r_{1}}-1
and
1\leq{\boldsymbol{m}}\leq p^{r_{1}}
, we see that

\Xi({\boldsymbol{g}},l_{1},l_{2})=\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-r_{1}}-1}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{m}}\leq p^{r_{1}}}e\biggl(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{m}})\widetilde{l}_{1}}{p^{r_{1}}}\biggr)e\biggl(\frac{\langle p^{r_{1}}{\boldsymbol{n}}+{\boldsymbol{m}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle l_{2}}{p^{r}}\biggr).

On substituting this expression into
S({\boldsymbol{g}})
and that into
(\ref{eqeqeq5.3})
, it follows by the triangle inequality that

(5.5)

\displaystyle p^{-r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2}\leq r\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}S({\boldsymbol{g}})

\displaystyle\leq p^{-r(n+1)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r_{1}\leq r\\
0\leq r_{2}\leq r\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\widetilde{l}_{1}\leq p^{r_{1}}\\
(\widetilde{l}_{1},p)=1\end{subarray}}\bigl|S_{1}(\widetilde{l}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}})\bigr|\cdot\bigl|S_{2}({\boldsymbol{g}})\bigr|,

where

S_{1}(\widetilde{l}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}})=\sup_{1\leq l_{2}\leq p^{r}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{m}}\leq p^{r_{1}}}e\biggl(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{m}})\widetilde{l}_{1}}{p^{r_{1}}}\biggr)e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{m}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle l_{2}}{p^{r}}\biggr)\biggr|

and

S_{2}({\boldsymbol{g}})=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq l_{2}\leq p^{r}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-r_{1}}-1}e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{n}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle l_{2}}{p^{r-r_{1}}}\biggr)\biggr|.

We first analyze the sum
S_{1}(\widetilde{l}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}).
Since
P({\boldsymbol{m}})
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial and
(p^{r_{1}},\widetilde{l}_{1})=1
, it follows by the Weyl type estimate for exponential sums over minor arcs [
References
, Lemma 5.4] that

(5.6)

S_{1}(\widetilde{l}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}})\ll p^{Nr_{1}-Nr_{1}/(2^{k-1}(k-1))+\epsilon}.

Next, we analyze the sum
S_{2}({\boldsymbol{g}})
. We infer from orthogonality that the inner sum in
S_{2}({\boldsymbol{g}})
is a non-negative integer. Thus, by changing the order of summations, we have

S_{2}({\boldsymbol{g}})=\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-r_{1}}-1}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq l_{2}\leq p^{r}}e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{n}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle l_{2}}{p^{r-r_{1}}}\biggr).

Then, by orthogonality, one has

(5.7)

\displaystyle S_{2}({\boldsymbol{g}})

\displaystyle=p^{r}\cdot\{0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-r_{1}}-1|\ \langle{\boldsymbol{n}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r-r_{1}}\}

For fixed
{\boldsymbol{g}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{n}
with
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}},
we observe that there exists
i
such that

((\nu_{d,n}(p^{-r_{2}}{\boldsymbol{g}}))_{i},p)=1.

With this observation in mind, we infer from (
5.7
) that

(5.8)

\displaystyle S_{2}({\boldsymbol{g}})

\displaystyle=p^{r}\cdot\{0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-r_{1}}-1|\ \langle{\boldsymbol{n}},\nu_{d,n}(p^{-r_{2}}{\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{\max\{0,r-r_{1}-dr_{2}\}}\}

\displaystyle=p^{r}\cdot p^{(r-r_{1})N}\cdot p^{-\max\{0,r-r_{1}-dr_{2}\}}.

Therefore, on substituting (
5.6
) and
(\ref{6.86.8})
into
(\ref{5.45.4})
, we find that

\displaystyle p^{-r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2}\leq r\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}S({\boldsymbol{g}})

\displaystyle\ll p^{-r(n+1)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r_{1}\leq r\\
0\leq r_{2}\leq r\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}p^{r_{1}}\cdot p^{r+rN-r_{1}N/(2^{k-1}(k-1))+\epsilon}\cdot p^{-\max\{0,r-r_{1}-dr_{2}\}}

\displaystyle\ll p^{-r(n+1)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r_{1}\leq r\\
0\leq r_{2}\leq r\end{subarray}}p^{(r-r_{2})n}\cdot p^{r_{1}}\cdot p^{r+rN-r_{1}N/(2^{k-1}(k-1))+\epsilon}\cdot p^{-\max\{0,r-r_{1}-dr_{2}\}}.

By splitting the sum over
r_{2}
, we find that

(5.9)

p^{-r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2}\leq r\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}S({\boldsymbol{g}})\ll X_{1}+X_{2},

where

X_{1}=p^{-r(n+1)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r_{1}\leq r\\
0\leq r_{2}\leq(r-r_{1})/d\end{subarray}}p^{(r-r_{2})n}\cdot p^{r_{1}}\cdot p^{r+rN-r_{1}N/(2^{k-1}(k-1))+\epsilon}\cdot p^{-(r-r_{1}-dr_{2})}

and

X_{2}=p^{-r(n+1)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r_{1}\leq r\\
(r-r_{1})/d<r_{2}\leq r\end{subarray}}p^{(r-r_{2})n}\cdot p^{r_{1}}\cdot p^{r+rN-r_{1}N/(2^{k-1}(k-1))+\epsilon}.

With our choice of
N
in the statement of Lemma
5.3
, a modicum of computation delivers that

X_{1}\ll p^{rN-r+2-N/(2^{k-1}(k-1)))+\epsilon}\ \text{and}\ X_{2}\ll p^{rN-(n/d)r+n/d-N/(2^{k-1}(k-1))+\epsilon}.

It remains to estimate
p^{-r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2}\leq r\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}T({\boldsymbol{g}})
in
(\ref{eqeqeq5.3}).
By splitting the sum over
{\boldsymbol{a}}
in
T({\boldsymbol{g}})
in terms of values of
({\boldsymbol{a}},p^{r})
, we see that

(5.10)

\displaystyle p^{-r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2}\leq r\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}T({\boldsymbol{g}})

\displaystyle=p^{-r(n+1)}\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq r_{2}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}}\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq s\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{(p^{r},{\boldsymbol{a}})=p^{r-s}}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq l_{2}\leq p^{r}}e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle l_{2}}{p^{r}}\biggr).

On noting that
(\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})p^{-dr_{2}},p)=1
and
({\boldsymbol{a}}p^{-r+s},p)=1,
we find by applying orthogonality that the innermost sum is turned into

(5.11)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq l_{2}\leq p^{r}}e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{a}}p^{-r+s},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})p^{-dr_{2}}\rangle}{p^{r}}\cdot p^{dr_{2}+r-s}\cdot l_{2}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{aligned} &p^{r}\ \ \text{when}\ \langle{\boldsymbol{a}}p^{-r+s},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})p^{-dr_{2}}\rangle\equiv 0\ \textrm{mod}\ p^{\max\{0,s-dr_{2}\}}\\
&0\ \ \ \textrm{otherwise}.\end{aligned}\right.

By splitting sums over
s
and
r_{2}
according to whether
\max\{0,s-dr_{2}\}=0
or
\max\{0,s-dr_{2}\}\neq 0
, we see from
(\ref{6.1111})
and
(\ref{6.126.12})
that

(5.12)

p^{-r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2}\leq r\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}T({\boldsymbol{g}})=p^{-r(n+1)}(U_{1}+U_{2}),

where

U_{1}=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2},s\leq r\\
s\leq dr_{2}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}}\displaystyle\sum_{(p^{r},{\boldsymbol{a}})=p^{r-s}}p^{r}\ \text{and}\ U_{2}=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2},s\leq r\\
s>dr_{2}\end{subarray}}p^{r}\displaystyle\sum_{({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}}V,

in which

V=\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\middle|\ \begin{aligned} \langle{\boldsymbol{a}}p^{-r+s},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})p^{-dr_{2}}\rangle&\equiv 0\ \textrm{mod}\ p^{s-dr_{2}}\\
(p^{r},{\boldsymbol{a}})&=p^{r-s}\end{aligned}\right\}.

We first see that

(5.13)

\displaystyle p^{-r(n+1)}U_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2},s\leq r\\
s\leq dr_{2}\end{subarray}}p^{r}p^{Ns}p^{(r-r_{2})n}p^{-r(n+1)}\leq\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s\leq r\\
s/d\leq r_{2}\leq r\end{subarray}}p^{Ns-r_{2}n}\ll p^{rN-\lceil r/d\rceil n}.

Next, we find that

(5.14)

\displaystyle p^{-r(n+1)}U_{2}=p^{-rn}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2},s\leq r\\
s>dr_{2}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}}V.

Meanwhile, for
{\boldsymbol{g}}
with
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}
and
{\boldsymbol{a}}
with
(p^{r},{\boldsymbol{a}})=p^{r-s}
, one has
(\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})p^{-dr_{2}},p)=1
and
({\boldsymbol{a}}p^{-r+s},p)=1.
Hence, we infer that

V=p^{sN}\cdot p^{-s+dr_{2}}-p^{(s-1)N}\cdot p^{-s+dr_{2}+1}.

Then, on noting that the number of
{\boldsymbol{g}}
with
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}
is
p^{(r-r_{2})n}(1+O(p^{-n})),
we deduce that

(5.15)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}}V=p^{(r-r_{2})n}(1+O(p^{-n}))(p^{s(N-1)}\cdot p^{dr_{2}}-p^{(s-1)(N-1)}\cdot p^{dr_{2}})

\displaystyle=(p^{s(N-1)}\cdot p^{rn-r_{2}n+dr_{2}}-p^{(s-1)(N-1)}\cdot p^{rn-r_{2}n+dr_{2}})(1+O(p^{-n}))

\displaystyle=p^{s(N-1)}\cdot p^{rn-r_{2}n+dr_{2}}(1+O(p^{-n})).

Therefore, on substituting
(\ref{5.14})
into
(\ref{5.13}),
we conclude that

(5.16)

\displaystyle p^{-r(n+1)}U_{2}

\displaystyle=p^{-rn}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2},s\leq r\\
s>dr_{2}\end{subarray}}p^{s(N-1)}\cdot p^{rn-r_{2}n+dr_{2}}(1+O(p^{-n}))

\displaystyle=p^{r(N-1)}+O(p^{r(N-1)-(n-d)}).

Furthermore, by substituting
(\ref{5.12})
and
(\ref{5.15})
into
(\ref{5.11})
, we find that

(5.17)

p^{-r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2}\leq r\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}T({\boldsymbol{g}})=p^{rN-r}+O(p^{rN-\lceil r/d\rceil n}+p^{rN-r-n+d}),

and substituting
(\ref{6.96.96.9})
and
(\ref{5.16})
into
(\ref{eqeqeq5.3})
, it follows that whenever

N>(k-1)2^{k-1}(2+n/d+n-d),

we have

N_{2}(p^{r})=p^{rN-r}+O(p^{rN-\lceil r/d\rceil n}+p^{rN-r-n+d}).

∎

5.1.2.
Proof of Lemma
5.4

Recall the definition
(\ref{def6.1})
of
\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})
and that
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N
variables of degree
k
. The following lemma provides the bound for a variance of
\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Suppose that
p
is a prime number, and
r
is a natural number. Then, whenever
n>d
and
N>(k-1)2^{k-1}(n-d+3)
, one has

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}(\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})-1)^{2}\ll p^{rN-n\lceil r/d\rceil}+p^{rN-r+d-n}.

We provide the proof of Lemma
5.4
at the end of this subsection
5.1.2
. We note here that this proof is technical and long, and thus we temporarily pause and provide a sketch of the proof of Lemma
\ref{lem5.3}.
The proof is based on a method combining the classical treatment of major arcs with the strategy used in [
\ref{ref3},
section 5].

First of all, by squaring out, one obtains

(5.18)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}(\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})-1)^{2}=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}(\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})^{2}-2\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})+1).

To bound sums associated with the second and third summands in
(\ref{5.1717}),
we make use of Lemma
5.2
and Lemma
\ref{lem5.2}.
It remains to estimate the second moment of
\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})
as
{\boldsymbol{a}}
runs over
1\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq p^{r}
with
P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}
. Define

N_{3}(p^{r}):=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\ \textrm{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})^{2}.

Then, inverting order of summations we have

(5.19)

N_{3}(p^{r})=p^{-2r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\in[1,p^{r}]^{n}}\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\middle|\ \begin{aligned} \langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{i})\rangle&\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\ (i=1,2)\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})&\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{aligned}\right\}.

By orthogonality, the summand on the right hand side in (
5.19
) is seen to be

(5.20)

p^{-3r}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq l_{1},l_{2},l_{3}\leq p^{r}}\Xi({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},l_{1},l_{2},l_{3}),

where

(5.21)

\Xi({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},l_{1},l_{2},l_{3})=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq p^{r}}e\biggl(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{a}})l_{1}}{p^{r}}\biggr)e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1})\rangle l_{2}}{p^{r}}\biggr)e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\rangle l_{3}}{p^{r}}\biggr).

Then, on substituting
(\ref{eq5.2})
into
(\ref{eq5.1})
and by splitting summation over
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}
and
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}
in terms of values of
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})
and
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})
, we see that

(5.22)

N_{3}(p^{r})=p^{-2r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}(S({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})+T({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})),

where

(5.23)

S({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=p^{-3r}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r_{1}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l_{1},p^{r})=p^{r-r_{1}}\\
1\leq l_{2},l_{3}\leq p^{r}\end{subarray}}\Xi({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},l_{1},l_{2},l_{3})

and

T({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=p^{-3r}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq l_{2},l_{3}\leq p^{r}}\Xi({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r},l_{2},l_{3}).

It is worth noting that the sum of the second summand
T({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})
in
(\ref{5.19})
gives the main term of
N_{3}(p^{r}),
which is provided in Lemma
\ref{lem5.6}.
Furthermore, the sum of the first summand
S({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})
in
(\ref{5.19})
yields the error term of
N_{3}(p^{r}),
which is provided in Lemma
\ref{lem5.5}.
Therefore, we combine all to prove Lemma
\ref{lem5.3}.

For the proofs of Lemmas
5.7
and
\ref{lem5.5},
we require two auxiliary lemmas.
In order to describe the first auxiliary lemma, we recall the definition of
N(d,m)=\binom{m+d-1}{d}
with
m,d\in{\mathbb{N}}.
Furthermore, given vectors
{\boldsymbol{z}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{z}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{M},
we use the notation
\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{z}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{z}}_{2})
for the greatest common divisor of the
2\times 2
minors of the
M\times 2
matrix whose columns are the vectors
{\boldsymbol{z}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{z}}_{2}.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
c_{1},c_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
(c_{1},p)=(c_{2},p)=1
, and let
t_{1},t_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
with
t_{1}\leq t_{2}.
Suppose that
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{h}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
with

{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}=c_{1}p^{t_{1}}{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},\ {\boldsymbol{h}}_{2}=c_{2}p^{t_{2}}{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2},

where vectors
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}
and
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}
are primitive in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
. Consider a quantity
\mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{h}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{h}}_{2})
defined by
