\mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{h}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{h}}_{2}):=\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{n}}\in[0,p^{v}-1]^{N(d,m)}\middle|\begin{aligned} \langle{\boldsymbol{n}},\nu_{d,m}({\boldsymbol{h}}_{i})\rangle\equiv 0\ \ \text{mod}\ p^{v}\ (i=1,2)\end{aligned}\right\}.

Then, we have

\mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{h}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{h}}_{2})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &p^{v(N(d,m)-1)}\cdot p^{-\max\{0,v-dt_{1}\}}\cdot\mathcal{V}\ \ \ \text{when}\ {\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}\neq\pm{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}\\
&p^{vN(d,m)}\cdot p^{-\max\{0,v-dt_{1}\}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{when}\ {\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}=\pm{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2},\end{aligned}\right.

where

\mathcal{V}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &p^{dt_{2}}{\rm{gcd}}(\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}),p^{v-dt_{2}})\ \ \text{when}\ v-dt_{2}\geq 0\\
&p^{v}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{when}\ v-dt_{2}<0.\end{aligned}\right.

Proof.

Notice that

(5.24)

\mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{h}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{h}}_{2}):=\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{n}}\in[0,p^{v}-1]^{N(d,m)}\middle|\begin{aligned} &\langle{\boldsymbol{n}},\nu_{d,m}(c_{1}{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1})\rangle\equiv 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{mod}\ p^{\max\{0,v-dt_{1}\}}\\
&\langle{\boldsymbol{n}},\nu_{d,m}(p^{t_{2}-t_{1}}c_{2}{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2})\rangle\equiv 0\ \ \text{mod}\ p^{\max\{0,v-dt_{1}\}}\end{aligned}\right\}.

We first investigate the case
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}\neq\pm{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}.
Observe that
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}\neq\pm{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}
implies that
\nu_{d,m}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1})
and
\nu_{d,m}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{2})
are linearly independent. We find by the Smith normal form theorem that there exists
f\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
T\in GL_{N(d,m)}({\mathbb{Z}})
with
|\text{det}(T)|=1
such that

\biggl(\begin{aligned} \nu_{d,m}(&c_{1}{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1})\\
\nu_{d,m}(c_{2}&p^{t_{2}-t_{1}}{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2})\end{aligned}\biggr)T=\left(\begin{aligned} 1\ \ &0\ \ 0\ \cdots\ 0\\
f\ \ &S\ \ 0\ \cdots\ 0\end{aligned}\right),

where
S:=S({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2})=\mathcal{G}\left(\nu_{d,m}(c_{1}{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}),\nu_{d,m}(c_{2}p^{t_{2}-t_{1}}{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2})\right).
Thus, one has

N({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2})=\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{n}}\in[0,p^{v}-1]^{N(d,m)}\middle|\left(\begin{aligned} 1\ \ &0\ \ 0\ \cdots\ 0\\
f\ \ &S\ \ 0\ \cdots\ 0\end{aligned}\right){\boldsymbol{n}}^{\top}\equiv{\bf 0}\ \ \text{mod}\ p^{\max\{0,v-dt_{1}\}}\right\}.

By [
References
, Lemma 3.11], we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(\nu_{d,m}(c_{1}{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}),\nu_{d,m}(c_{2}p^{(t_{2}-t_{1})}{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}))

\displaystyle=c_{1}^{d}\cdot c_{2}^{d}\cdot p^{(t_{2}-t_{1})d}\cdot\mathcal{G}(\nu_{d,m}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}),\nu_{d,m}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}))

\displaystyle=c_{1}^{d}\cdot c_{2}^{d}\cdot p^{(t_{2}-t_{1})d}\cdot\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}).

Hence, on recalling that
(c_{1},p)=(c_{2},p)=1
, we find that

(5.25)

\mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{h}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{h}}_{2})=(p^{v})^{N(d,m)-2}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{1}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{2},

where

\mathcal{Z}_{1}=\#\{0\leq n_{1}\leq p^{v}-1|\ n_{1}\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{\max\{0,v-dt_{1}\}}\}

and

\mathcal{Z}_{2}=\#\{0\leq n_{2}\leq p^{v}-1|\ p^{(t_{2}-t_{1})d}\cdot\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2})\cdot n_{2}\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{\max\{0,v-dt_{1}\}}\}.

First, consider the case
v-dt_{2}\geq 0
. Since
v-dt_{2}\geq 0.
Since
t_{1}\leq t_{2},
one has
v-dt_{1}\geq 0.
Then, the condition
p^{(t_{2}-t_{1})d}\cdot\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2})\cdot n_{2}\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{\max\{0,v-dt_{1}\}}

implies that

\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2})\cdot n_{2}\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{v-dt_{2}}.

Hence, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{2}

\displaystyle=p^{v}(p^{v-dt_{2}}(\text{gcd}(\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}),p^{v-dt_{2}}))^{-1})^{-1}

\displaystyle=p^{dt_{2}}\text{gcd}(\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}),p^{v-dt_{2}}).

Next, consider the case
v-dt_{2}<0
. Obviously, we have

\mathcal{Z}_{2}=p^{v}.

Therefore, on noting that
\mathcal{Z}_{1}=p^{v}\cdot p^{-\max\{0,v-dt_{1}\}}
, it follows from
(\ref{5.2121})
that

(5.26)

\mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{h}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{h}}_{2})=(p^{v})^{N(d,m)-1}\cdot p^{-\max\{0,v-dt_{1}\}}\cdot\mathcal{V},

where

\mathcal{V}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &p^{dt_{2}}\textrm{gcd}(\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}),p^{v-dt_{2}})\ \text{when}\ v-dt_{2}\geq 0\\
&p^{v}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{when}\ v-dt_{2}<0\end{aligned}\right.

Next, we analyse
N({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2})
with
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}=\pm{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}.
On recalling that
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}
is primitive and noting that
p\nmid{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}
implies
p\nmid\nu_{d,m}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}),
we see from (
5.24
) that

(5.27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{h}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{h}}_{2})

\displaystyle=\#\{0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{v}|\ \langle{\boldsymbol{n}},\nu_{d,m}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1})\rangle\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{\max\{0,v-dt_{1}\}}\}

\displaystyle=p^{vN(d,m)}\cdot p^{-\max\{0,v-dt_{1}\}}.

Then, by
(\ref{5.2222})
and
(\ref{5.2323})
, we complete the proof of Lemma
\ref{lem5.4}.

∎

We make use of Lemma
5.5
in order to prove the second auxiliary lemma, which will be used in the proofs of Lemmas
5.7
and
\ref{lem5.5}.
In advance of the statement of Lemma
5.6
, we recall the definition
(\ref{1.21.2})
of
N.
Furthermore, for
z\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
and
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
, we define

\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{z}]^{N}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\middle|\ \langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{i})\rangle\equiv 0\ \textrm{mod}\ p^{z}\ (i=1,2)\right\}.

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose that
r
and
z
are non-negative integers with
z\leq r
. Then, whenever
n>d
, one has

(5.28)

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\\
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\\
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=p^{z(N-2)+2rn}\left(1+O\left(p^{d-n}+p^{z-n\lceil z/d\rceil}\right)\right).

Proof.

For
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
and
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
with
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}
and
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}
, we see that there exist
c_{1},c_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
(c_{1},p)=(c_{2},p)=1
and primitive vectors
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}
and
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}
in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
such that

(5.29)

{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}=c_{1}p^{r_{2}}{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},\ {\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}=c_{2}p^{r_{3}}{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}.

Then, in the case that
r_{2}\leq r_{3}
, by applying Lemma
5.5
with
t_{1}=r_{2},t_{2}=r_{3},m=n
and
v=z
, one has

(5.30)

\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &p^{z(N-1)}\cdot p^{-\max\{0,z-dr_{2}\}}\cdot\mathcal{V}_{1}\ \text{when}\ {\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}\neq\pm{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}\\
&p^{zN}\cdot p^{-\max\{0,z-dr_{2}\}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{when}\ {\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}=\pm{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2},\end{aligned}\right.,

where

\mathcal{V}_{1}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &p^{dr_{3}}\text{gcd}(\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}),p^{z-dr_{3}})\ \text{when}\ z-dr_{3}\geq 0\\
&p^{z}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{when}\ z-dr_{3}<0.\end{aligned}\right.

Define

\Sigma_{1}=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=1\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=1\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}),

(5.31)

\Sigma_{2}=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r\\
r_{2}\leq r_{3}\\
(r_{2},r_{3})\neq(0,0))\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})

and

\Sigma_{3}=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r\\
r_{2}>r_{3}\\
(r_{2},r_{3})\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}).

On writing the left-hand side in
(\ref{6.2888})
by
\mathfrak{T}
, we notice here that

(5.32)

\mathfrak{T}=\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2}+\Sigma_{3}.

We first analyze
\Sigma_{1}.
When
r_{2}=r_{3}=0
, it follows from
(\ref{6.292929})
that

\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=p^{zN-2z}\text{gcd}(\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}),p^{z}),

since
\text{gcd}(\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}),p^{z})=p^{z}
in the case that
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}=\pm{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}.
Hence, we find that

\Sigma_{1}=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p)=1\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p)=1\end{subarray}}p^{zN-2z}\text{gcd}(\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}),p^{z}).

By introducing

\mathfrak{F}^{(e)}(p^{z})=\left\{({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\in[1,p^{r}]^{n}\times[1,p^{r}]^{n}\middle|\ \begin{aligned} &\text{gcd}(\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}),p^{z})=p^{e}\\
&({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p)=({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p)=1\end{aligned}\right\},

one finds that

\Sigma_{1}=p^{zN-2z}\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p)=1\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p)=1\end{subarray}}1+O\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq e\leq z}p^{e}\cdot\#\mathfrak{F}^{(e)}(p^{z})\biggr)\biggr).

Note that
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p)=1\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p)=1\end{subarray}}1=p^{2rn}(1+O(p^{-n})).
Furthermore, for given
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\in[1,p^{r}]^{n}
with
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p)=1
, one infers that the number of
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}\in[1,p^{r}]^{n}
such that
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\in\mathfrak{F}^{(e)}(p^{z})
is at most
p^{rn-e(n-1)}.
Thus, this gives
\#\mathfrak{F}^{(e)}(p^{z})\ll p^{2rn-e(n-1)}.
Therefore, we deduce that

(5.33)

\Sigma_{1}=p^{z(N-2)+2rn}(1+O(p^{-n+2})).

Next, we turn to estimate
\Sigma_{2}.
When
dr_{2}\leq z<dr_{3}
, we find from
(\ref{6.292929})
that

(5.34)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{aligned} &p^{z(N-1)}\cdot p^{-(z-dr_{2})}\cdot p^{z}\ \text{when}\ {\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}\neq\pm{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}\\
&p^{zN}\cdot p^{-(z-dr_{2})}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{when}\ {\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}=\pm{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2},\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle=p^{z(N-1)}\cdot p^{dr_{2}}.

Furthermore, when
z<dr_{2}\leq dr_{3}
, one finds from
(\ref{6.292929})
again that

(5.35)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{aligned} &p^{z(N-1)}\cdot p^{z}\ \text{when}\ {\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}\neq\pm{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}\\
&p^{zN}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{when}\ {\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}=\pm{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2},\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle=p^{zN}.

On noting that

\#\{({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\in[1,p^{r}]^{2n}|\ ({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}},\ ({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\}\ll(p^{r-r_{2}})^{n}\cdot(p^{r-r_{3}})^{n},

we find from
(\ref{6.323232})
and
(\ref{6.3333})
that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r\\
r_{2}\leq r_{3}\\
z<dr_{3}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})

\displaystyle\ll\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r\\
r_{2}\leq r_{3}\\
dr_{2}\leq z<dr_{3}\end{subarray}}(p^{r-r_{2}})^{n}\cdot(p^{r-r_{3}})^{n}\cdot p^{z(N-1)+dr_{2}}+\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r\\
r_{2}\leq r_{3}\\
z<dr_{2}\leq dr_{3}\end{subarray}}(p^{r-r_{2}})^{n}\cdot(p^{r-r_{3}})^{n}\cdot p^{zN}.

A modicum of calculation leads to

(5.36)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r\\
r_{2}\leq r_{3}\\
z<dr_{3}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})

\displaystyle\ll\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{3}\leq r\\
z<dr_{3}\end{subarray}}(p^{r})^{n}\cdot(p^{r-r_{3}})^{n}\cdot p^{z(N-1)}+\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{3}\leq r\\
z<dr_{3}\end{subarray}}\left(p^{r-\lceil z/d\rceil}\right)^{n}\cdot(p^{r-r_{3}})^{n}\cdot p^{zN}

\displaystyle\ll\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{3}\leq r\\
\lceil z/d\rceil\leq r_{3}\end{subarray}}(p^{z(N-1)+2rn-nr_{3}}+p^{zN+2rn-n\lceil z/d\rceil-nr_{3}})

\displaystyle\ll p^{z(N-1)+2rn-n\lceil z/d\rceil},

where we have used the inequality
z-n\lceil z/d\rceil<0
derived by the hypothesis
n>d
in the statement of Lemma
5.6
.

Consider the case that
dr_{3}\leq z.
Then, it follows from
(\ref{6.292929})
again that

\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &p^{z(N-1)}\cdot p^{-(z-dr_{2})}\cdot\mathcal{V}_{1}\ \ \text{when}\ {\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}\neq\pm{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}\\
&p^{zN}\cdot p^{-(z-dr_{2})}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{when}\ {\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}=\pm{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2},\end{aligned}\right.,

where

\mathcal{V}_{1}=p^{dr_{3}}\text{gcd}(\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}),p^{z-dr_{3}}).

On noting that
\text{gcd}(\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}),p^{z-dr_{3}})=p^{z-dr_{3}}
when
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}\pm{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2},
we see that

(5.37)

\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=p^{z(N-2)}\cdot p^{dr_{2}+dr_{3}}\cdot\text{gcd}(\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}),p^{z-dr_{3}}).

Let us temporarily define

G=\{({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\in[1,p^{r}]^{2n}|\ ({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}},\ ({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\}.

For given
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\in G
, recall the definition
(\ref{6.29292929})
of
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}
and
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}.
Then, by introducing

\mathfrak{F}^{(e)}=\left\{({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\in[1,p^{r}]^{n}\times[1,p^{r}]^{n}\middle|\ \text{gcd}(\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{f}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}),p^{z-dr_{3}})=p^{e}\right\},

we deduce from
(\ref{6.343434})
that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=p^{z(N-2)}\cdot p^{dr_{2}+dr_{3}}\cdot\biggl(|G|+O\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq e\leq z-dr_{3}}p^{e}\cdot\#\mathfrak{F}^{(e)}\biggr)\biggr).

Note that
|G|=p^{(r-r_{2})n+(r-r_{3})n}(1+O(p^{-n})).
Furthermore, for given
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\in[1,p^{r}]^{n}
with
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}
, the number of
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}\in[1,p^{r}]^{n}
with
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}
such that
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\in\mathfrak{F}^{(e)}
is at most
(p^{r-r_{2}})^{n}\cdot p^{-e(n-1)}.
Thus, this gives
\#\mathfrak{F}^{(e)}\ll p^{2rn-e(n-1)}.
Therefore, we deduce that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=p^{z(N-2)}\cdot p^{dr_{2}+dr_{3}}\cdot p^{(r-r_{2})n+(r-r_{3})n}(1+O(p^{-n+2})).

This equality yields that

(5.38)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r\\
r_{2}\leq r_{3}\\
dr_{3}\leq z\\
(r_{2},r_{3})\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}_{z}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r\\
r_{2}\leq r_{3}\\
dr_{3}\leq z\\
(r_{2},r_{3})\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}p^{z(N-2)}\cdot p^{dr_{2}+dr_{3}}\cdot p^{(r-r_{2})n+(r-r_{3})n}(1+O(p^{-n+2}))

\displaystyle\ll p^{z(N-2)+2rn+d-n}.

By adding the bounds in
(\ref{6.353535})
and
(\ref{6.363636})
and substituting that into
(\ref{6.313131})
, we find that

(5.39)

\Sigma_{2}\ll p^{z(N-2)+2rn}\left(p^{z-n\lceil z/d\rceil}+p^{d-n}\right).

Furthermore, the same argument leading from (
5.34
) and
(\ref{6.393939})
delivers that

(5.40)

\Sigma_{3}\ll p^{z(N-2)+2rn}\left(p^{z-n\lceil z/d\rceil}+p^{d-n}\right),

by reversing the roles of
r_{2}
and
r_{3}.

Therefore, on substituting
(\ref{6.333})
,
5.39
and
5.40
into
(\ref{6.32323232})
, we conclude that

\mathfrak{T}=p^{z(N-2)+2rn}(1+O(p^{d-n}+p^{z-n\lceil z/d\rceil})).

∎

In advance of the statement of the following lemma, we recall the formulation (
5.22
) of
N_{3}(p^{r})
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Whenever
n>d
, we have

p^{-2r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}T({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=p^{rN-r}(1+O(p^{d-n}+p^{r-n\lceil r/d\rceil})).

Proof.

For simplicity, we write

(5.41)

\mathfrak{T}=p^{-2r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}T({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}).

On recalling the definition of
T({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})
leading to
(\ref{5.19})
, we see that

(5.42)

\mathfrak{T}=p^{-2rn-r}\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq p^{r}}\mathcal{T}_{1}({\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}),

where

\mathcal{T}_{1}({\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq l_{2},l_{3}\leq p^{r}}e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1})\rangle l_{2}}{p^{r}}\biggr)\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\rangle l_{3}}{p^{r}}\biggr).

It follows by orthogonality that for given
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}
and
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}
with
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}
and
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}
, one has

(5.43)

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq p^{r}}\mathcal{T}_{1}({\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=p^{2r}\#\left\{1\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq p^{r}\middle|\begin{aligned} \langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{i})\rangle\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\ (i=1,2)\end{aligned}\right\}.

Notice here that
\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq p^{r}}\mathcal{T}_{1}({\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=p^{2r}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{r}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})
, where
\mathcal{W}_{r}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})
is defined in the preamble to Lemma
5.6
.

Therefore, by substituting
(\ref{6.28})
into
(\ref{5.44})
and by applying Lemma
5.6
with
z=r
, we see that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}

\displaystyle=p^{-2rn+r}\cdot p^{r(N-2)+2rn}(1+O(p^{d-n}+p^{r-n\lceil r/d\rceil}))

\displaystyle=p^{rN-r}(1+O(p^{d-n}+p^{r-n\lceil r/d\rceil})).

This completes the proof of Lemma
\ref{lem5.6}.

∎

In advance of the statement of the following lemma, we recall the formulation (
5.22
) of
N_{3}(p^{r})
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Whenever
n>d
and
N>(k-1)2^{k-1}(n-d+3)
, one has

p^{-2r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}S({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\ll p^{rN-r-(N/(2^{k-1}(k-1))-3)+\epsilon}.

Proof.

On recalling that
\gcd(l_{1},p^{r})=p^{r-r_{1}}
, let us write
l_{1}=p^{r-r_{1}}\widetilde{l}_{1}
with
\gcd(\widetilde{l}_{1},p)=1.
Then, on recalling the definition
(\ref{6.2121})
of
\Xi({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},l_{1},l_{2},l_{3})
and by writing
{\boldsymbol{a}}=p^{r_{1}}{\boldsymbol{n}}+{\boldsymbol{m}}
with
0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-r_{1}}-1
and
1\leq{\boldsymbol{m}}\leq p^{r_{1}}
, we see that

\displaystyle\Xi({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},l_{1},l_{2},l_{3})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-r_{1}}-1}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{m}}\leq p^{r_{1}}}e\biggl(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{m}})\widetilde{l}_{1}}{p^{r_{1}}}\biggr)e\biggl(\frac{\langle p^{r_{1}}{\boldsymbol{n}}+{\boldsymbol{m}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1})\rangle l_{2}+\langle p^{r_{1}}{\boldsymbol{n}}+{\boldsymbol{m}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\rangle l_{3}}{p^{r}}\biggr).

On recalling (
5.22
) and
(\ref{6.2323})
and by substituting this expression for
\Xi({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},l_{1},l_{2},l_{3})
into
S({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})
, it follows by the triangle inequality that

(5.44)

\displaystyle p^{-2r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}S({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq p^{-2rn-r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r_{1}\leq r\\
0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\widetilde{l}_{1}\leq p^{r_{1}}\\
(\widetilde{l}_{1},p)=1\end{subarray}}\bigl|S_{1}(\widetilde{l}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\bigr|\cdot\bigl|S_{2}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\bigr|,

where

\displaystyle S_{1}(\widetilde{l}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})

\displaystyle=\sup_{1\leq l_{2},l_{3}\leq p^{r}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{m}}\leq p^{r_{1}}}e\biggl(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{m}})\widetilde{l}_{1}}{p^{r_{1}}}\biggr)e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{m}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1})\rangle l_{2}}{p^{r}}\biggr)e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{m}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\rangle l_{3}}{p^{r}}\biggr)\biggr|

and

S_{2}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq l_{2},l_{3}\leq p^{r}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-r_{1}}-1}e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{n}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1})\rangle l_{2}}{p^{r-r_{1}}}\biggr)e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{n}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\rangle l_{3}}{p^{r-r_{1}}}\biggr)\biggr|.

First, by the same explanation leading to
(\ref{5.55.5})
, one has

(5.45)

S_{1}(\widetilde{l}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\ll p^{Nr_{1}-Nr_{1}/(2^{k-1}(k-1))+\epsilon}.

Next, we analyse
S_{2}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}).
We infer from orthogonality that the inner sum of
S_{2}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})
is a non-negative integer. Thus, by changing the order of summations, we have

S_{2}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-r_{1}}-1}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq l_{2},l_{3}\leq p^{r}}e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{n}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1})\rangle l_{2}}{p^{r-r_{1}}}\biggr)e\biggl(\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{n}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})\rangle l_{3}}{p^{r-r_{1}}}\biggr).

Hence, by orthogonality, we find that

(5.46)

S_{2}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=p^{2r}\#\left\{0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-r_{1}}-1\middle|\begin{aligned} \langle{\boldsymbol{n}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{i})\rangle\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r-r_{1}}\ (i=1,2)\end{aligned}\right\}.

Notice here that
S_{2}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=p^{2r}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{r-r_{1}}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})
, where
\mathcal{W}_{r-r_{1}}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})
is defined in the preamble to Lemma
\ref{lem6.6}.

Therefore, we deduce by applying Lemma
5.6
with
z=r-r_{1}
that

(5.47)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}S_{2}({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})

\displaystyle=p^{2r}\cdot p^{(r-r_{1})(N-2)+2rn}\left(1+O\left(p^{d-n}+p^{r-r_{1}-n\lceil(r-r_{1})/d\rceil}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\ll p^{2r}\cdot p^{(r-r_{1})(N-2)+2rn}=p^{(r-r_{1})N+2rn+2r_{1}},

where we have used the inequality
r-r_{1}-n\lceil(r-r_{1})/d\rceil<0
derived from the hypothesis
n>d.

By substituting (
5.45
) and
(\ref{6.47})
into
(\ref{5.20})
and by recalling the hypothesis
N>(k-1)2^{k-1}(n-d+3)
, we conclude that

\displaystyle p^{-2r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq r_{2},r_{3}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},p^{r})=p^{r_{3}}\end{subarray}}S({\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq p^{-2rn-r}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r_{1}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\widetilde{l_{1}}\leq p^{r_{1}}\\
(\widetilde{l_{1}},p)=1\end{subarray}}p^{Nr_{1}-Nr_{1}/(2^{k-1}(k-1))+\epsilon}\cdot p^{(r-r_{1})N+2rn+2r_{1}}

\displaystyle\ll p^{rN-r-(N/(2^{k-1}(k-1))-3)+\epsilon}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
\ref{lem5.5}.
∎

We now provide the proof of Lemma
5.4
by making use of the method described at the beginning of section
5.1.2
, following the statement of Lemma
\ref{lem5.3}.

Proof of Lemma
5.4
.

Recall (
5.18
), that is

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}(\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})-1)^{2}=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}(\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})^{2}-2\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})+1).

On substituting the bounds from Lemma
5.7
and Lemma
5.8
into
(\ref{5.19}),
it follows that
whenever
N>(k-1)2^{k-1}(n-d+3)
, one has

(5.48)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})^{2}=p^{rN-r}\bigl(1+O\bigl(p^{d-n}+p^{r-n\lceil r/d\rceil}\bigr)\bigr).

Therefore, by adding
(\ref{5.57})
and the expressions obtained in Lemma
5.2
and Lemma
5.3
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}(\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})-1)^{2}=O(p^{rN-n\lceil r/d\rceil}+p^{rN-r+d-n}).

This completes the proof of Lemma
\ref{lem5.3}.

∎

5.2.
Singular series treatment II

In this section, we provide an upper bound for
\#A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})
, and give a lower bound for
\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})
with
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r}).
Furthermore, combining all the estimates obtained in sections
5.1
and
5.2
together with the strategy used in [
\ref{ref3},
section 5], we shall prove Proposition
5.1
at the end of this section.

5.2.1.
The proof of Lemma
5.9

We recall the definition of the condition
\mathcal{C}_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})
with
0\leq e\leq r-v
in the preamble to the definition
(\ref{6.59590})
of
A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})
, that is

\displaystyle A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})=\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\middle|\ {\boldsymbol{a}}\ \text{satisfies the condition}\ \mathcal{C}_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})\right\}.

Recall that
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N
variables of degree
k.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
d\geq 3.
Let
p
be a prime number and
r\geq 1.
For
e\in\{0,\ldots,r\}
, whenever
N>(k-1)2^{k-1}(n+3)
we have

\#A^{(e)}(p^{r})\ll p^{rN-r-e}.

We shall prove Lemma
5.9
at the end of section
\ref{6.1.4}.
In order to prove this lemma, we require an auxiliary lemma. In advance of describing this auxiliary lemma, it is convenient to define

M_{1}(r,v,h;k)=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{b}}\in[1,p^{r-v}]^{N}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r-kv}\end{subarray}}p^{-h}\cdot\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{h})\middle|\begin{aligned} (\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))\equiv\boldsymbol{0}\ \text{mod}\ p^{h}\end{aligned}\right\},

with
r,v,h\geq 0,

r-v\geq h
and
r-kv\geq 0.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}},
and let
r,v,h
be non-negative integers with
r-v\geq h
and
r-kv\geq 0
. Then, whenever
N>(k-1)2^{k-1}(n+3)
, one has

(5.49)

M_{1}(r,v,h;k)\ll p^{-h}\cdot p^{kv-r}\cdot p^{(r-v)N}.

Proof.

By orthogonality, one finds that

(5.50)

\displaystyle M_{1}(r,v,h;k)=p^{-(n+2)h-r}p^{kv}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{h})}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq l_{1}\leq p^{r-kv}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{l}}\leq p^{h}\\
{\boldsymbol{l}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n+1}\end{subarray}}\Xi({\boldsymbol{x}},l_{1},{\boldsymbol{l}}),

where

\Xi({\boldsymbol{x}},l_{1},{\boldsymbol{l}})=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r-v}}e\left(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{b}})l_{1}}{p^{r-kv}}\right)e\left(\frac{\langle(\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})),{\boldsymbol{l}}\rangle}{p^{h}}\right).

Furthermore, on splitting the sum over
l_{1}
in
(\ref{6.60})
in terms of values of
(l_{1},p^{r-kv})
, we see that

(5.51)

M_{1}(r,v,h;k)=p^{-(n+2)h-r}p^{kv}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{h})}(S({\boldsymbol{x}})+T({\boldsymbol{x}})),

where

S({\boldsymbol{x}})=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r_{1}\leq r-kv}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq l_{1}\leq p^{r-kv}\\
(l_{1},p^{r-kv})=p^{r-kv-r_{1}}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{l}}\leq p^{h}\\
{\boldsymbol{l}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n+1}\end{subarray}}\Xi({\boldsymbol{x}},l_{1},{\boldsymbol{l}})\ \text{and}\ T({\boldsymbol{x}})=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{l}}\leq p^{h}\\
{\boldsymbol{l}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n+1}\end{subarray}}\Xi({\boldsymbol{x}},p^{r-kv},{\boldsymbol{l}}).

We first analyze
S({\boldsymbol{x}})
. For fixed
r_{1}
with
p^{r-kv-r_{1}}=(l_{1},p^{r-kv})
, let us write
l_{1}=p^{r-kv-r_{1}}\widetilde{l}_{1}.
Then, on writing
{\boldsymbol{b}}=p^{r_{1}}{\boldsymbol{n}}+{\boldsymbol{m}}
with
0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-v-r_{1}}-1
and
1\leq{\boldsymbol{m}}\leq p^{r_{1}}
, we see that

\displaystyle\Xi({\boldsymbol{x}},l_{1},{\boldsymbol{l}})=\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-v-r_{1}}-1}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{m}}\leq p^{r_{1}}}e\left(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{m}})\widetilde{l}_{1}}{p^{r_{1}}}\right)e\left(\frac{\langle(\nabla f_{p^{r_{1}}{\boldsymbol{n}}+{\boldsymbol{m}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{p^{r_{1}}{\boldsymbol{n}}+{\boldsymbol{m}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})),{\boldsymbol{l}}\rangle}{p^{h}}\right).

On substituting this expression into
S({\boldsymbol{x}})
and from that into
(\ref{6.61})
, it follows by the triangle inequality that

(5.52)

\displaystyle p^{-(n+2)h-r}p^{kv}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{h})}S({\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle\leq p^{-(n+2)h-r}p^{kv}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r_{1}\leq r-kv\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{h})}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq l_{1}\leq p^{r-kv}\\
(l_{1},p^{r-kv})=p^{r-kv-r_{1}}\end{subarray}}|S_{1}(\widetilde{l}_{1},{\boldsymbol{x}})|\cdot\left|S_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}})\right|,

where

S_{1}(\widetilde{l}_{1},{\boldsymbol{x}})=\sup_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{l}}\leq p^{h}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{m}}\leq p^{r_{1}}}e\left(\frac{P({\boldsymbol{m}})\widetilde{l}_{1}}{p^{r_{1}}}\right)e\left(\frac{\langle(\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{m}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{{\boldsymbol{m}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})),{\boldsymbol{l}}\rangle}{p^{h}}\right)\biggr|

and

S_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{l}}\leq p^{h}}\biggl|\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-v-r_{1}}-1}e\left(\frac{\langle(\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{n}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{{\boldsymbol{n}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})),{\boldsymbol{l}}\rangle}{p^{h-r_{1}}}\right)\biggr|.

First, by the same explanation leading to
(\ref{5.55.5})
, we have

(5.53)

S_{1}(\widetilde{l}_{1},{\boldsymbol{x}})\ll p^{Nr_{1}-Nr_{1}/(2^{k-1}(k-1))+\epsilon}.

Next, we turn to estimate the sum
S_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}}).
We first consider the case
h-r_{1}>0.
Since
r-v\geq h,
we infer from orthogonality that the inner sum in
S_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}})
is a non-negative integer. Thus, by changing the order of summations, we see that

S_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-v-r_{1}}-1}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq{\boldsymbol{l}}\leq p^{h}}e\left(\frac{\langle(\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{n}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{{\boldsymbol{n}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})),{\boldsymbol{l}}\rangle}{p^{h-r_{1}}}\right).

Then, we find by orthogonality that

\displaystyle S_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle=p^{h(n+1)}\cdot\#\left\{0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{r-v-r_{1}}-1\middle|\ \begin{aligned} (\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{n}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{{\boldsymbol{n}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))\equiv{\bf 0}\ \text{mod}\ p^{h-r_{1}}\end{aligned}\right\}

\displaystyle=p^{h(n+1)+(r-v-h)N}\cdot\#\left\{0\leq{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq p^{h-r_{1}}-1\middle|\ \begin{aligned} (\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{n}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{{\boldsymbol{n}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))\equiv{\bf 0}\ \text{mod}\ p^{h-r_{1}}\end{aligned}\right\}.

By splitting this expression for
S_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}})
in terms of values of
({\boldsymbol{n}},p^{h-r_{1}})
, we see that

(5.54)

S_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}})=p^{h(n+1)+(r-v-h)N}\cdot\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq g\leq h-r_{1}}S^{g}({\boldsymbol{x}}),

where

S^{g}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{n}}\in\mathcal{R}_{N}(p^{h-r_{1}-g})\middle|\ \begin{aligned} (\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{n}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{{\boldsymbol{n}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))\equiv{\bf 0}\ \text{mod}\ p^{h-r_{1}-g}\end{aligned}\right\}.

By applying Lemma
2.5
with
h-r_{1}-g
in place of
r
and
e
, one obtains

(5.55)

S^{g}({\boldsymbol{x}})\ll p^{(h-r_{1}-g)(N-n)}.

Hence, on substituting (
5.55
) into
(\ref{6.6565})
, it follows that when
h-r_{1}>0
, one has

(5.56)

S_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}})\ll p^{h(n+1)+(r-v-h)N}\cdot p^{(h-r_{1})(N-n)}\ll p^{(r-v-r_{1})N+r_{1}n+h}.

Next, we consider the case
h-r_{1}\leq 0.
Then, it follows by the trivial bound that

(5.57)

S_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}})=p^{(n+1)h}\cdot p^{(r-v-r_{1})N}.

Meanwhile, it follows from
(\ref{6.6262})
that

(5.58)

\displaystyle p^{-(n+2)h-r}\cdot p^{kv}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{h})}S({\boldsymbol{x}})\ll X_{1}+X_{2},

where

X_{1}=p^{-(n+2)h-r}\cdot p^{kv}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r_{1}<h\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{h})}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq l_{1}\leq p^{r-kv}\\
(l_{1},p^{r-kv})=p^{r-kv-r_{1}}\end{subarray}}|S_{1}(\widetilde{l}_{1},{\boldsymbol{x}})|\cdot\left|S_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}})\right|

and

X_{2}=p^{-(n+2)h-r}\cdot p^{kv}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\leq r_{1}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{h})}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq l_{1}\leq p^{r-kv}\\
(l_{1},p^{r-kv})=p^{r-kv-r_{1}}\end{subarray}}|S_{1}(\widetilde{l}_{1},{\boldsymbol{x}})|\cdot\left|S_{2}({\boldsymbol{x}})\right|

Hence, on substituting the bound
(\ref{6.6767})
into
X_{1}
and the quantity
(\ref{6.666})
into
X_{2}
together with the bound
(\ref{6.6363})
for
S_{1}(\widetilde{l}_{1},{\boldsymbol{x}})
, it follows that whenever
N>(k-1)2^{k-1}(n+3)
, one has

\displaystyle X_{1}

\displaystyle\ll p^{-(n+2)h-r}\cdot p^{kv}\cdot p^{hn}\cdot p^{(r-v)N+h}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r_{1}<h}p^{r_{1}-Nr_{1}/(2^{k-1}(k-1))+r_{1}n+\epsilon}

\displaystyle\ll p^{-h-1}\cdot p^{kv-r}\cdot p^{(r-v)N},

and

\displaystyle X_{2}

\displaystyle\ll p^{-(n+2)h-r}\cdot p^{kv}\cdot p^{hn}\cdot p^{(r-v)N+(n+1)h}\displaystyle\sum_{h\leq r_{1}}p^{r_{1}-Nr_{1}/(2^{k-1}(k-1))+\epsilon}

\displaystyle\ll p^{-h-1}\cdot p^{kv-r}\cdot p^{(r-v)N}.

Thus, it follows from
(\ref{6.68})
together with these estimates for
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
that one has

(5.59)

p^{-(n+2)h-r}\cdot p^{kv}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{h})}S({\boldsymbol{x}})\ll p^{-h-1}\cdot p^{kv-r}\cdot p^{(r-v)N}.

As the endgame, we turn to estimate the remaining quantity in (
5.51
), that is

(5.60)

p^{-(n+2)h-r}p^{kv}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{h})}T({\boldsymbol{x}}).

It follows from orthogonality that one has

\displaystyle T({\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle=p^{h(n+1)}\cdot\#\left\{1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r-v}\middle|\ \begin{aligned} (\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))\equiv\boldsymbol{0}\ \text{mod}\ p^{h}\end{aligned}\right\}

\displaystyle=p^{h(n+1)+(r-v-h)N}\cdot\#\left\{1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{h}\middle|\ \begin{aligned} (\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))\equiv\boldsymbol{0}\ \text{mod}\ p^{h}\end{aligned}\right\}.

By splitting this expression for
T({\boldsymbol{x}})
in terms of values of
({\boldsymbol{b}},p^{h})
, we see that

(5.61)

T({\boldsymbol{x}})=p^{h(n+1)+(r-v-h)N}\cdot\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq g\leq h}T^{g}({\boldsymbol{x}}),

where

T^{g}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{b}}\in\mathcal{R}_{N}(p^{h-g})\middle|\ \begin{aligned} (\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))\equiv\boldsymbol{0}\ \text{mod}\ p^{h-g}\end{aligned}\right\}.

By applying Lemma
2.5
with
h-g
in place of
r
and
e
, one obtains

(5.62)

T^{g}({\boldsymbol{x}})\ll p^{(h-g)(N-n)}.

Therefore, on substituting
(\ref{6.83})
into
(\ref{6.82})
and that into (
5.60
), one concludes that

(5.63)

\displaystyle p^{-(n+2)h-r}p^{kv}\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{h})}T({\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle\ll p^{-(n+2)h-r}\cdot p^{kv}\cdot p^{hn}\cdot p^{h(n+1)+(r-v-h)N}\cdot p^{h(N-n)}

\displaystyle\ll p^{-h}\cdot p^{kv-r}\cdot p^{(r-v)N}.

Thus, on substituting
(\ref{6.68})
and
(\ref{6.86})
into
(\ref{6.61})
, we conclude that

\displaystyle M_{1}(r,v,h;k)

\displaystyle\ll p^{-h}\cdot p^{kv-r}\cdot p^{(r-v)N}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
\ref{lem6.96.9}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
5.9
.

We recall the definition of the condition
\mathcal{C}_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})
with
0\leq e\leq r-v
in the preamble to the definition
(\ref{6.59590})
of
A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})
, that is

\displaystyle A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})=\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\middle|\ {\boldsymbol{a}}\ \text{satisfies the condition}\ \mathcal{C}_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})\right\}.

For
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}
with
p^{v}|{\boldsymbol{a}}
, we define

(5.64)

\mathcal{D}_{p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}}(p^{h})=\left\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{h})\middle|\ \begin{aligned} (\nabla f_{p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))\equiv\boldsymbol{0}\ \text{mod}\ p^{h}\end{aligned}\right\}.

Observe that given
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{D}_{p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}}(p^{h})
, we see that for any
g\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p^{h}{\mathbb{Z}}
with
(g,p)=1
we have
g{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{D}_{p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}}(p^{h}).

First, we consider the case
r-kv\leq 0
. By our assumptions concerning
e
and
v
in the definition of
A^{(e)}_{v}(p^{r})
we have
r-v\geq e
. Thus, one finds that

(5.65)

\displaystyle\#A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})

\displaystyle\leq\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\middle|\ \begin{aligned} &\mathcal{D}_{p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}}(p^{e})\neq\emptyset\\
&P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
&p^{v}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\end{aligned}\right\}.

Hence, we deduce from
(\ref{6.7878})
and the observation following the definition
(\ref{6.888})
of
\mathcal{D}_{p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}}(p^{h})
that

(5.66)

\displaystyle\#A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{N}\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
p^{v}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{p^{e}(1-p^{-1})}\cdot\#(\mathcal{D}_{p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}}(p^{e})).

Recall that
P
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
k
and that
r-kv\leq 0
. Then, from the condition
p^{v}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}
, we may drop the condition
P({\boldsymbol{a}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}
in (
5.66
). Hence, we see from
(\ref{6.899})
together with the substitution
{\boldsymbol{b}}=p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}
that

\#A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})\ll\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{b}}\in[1,p^{r-v}]^{N}\end{subarray}}p^{-e}\cdot\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{e})\middle|\begin{aligned} (\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))\equiv\boldsymbol{0}\ \text{mod}\ p^{e}\end{aligned}\right\}.

By changing the order of summations, we have

(5.67)

\#A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})\ll\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{e})\end{subarray}}p^{-e}\cdot\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{b}}\in[1,p^{r-v}]^{N}\middle|\begin{aligned} (\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))\equiv\boldsymbol{0}\ \text{mod}\ p^{e}\end{aligned}\right\}.

Then, by the same argument leading from
(\ref{6.82})
to
(\ref{6.86})
, it readily follows by Lemma
2.5
with
r-v
in place of
r
that

(5.68)

\#A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})\ll\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{e})\end{subarray}}p^{-e}\cdot p^{(r-v)(N-n)}\cdot p^{(r-v-e)n}\ll p^{(r-v)N-e}\ll p^{rN-r-e},

where we have used the inequalities
\#\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{e})\leq p^{en}
and
vN\geq r
stemming from
v\geq r/k.

Next, we consider the case
r-kv>0
. We obtain again
(\ref{6.7878})
and
(\ref{6.899})
. Hence, we find from
(\ref{6.899})
together with a substitution
{\boldsymbol{b}}=p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}
that

\#A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})\ll\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{b}}\in[1,p^{r-v}]^{N}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r-kv}\end{subarray}}p^{-e}\cdot\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{e})\middle|\begin{aligned} (\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))\equiv\boldsymbol{0}\ \text{mod}\ p^{e}\end{aligned}\right\}.

Therefore, by applying Lemma
5.10
with
e
in place of
h
, we have

(5.69)

\#A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})\ll p^{-e}\cdot p^{kv-r}\cdot p^{(r-v)N}\ll p^{rN-r-e}.

Combining both the bounds
(\ref{6.777})
and
(\ref{6.8383})
, we conclude that

\#A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})\ll p^{rN-r-e}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
\ref{lem6.8}.

∎

5.2.2.
The proof of Lemma
5.11

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
d\geq 3
. Let
p
be a prime number and
r\geq 1.
For
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})
with
e\in\{0,\ldots,r-v\}
, we have

\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})\geq p^{v-(e+1)(n-1)}.

Proof.

When
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r}),
one has

(5.70)

\displaystyle\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})

\displaystyle=p^{-r(n-1)}\cdot\#\{{\boldsymbol{g}}\in[1,p^{r}]^{n}|\ \langle{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\}

\displaystyle=p^{-r(n-1)}\cdot p^{vn}\cdot\#\{{\boldsymbol{g}}\in[1,p^{r-v}]^{n}|\ \langle p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r-v}\}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{p^{v}}{p^{(r-v)n-(r-v)}}\cdot\#\{{\boldsymbol{g}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{r-v})|\ \langle p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r-v}\}.

Since
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r}),
there exists
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{r-v})
such that

(5.71)

v_{p^{r-v}}(\nabla f_{p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}))=e\ \text{and}\ f_{p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r-v},

with
p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{R}_{N}(p^{r-v}).
Thus, it follows by Lemma
2.4
with
p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
r-v
in place of
{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
r
that we obtain the lower bound

\frac{p^{v}}{p^{(r-v)n-(r-v)}}\cdot\#\{{\boldsymbol{g}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{r-v})|\ \langle p^{-v}{\boldsymbol{a}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r-v}\}\geq\frac{p^{v}}{p^{(e+1)(n-1)}}.

Hence, we conclude that

\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};p^{r})\geq p^{v-(e+1)(n-1)}.

∎

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Note that a modicum of computation reveals that the condition on
N=N_{d,n}\geq 1000n^{2}8^{k}
satisfies conditions on
N
in Lemma
5.2
, Lemma
5.3
, Lemma
5.4
, Lemma
5.9
, and Theorem
2.8
, and thus these lemmas are available in this proof.

For given
{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N},
we temporarily define
\mathbb{B}({\boldsymbol{b}})
to be the set consisting of all prime powers
p^{r}
having the property that there exists
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(p^{r}/(p^{r},{\boldsymbol{b}}))
such that
f_{{\boldsymbol{b}}/(p^{r},{\boldsymbol{b}})}({\boldsymbol{x}})\equiv 0
mod
p^{r}/(p^{r},{\boldsymbol{b}}).
Then, we define a set
\mathbb{F}^{loc}_{d,n}(Q)
to be the set consisting of
{\boldsymbol{b}}\in[1,Q]^{N}
such that for all
p^{r}\|Q
, one has
p^{r}\in\mathbb{B}({\boldsymbol{b}}).

For simplicity, we write

\mathcal{S}(A)=A^{-N+k}\cdot\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(A;P)\middle|\ \sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};W)\leq(\log A)^{-\eta}\right\}.

Note that
\sigma({\boldsymbol{a}};W)=\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};W)
for
{\boldsymbol{a}}\equiv{\boldsymbol{b}}\ \text{mod}\ W.
Then, one infers that

(5.72)

\mathcal{S}(A)=A^{-N+k}\cdot\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{b}}\in\mathbb{F}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}(W)\\
\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};W)\leq(\log A)^{-\eta}\end{subarray}}\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}(A;P)\middle|\ {\boldsymbol{a}}\equiv{\boldsymbol{b}}\ \text{mod}\ W\right\}.

Since the relations
{\boldsymbol{a}}\equiv{\boldsymbol{b}}\ \text{mod}\ W
and
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0
together imply that
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W,
we observe that the summands
{\boldsymbol{b}}
in
(\ref{6.79})
are additionally restricted to satisfy
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W
. With this observation in mind, we deduce by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

(5.73)

\mathcal{S}(A)\leq A^{-N+k}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{1}^{1/2}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{2}^{1/2},

where

\mathcal{S}_{1}=\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(W)\middle|\ \begin{aligned} \sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};W)\leq(\log A)^{-\eta},\ P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W\end{aligned}\right\}

and

\mathcal{S}_{2}=\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{b}}\in[1,W]^{N}}\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}(A;P)\middle|\ {\boldsymbol{a}}\equiv{\boldsymbol{b}}\ \text{mod}\ W\right\}^{2}.

Note that

\mathcal{S}_{2}\leq\{{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{a}}_{2}\in[-A,A]^{N}|\ P({\boldsymbol{a}}_{1})=P({\boldsymbol{a}}_{2})=0,\ {\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}\equiv{\boldsymbol{a}}_{2}\ \text{mod}\ W\}.

Recall that
W\leq X^{2}
and that we have the hypothesis
X^{3}\leq A
in the statement of Proposition
5.1
, and thus
W\leq A^{2/3}.
Then, since
N\geq 18k(k-1)4^{k+3},
on noting that the upper bound for
\mathcal{N}(A)
in Theorem
2.8
is valid without the condition that
P({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
has a nontrivial integer solution, we find by applying Theorem
2.8
that

(5.74)

\mathcal{S}_{2}\ll A^{2N-2k}W^{1-N}.

We now analyse
\mathcal{S}_{1}
. Since
\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};W)>0
, we deduce that for any
\kappa>0
one has

\mathcal{S}_{1}\ll\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(W)\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{1}{(\log A)^{\eta}\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};W)}\right)^{\kappa}.

Thus, we find from (
1.9
) that

\mathcal{S}_{1}\ll\frac{1}{(\log A)^{\eta\cdot\kappa}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(W)\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ W\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\prod_{p^{r}\|W}\frac{1}{\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})^{\kappa}}.

Furthermore, an application of the Chinese remainder theorem delivers that

\mathcal{S}_{1}\ll\frac{1}{(\log A)^{\eta\cdot\kappa}}\displaystyle\prod_{p^{r}\|W}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(p^{r})\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})^{\kappa}}.

We investigate the sum over
{\boldsymbol{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(p^{r})
with
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}
by dividing this sum in terms of size of
\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r}).
Write

\mathcal{S}_{>}^{(\kappa)}(p^{r})=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(p^{r})\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})>1/2\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})^{\kappa}}\ \text{and}\ \mathcal{S}_{\leq}^{(\kappa)}(p^{r})=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(p^{r})\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})\leq 1/2\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})^{\kappa}},

so that

(5.75)

\mathcal{S}_{1}\ll\frac{1}{(\log A)^{\eta\cdot\kappa}}\displaystyle\prod_{p^{r}\|W}\left(\mathcal{S}_{>}^{(\kappa)}(p^{r})+\mathcal{S}_{\leq}^{(\kappa)}(p^{r})\right).

We first analyse the sum
\mathcal{S}_{>}^{(\kappa)}(p^{r}).
In order to do this, we use the estimate

\frac{1}{\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})^{\kappa}}=1-\kappa(\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})-1)+O((\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})-1)^{2}),

where the implied constant depends at most on
\kappa.
Then, we find that

(5.76)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{>}^{(\kappa)}(p^{r})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(p^{r})\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})>1/2\end{subarray}}1-\kappa\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(p^{r})\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})>1/2\end{subarray}}(\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})-1)+O\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}(\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})-1)^{2}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}1-\kappa\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(p^{r})\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}(\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})-1)+O\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}(\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})-1)^{2}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}1-\kappa\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}(\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})-1)+O\biggl(\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}(\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})-1)^{2}\biggr)+\kappa\cdot\mathcal{E},

where

\mathcal{E}=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
{\boldsymbol{b}}\notin\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(p^{r})\end{subarray}}\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r}).

By splitting the sum
\mathcal{E}
in terms of values of
({\boldsymbol{b}},p^{r}),
we see that

(5.77)

\mathcal{E}\leq\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
{\boldsymbol{b}}\notin\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(p^{r})\\
({\boldsymbol{b}},p^{r})=1\end{subarray}}\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})+\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq d_{0}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{b}},p^{r})=p^{d_{0}}\end{subarray}}\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r}).

We analyze the second term in
(\ref{6.838383})
. Recall the definition (
1.8
) of
\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})
. Also, when
({\boldsymbol{b}},p^{r})=p^{d_{0}}
, we write
\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{b}}}=p^{-d_{0}}{\boldsymbol{b}}.
Then, in the case
d_{0}\leq r/k,
one has

\displaystyle\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})

\displaystyle=p^{-r(n-1)}\#\{1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}|\ \langle\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{b}}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r-d_{0}}\}

\displaystyle\leq p^{-r(n-1)}\cdot p^{kd_{0}n}\#\{1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r-kd_{0}}|\ \langle\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{b}}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r-kd_{0}}\}

\displaystyle=p^{kd_{0}}\sigma(\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{b}}},p^{r-kd_{0}}).

Hence, we deduce that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq d_{0}\leq r/k}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{b}},p^{r})=p^{d_{0}}\end{subarray}}\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq d_{0}\leq r/k}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\leq p^{r-d_{0}}\\
P(\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{b}}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r-kd_{0}}\end{subarray}}p^{kd_{0}}\sigma(\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{b}}},p^{r-kd_{0}})

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq d_{0}\leq r/k}p^{kd_{0}}\cdot p^{d_{0}(k-1)N}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\leq p^{r-kd_{0}}\\
P(\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{b}}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r-kd_{0}}\end{subarray}}\sigma(\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{b}}},p^{r-kd_{0}}).

Then, by applying Lemma
5.3
with
r-kd_{0}
in place of
r,
we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq d_{0}\leq r/k}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{b}},p^{r})=p^{d_{0}}\end{subarray}}\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})\leq\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq d_{0}\leq r/k}p^{kd_{0}+{d_{0}(k-1)N}}\cdot(p^{(r-kd_{0})(N-1)}+O(E)),

where

E=p^{(r-kd_{0})N-\lceil(r-kd_{0})/d\rceil n}+p^{(r-kd_{0})N-(r-kd_{0})-n+d}.

A modicum of computation delivers that

(5.78)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq d_{0}\leq r/k}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{b}},p^{r})=p^{d_{0}}\end{subarray}}\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})

\displaystyle\leq p^{r(N-1)+2k-N}+O(p^{rN-\lceil(r-k)/d\rceil n+k-N}+p^{rN-r-n+d-N+2k}).

In the case
d_{0}>r/k,
it follows by the trivial bound
\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})\leq p^{r}
that

(5.79)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{d_{0}>r/k}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{b}},p^{r})=p^{d_{0}}\end{subarray}}\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})\leq\displaystyle\sum_{d_{0}>r/k}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{b}},p^{r})=p^{d_{0}}\end{subarray}}p^{r}\leq\displaystyle\sum_{d_{0}>r/k}p^{(r-d_{0})N+r}\leq p^{(r-r/k)N+r}.

Hence, it follows by
(\ref{6.84})
and
(\ref{6.85})
that

(5.80)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq d_{0}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{b}},p^{r})=p^{d_{0}}\end{subarray}}\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})\ll p^{r(N-1)+2k-N}+p^{rN-\lceil(r-k)/d\rceil n+k-N}+p^{(r-r/k)N+r}.

We turn to estimate the first term in
(\ref{6.838383}).
Recall the definition of
\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(p^{r})
at the beginning of this proof. Then, for
{\boldsymbol{b}}\notin\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(p^{r})
and
({\boldsymbol{b}},p^{r})=1
, one has

(5.81)

\displaystyle\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})

\displaystyle=p^{-r(n-1)}\#\{1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}|\ \langle{\boldsymbol{b}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\}

\displaystyle=p^{-r(n-1)}\#\{1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}|\ p|{\boldsymbol{g}},\ \langle{\boldsymbol{b}},\nu_{d,n}({\boldsymbol{g}})\rangle\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\}.

Hence, for
{\boldsymbol{b}}\notin\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(p^{r})
and
({\boldsymbol{b}},p^{r})=1
, it follows by the argument leading from
(\ref{eqeq5.1})
to
(\ref{eqeqeq5.3})
that

(5.82)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
{\boldsymbol{b}}\notin\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(p^{r})\\
({\boldsymbol{b}},p^{r})=1\end{subarray}}\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})\leq p^{-r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r_{2}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}(S({\boldsymbol{g}})+T({\boldsymbol{g}})),

where
S({\boldsymbol{g}})
and
T({\boldsymbol{g}})
are defined in the sequel to
(\ref{eqeqeq5.3})
. By applying the same argument leading from
(\ref{5.45.4})
to
(\ref{6.96.96.9})
together with the hypotheses on
N,n,
and
d
, we readily deduce that

(5.83)

p^{-r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r_{2}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}S({\boldsymbol{g}})\ll p^{rN-r-2}.

Moreover, by applying the argument leading from
(\ref{5.11})
to
(\ref{5.15})
with the range
1\leq r_{2}\leq r
in place of
0\leq r_{2}\leq r
, one infers that

(5.84)

\displaystyle p^{-r(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r_{2}\leq r}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{g}}\leq p^{r}\\
({\boldsymbol{g}},p^{r})=p^{r_{2}}\end{subarray}}T({\boldsymbol{g}})=p^{-r(n+1)}(U_{1}+U_{2})\ll p^{rN-\lceil r/d\rceil n}+p^{r(N-1)+d-n},

where
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
are defined in the sequel to
(\ref{5.11})
with the range
1\leq r_{2}\leq r
in place of
0\leq r_{2}\leq r
and we have used inequalities

p^{-r(n+1)}U_{1}\ll p^{rN-\lceil r/d\rceil n}\ \text{and}\ p^{-r(n+1)}U_{2}\ll p^{r(N-1)+d-n}.

By substituting
(\ref{6.886.88})
and
(\ref{6.896.89})
into
(\ref{6.876.87})
and by the hypothesis
n>d+1
in the statement of Proposition
5.1
, we find that

(5.85)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
{\boldsymbol{b}}\notin\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(p^{r})\\
({\boldsymbol{b}},p^{r})=1\end{subarray}}\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})\ll p^{rN-r-2}.

Hence, on substituting (
5.80
) and
(\ref{6.92})
into (
5.77
), we obtain

(5.86)

\mathcal{E}\ll p^{rN-r-2}.

Then, by Lemma
5.2
, Lemma
5.3
and Lemma
5.4
, it follows from (
5.76
) that

(5.87)

\mathcal{S}_{>}^{(\kappa)}(p^{r})\leq p^{rN-r}(1+O(p^{-2})).

Next, we analyse the sum
\mathcal{S}_{\leq}^{(\kappa)}(p^{r}).
Recall the definition
(\ref{6.59590})
of
A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})
for given
e\in\{0,\ldots,r\}.
On observing that

\{{\boldsymbol{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(p^{r})|\ P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\}=\bigcup_{e=0}^{r}\bigcup_{v=0}^{r-e}A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r}),

it follows that

(5.88)

\mathcal{S}_{\leq}^{(\kappa)}(p^{r})\leq\displaystyle\sum_{e=0}^{r}\displaystyle\sum_{v=0}^{r-e}\mathcal{T}^{(\kappa)}(e,v;p^{r}),

where, for
e\in\{0,\ldots,r\}
and
v\in\{0,\ldots,r-e\}
, we have set

\mathcal{T}^{(\kappa)}(e,v;p^{r})=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{b}}\in A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r})\\
\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})\leq 1/2\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})^{\kappa}}.

We first consider the case where
e\in\{0,1\}
. By applying Lemma
5.11
, we find that

\mathcal{T}^{(\kappa)}(e,v;p^{r})\leq p^{-\kappa v}\cdot p^{\kappa(e+1)(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\\
\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})\leq 1/2\end{subarray}}1.

From the elementary inequality
1\leq 4(\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})-1)^{2}
whenever
\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})\leq 1/2,
we obtain

\mathcal{T}^{(\kappa)}(e,v;p^{r})\leq 4p^{-\kappa v}\cdot p^{\kappa(e+1)(n-1)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq p^{r}\\
P({\boldsymbol{b}})\equiv 0\ \text{mod}\ p^{r}\end{subarray}}(\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})-1)^{2}.

Thus, it follows from Lemma
5.4
that

\mathcal{T}^{(\kappa)}(e,v;p^{r})\ll p^{rN-r-\kappa v+\kappa(e+1)(n-1)}(p^{r-n\lceil r/d\rceil}+p^{d-n}).

Then, we deduce that

(5.89)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{v=0}^{r}\mathcal{T}^{(\kappa)}(0,v;p^{r})+\displaystyle\sum_{v=0}^{r-1}\mathcal{T}^{(\kappa)}(1,v;p^{r})\ll p^{rN-r+2\kappa(n-1)}(p^{r-n\lceil r/d\rceil}+p^{d-n}).

We now consider the case where
e\in\{2,\ldots,r\}.
On dropping the condition
\sigma({\boldsymbol{b}};p^{r})\leq 1/2
and applying Lemma
5.11
, we find that

\mathcal{T}^{(\kappa)}(e,v;p^{r})\leq p^{-\kappa v}\cdot p^{\kappa(e+1)(n-1)}\#A_{v}^{(e)}(p^{r}).

By taking
\kappa=1/(10n)
, we deduce from Lemma
5.9
that

(5.90)

\displaystyle\sum_{e=2}^{r}\displaystyle\sum_{v=0}^{r-e}\mathcal{T}^{(\kappa)}(e,v;p^{r})\ll p^{rN-r-2+3\kappa(n-1)}.

It follows from
(\ref{6.95})
together with bounds (
5.89
) and (
5.90
) that

\mathcal{S}_{\leq}^{(\kappa)}(p^{r})\ll p^{rN-r}\left(\frac{1}{p^{n\lceil r/d\rceil-r-2\kappa(n-1)}}+\frac{1}{p^{n-d-2\kappa(n-1)}}+\frac{1}{p^{2-3\kappa(n-1)}}\right).

By the assumption
n>d+1
and the choice
\kappa=1/(10n)
, we have

(5.91)

\mathcal{S}_{\leq}^{(\kappa)}(p^{r})\ll p^{rN-r-3/2}.

On substituting (
5.87
) and
(\ref{6.98})
into (
5.75
), one finds that

(5.92)

\mathcal{S}_{1}\ll\frac{W^{N-1}}{(\log A)^{\eta\cdot\kappa}}\displaystyle\prod_{p^{r}\|W}\left(1+\frac{1}{p^{3/2}}\right).

By substituting
(\ref{6.826.826.82})
and
(\ref{6.100})
into (
5.73
), we conclude that

\mathcal{S}(A)\ll\frac{1}{(\log A)^{(\eta\cdot\kappa)/2}}.

Thus, since
\kappa=1/(10n)
, this completes the proof of Proposition
5.1
.
∎

5.3.
Singular integrals treatment

Our goal in this subsection is to prove Proposition
5.12
below. In advance of the statement of this proposition, we recall the definition
\mathcal{A}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(A;P)
in section 1, and recall the definition (
1.11
) of
\mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}.

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
A
and
X
be positive number with
X^{3}\leq A.
Suppose that
n
and
d
are natural numbers with
d\geq 2
and
n\geq 4
. Suppose that
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N_{d,n}
variables of degree
k\geq 2.
Then, whenever
N\geq 1000n^{2}8^{k}
, one has

{A^{-N+k}}\cdot\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(A;P)\middle|\ \mathfrak{J}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{*}\leq X^{n-d}A^{-1}(\log A)^{-\eta}\right\}\ll(\log A)^{-\eta/(2n)},

for some
\eta>0
, where the implicit constant may depend on
n
and
d.

We begin this section by observing the following. Recall the definition of
w
and
N
. For
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
with
\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A,
we define

\tau({\boldsymbol{a}};b)=b\cdot\text{mes}\left(\left\{\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in[0,1]^{n}\middle|\ |f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})|\leq\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|\cdot\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|}{b}\right\}\right).

Observe from
\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|\leq N^{1/2}
and
\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|\leq AN^{1/2}
for
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in[0,1]^{n}
and
\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A
that there exists
C>0
such that

C\cdot\tau({\boldsymbol{a}};2w^{5}N)\leq\mathfrak{J}^{*}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\cdot AX^{-n+d}.

Hence, in order to prove Proposition
5.12
, we infer that it suffices to show that

(5.93)

{A^{-N+k}}\cdot\#\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\text{loc}}_{d,n}(A;P)\middle|\ C\cdot\tau({\boldsymbol{a}};2w^{5}N)\leq(\log A)^{-\eta}\right\}\ll(\log A)^{-\eta/(2n)},

where the implicit constant may depend on
n
and
d
. We will prove the inequality
(\ref{6.989898})
at the end of this section, via the strategy used in [
References
, section 5].

5.3.1.
The proof of Lemma
5.13

In this section, we prove Lemma
5.13
. In order to describe this lemma, we introduce some definitions.

We define the set

(5.94)

\mathbb{I}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}=\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}|\ \text{there exists}\ {\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\ \text{such that}\ f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0\},

where we wrote

\mathbb{S}^{n-1}=\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}|\ \|{\boldsymbol{x}}\|=1\}.

On recalling the definition of
B_{m}(u)
in the preamble to Lemma
2.6
, we define

\mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{a}})=\{{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}:{\boldsymbol{y}}-{\boldsymbol{a}}\in B_{N}(1)\}.

Furthermore, on recalling that
P
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N_{d,n}
variables of degree
k\geq 2,
we set

\mathcal{U}_{d,n}(A)=\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\cap[-A,A]^{N}\cap\mathbb{I}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}|\ \mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{a}})\not\subset\mathbb{I}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}},\ P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\}.

The following lemma shows that the cardinality of
\mathcal{U}_{d,n}(A)
is small enough so that we can begin the proof of Proposition
5.12
by excluding the set
\mathcal{U}_{d,n}(A)
from the set in the left-hand side of the inequality
(\ref{6.989898})
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
d\geq 2
and
n\geq 4.
Whenever
N_{d,n}>2^{k+1}(k-1)(k+n)
, we have

\#\mathcal{U}_{d,n}(A)\ll A^{N-k-1/2}.

Proof.

Given
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\mathcal{U}_{d,n}(A)
, let
{\boldsymbol{b}}\in\mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{a}})\setminus\mathbb{I}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}
and define

M_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}=\text{max}\left\{t\in(0,1]|\ {\boldsymbol{a}}+t({\boldsymbol{b}}-{\boldsymbol{a}})\in\mathbb{I}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}\right\}.

We also set
{\boldsymbol{c}}={\boldsymbol{a}}+M_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{b}}-{\boldsymbol{a}})
and we will show that for any
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
satisfying
f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
one has
\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
. Indeed, for
\rho\in(0,1/A^{2})
and
{\boldsymbol{y}}\in B_{n}(\rho)
we have

\displaystyle f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}+\rho^{2}({\boldsymbol{b}}-{\boldsymbol{a}})}({\boldsymbol{x}}+{\boldsymbol{y}})

\displaystyle=f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}+\rho^{2}({\boldsymbol{b}}-{\boldsymbol{a}})}({\boldsymbol{x}})+\langle\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}+\rho^{2}({\boldsymbol{b}}-{\boldsymbol{a}})}({\boldsymbol{x}}),{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle+O(A\rho^{2})

\displaystyle=f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})+\langle\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle+O(\rho^{3/2})

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle+O(\rho^{3/2}).

Assume that
\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\neq\boldsymbol{0}
and let
{\boldsymbol{y}}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
satisfying
\langle\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),{\boldsymbol{y}}_{0}\rangle\neq 0.
For
|u|\leq\rho
we thus have

f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}+\rho^{2}({\boldsymbol{b}}-{\boldsymbol{a}})}({\boldsymbol{x}}+u{\boldsymbol{y}}_{0})=u\cdot\langle\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),{\boldsymbol{y}}_{0}\rangle+O(\rho^{3/2}).

We see that if
\rho
is chosen sufficiently small then the intermediate value theorem shows that there exists
u_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}+\rho^{2}({\boldsymbol{b}}-{\boldsymbol{a}})}({\boldsymbol{x}}+u_{0}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{0})=0
, which contradicts the maximality of
M_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}.
Hence, we find that

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{d,n}(A)\subseteq\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\cap[-A,A]^{N}\cap\mathbb{I}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}|\ P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0,\ \exists{\boldsymbol{c}}\in\mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{a}})\ \exists{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1},\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0\}.

Next, we will show that given
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\cap[-A,A]^{N},
if
{\boldsymbol{c}}\in\mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{a}})
and
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
satisfy
\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\boldsymbol{0}
then for any
{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
\|{\boldsymbol{y}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}\|\leq A^{-1}
we have
\|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})\|\ll 1.
Indeed, the triangle inequality gives

\displaystyle\|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}-{\boldsymbol{c}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})\|+\|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})-\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\|+\|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\|

\displaystyle\ll\|{\boldsymbol{a}}-{\boldsymbol{c}}\|\cdot\|{\boldsymbol{y}}\|^{d-1}+\|{\boldsymbol{c}}\|\cdot\|{\boldsymbol{y}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}\|\cdot\text{max}\{\|{\boldsymbol{x}}\|,\|{\boldsymbol{y}}\|\}^{d-2}.

We thus get
\|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})\|\ll 1
as we claimed. Since we have in addition

\text{mes}\bigl(\bigl\{{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\big|\ \|{\boldsymbol{y}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}\|\leq A^{-1}\bigr\}\bigr)\gg A^{-n},

on recalling the definition of
\mathcal{H}_{n}(A)
in section
2.1
, it follows that

(5.95)

\displaystyle\#\mathcal{U}_{d,n}(A)

\displaystyle\ll A^{n}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\cap[-A,A]^{N}\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\text{mes}\left(\left\{{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\middle|\ \begin{aligned} &1-1/A\leq\|{\boldsymbol{y}}\|\leq 1+1/A\\
&\|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})\|\ll 1\end{aligned}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\ll A^{n}\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}_{n}(A)}\#\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\cap[-A,A]^{N}|\ \|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})\|\ll 1,\ P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\}d{\boldsymbol{y}}

We consider a function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
such that supp(
\varphi
)
\subseteq[-1,1],\ \varphi>0
and
\frac{d^{m}\varphi(y)}{dy^{m}}\ll 1,
where the implicit constant may depend on
m.

Furthermore, for fixed
{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, if we define a function
\mathbf{1}_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}({\boldsymbol{a}})
to be

\mathbf{1}_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}({\boldsymbol{a}}):=\begin{cases}1,&\text{when $\|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})\|\ll 1$}\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

we observe that there exists
D>0
such that

\mathbf{1}_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}({\boldsymbol{a}})\ll\prod_{i=1}^{n}\varphi\bigl(\frac{(\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}}))_{i}}{D}\bigr).

Then, it follows from
(\ref{6.9999})
that

\displaystyle\#\mathcal{U}_{d,n}(A)\ll A^{n}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{n}(A)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}({\boldsymbol{a}})d{\boldsymbol{y}}\ll A^{n}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{n}(A)}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|_{\infty}\leq A\\
P({\boldsymbol{a}})=0\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\varphi\biggl(\frac{(\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}}))_{i}}{D}\biggr)d{\boldsymbol{y}}.

On writing
\varphi(\xi)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\widehat{\varphi}(\beta)e(\beta\xi)d\beta,

it follows by orthogonality that

(5.96)

\displaystyle\#\mathcal{U}_{d,n}(A)

\displaystyle\ll A^{n}\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}_{n}(A)}\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\sum_{-A\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq A}e(\alpha P({\boldsymbol{a}}))\prod_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\varphi}(\beta_{i})e\biggl(\left\langle\boldsymbol{\beta},\frac{\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})}{D}\right\rangle\biggr)d\alpha d\boldsymbol{\beta}d{\boldsymbol{y}},

where
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{n})
and
d\boldsymbol{\beta}=d\beta_{1}d\beta_{2}\cdots d\beta_{n}.

We define

(5.97)

\mathfrak{M}_{\delta}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq a\leq q\leq A^{\delta}\\
(q,a)=1\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{M}_{\delta}(q,a),

where
\mathfrak{M}_{\delta}(q,a)=\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in[0,1)|\ |\alpha-a/q|\leq q^{-1}A^{\delta-k}\}
, and define
\mathfrak{m}_{\delta}=[0,1)\setminus\mathfrak{M}_{\delta}
. Then, we deduce from
(\ref{6.100100})
together with the triangle inequality that

(5.98)

\#\mathcal{U}_{d,n}(A)\ll A^{n}(\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2}),

where

(5.99)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}_{n}(A)}\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{M}_{1/4}}\displaystyle\sum_{-A\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq A}\biggl|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\varphi}(\beta_{i})e\biggl(\biggl\langle\boldsymbol{\beta},\frac{\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})}{D}\biggr\rangle\biggr)d\boldsymbol{\beta}\biggr|d\alpha d{\boldsymbol{y}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}_{n}(A)}\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{M}_{1/4}}\displaystyle\sum_{-A\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq A}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\varphi\biggl(\frac{(\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}}))_{i}}{D}\biggr)d\alpha d{\boldsymbol{y}}

and

\Sigma_{2}=\int_{\mathcal{H}_{n}(A)}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\varphi}(\beta_{i})\biggl|\int_{\mathfrak{m}_{1/4}}\displaystyle\sum_{-A\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq A}e(\alpha P({\boldsymbol{a}}))e\biggl(\biggl\langle\boldsymbol{\beta},\frac{\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})}{D}\biggr\rangle\biggr)d\alpha\biggr|d\boldsymbol{\beta}d{\boldsymbol{y}}.

First, we analyze
\Sigma_{2}.
Since
P({\boldsymbol{a}})
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial, it follows by the Weyl type estimate for exponential sums over minor arcs
[\ref{ref20},\text{Lemma}\ 3.6]
that

\sup_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{m}_{1/4}}\displaystyle\sum_{-A\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq A}e(\alpha P({\boldsymbol{a}}))e\biggl(\biggl\langle\boldsymbol{\beta},\frac{\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})}{D}\biggr\rangle\biggr)\ll A^{N-N/(2^{k+1}(k-1))+\epsilon}.

Since
N>2^{k+1}(k-1)(k+n)
,
\text{mes}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(A))\ll A^{-1}
and

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\varphi}(\beta_{i})d\boldsymbol{\beta}\ll 1,
we find that

(5.100)

\Sigma_{2}\ll A^{N-k-n-1}.

Next, we turn to estimate
\Sigma_{1}.
Note that there exists a positive number
D_{1}=O(1)
such that

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\varphi\biggl(\frac{(\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}}))_{i}}{D}\biggr)\ll\mathbf{1}_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\biggl(\frac{{\boldsymbol{a}}}{D_{1}}\biggr).

Hence, we have

(5.101)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}

\displaystyle\ll\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}_{n}(A)}\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{M}_{1/4}}\displaystyle\sum_{-A\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq A}\mathbf{1}_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\biggl(\frac{{\boldsymbol{a}}}{D_{1}}\biggr)d\alpha d{\boldsymbol{y}}

\displaystyle\ll\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{M}_{1/4}}\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}_{n}(A)}\#\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\cap[-A,A]^{N}|\ \|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})\|\ll 1\}d{\boldsymbol{y}}d\alpha,

where we have used the fact that
D_{1}=O(1).

Therefore, since
\text{mes}(\mathfrak{M}_{1/4})\leq A^{-k+1/2}
, we conclude by applying Lemma
2.6
that

(5.102)

\Sigma_{1}\ll A^{N-k-n+1/2}.

By substituting
(\ref{6.103})
and
(\ref{6.104})
into
(\ref{6.101})
, we complete the proof of Lemma
5.13
.
∎

Recall that
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\boldsymbol{x}}]
is a non-singular homogeneous polynomial in
N_{d,n}
variables of degree
k\geq 2.
We define the set

C_{P}(A)=\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in B_{N}(N)\middle|\ \|{\boldsymbol{a}}-{\boldsymbol{b}}A^{-1}\|\leq A^{-1}\ \text{with}\ {\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}\cap[-A,A]^{N}\ \text{and}\ P({\boldsymbol{b}})=0\right\}.

For
\lambda>0,
we introduce the set

(5.103)

B^{(\lambda)}_{N}=\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in C_{P}(A)\middle|\ \begin{aligned} \exists{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\ \text{such that}\ \left\{\begin{aligned} &(1)\ f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=0\\
&(2)\ \lambda\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|<\|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\|\leq 2\lambda\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\|\end{aligned}\right.\end{aligned}\right\}.

Furthermore, if we set

(5.104)

M_{d,n}=\max\left\{\|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\||\ ({\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{x}})\in\mathbb{S}^{N-1}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\right\},

then we observe that

(5.105)

\mathbb{I}_{d,n}^{\text{loc}}\cap C_{P}(A)=\bigcup_{l=1}^{\infty}B_{N}^{(M_{d,n}/2^{l})}.

In the proof of Proposition
\ref{prop6.11},
the following lemma plays a similar role with that of Lemma
5.9
in the proof of Proposition
\ref{prop6.1}.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
d\geq 2
and
n\geq 4.
For
\lambda\in(0,M_{d,n})
, whenever

N_{d,n}>6n(n+1)k(k-1)2^{k-1},

we have

\text{mes}\left(B_{N}^{(\lambda)}\right)\ll\lambda A^{-k}.

Proof.

For
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in B_{N}(N)
we define

\mathcal{D}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\lambda)=\left\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\middle|\ \begin{aligned} |f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})|\leq\lambda^{2},\ \|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\|\leq 2\lambda N\end{aligned}\right\},

and we observe that

\text{mes}\left(B_{N}^{(\lambda)}\right)\leq\text{mes}(\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in C_{P}(A)|\ \mathcal{D}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\lambda)\neq\emptyset\}).

Given
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{D}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\lambda/2)
, it follows from the estimates

f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})=f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})+\langle\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),{\boldsymbol{y}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle+O(\|{\boldsymbol{y}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}\|^{2}),

and

\|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{y}})\|=\|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\|+O(\|{\boldsymbol{y}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}\|),

that there exists an absolute constant
K>0
such that if
{\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
and
\|{\boldsymbol{y}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}\|\leq K\lambda
then
{\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathcal{D}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\lambda)
. Since we have

\text{mes}(\{{\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}|\ \|{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{y}}\|\leq K\lambda\})\gg\lambda^{n-1},

we see that

\text{mes}\left(B_{N}^{(\lambda)}\right)\ll\int_{C_{P}(A)}\frac{\text{mes}(\mathcal{D}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\lambda))}{\lambda^{n-1}}d{\boldsymbol{a}}.

Therefore, we find that

(5.106)

\text{mes}\left(B_{N}^{(\lambda)}\right)\ll\lambda^{1-n}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\text{mes}\left(\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in C_{P}(A)\middle|\ \begin{aligned} |f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})|\leq\lambda^{2},\ \|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\|\leq 2\lambda N\end{aligned}\right\}\right)d{\boldsymbol{x}}.

For simplicity, we write

\mathfrak{K}({\boldsymbol{x}},\lambda)=\text{mes}\left(\left\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in C_{P}(A)\middle|\ \begin{aligned} |f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})|\leq\lambda^{2},\ \|\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\|\leq 2\lambda N\end{aligned}\right\}\right).

We investigate
\mathfrak{K}({\boldsymbol{x}},\lambda)
by splitting into three cases

(a)\ \lambda\leq A^{-k}\ (b)\ A^{-k}<\lambda\leq A^{-1/(2n)}\ (c)\ A^{-1/(2n)}<\lambda.

Case (a): Assume that
\lambda\leq A^{-k}.
Since
C_{P}(A)\subseteq B_{N}(N)
and
N=O(1),
one infers from Lemma
2.7
with
2\lambda N
in place of
2\lambda
that

(5.107)

\displaystyle\text{mes}\left(B_{N}^{(\lambda)}\right)
